# Daily Summary: 2025-06-04

### Truth over Tricks: Measuring and Mitigating Shortcut Learning in Misinformation Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02350v1)
- **Authors**: Herun Wan, Jiaying Wu, Minnan Luo, Zhi Zeng, Zhixiong Su
- **Abstract**: Misinformation detection models often rely on superficial cues (i.e., \emph{shortcuts}) that correlate with misinformation in training data but fail to generalize to the diverse and evolving nature of real-world misinformation. This issue is exacerbated by large language models (LLMs), which can easily generate convincing misinformation through simple prompts. We introduce TruthOverTricks, a unified evaluation paradigm for measuring shortcut learning in misinformation detection. TruthOverTricks categorizes shortcut behaviors into intrinsic shortcut induction and extrinsic shortcut injection, and evaluates seven representative detectors across 14 popular benchmarks, along with two new factual misinformation datasets, NQ-Misinfo and Streaming-Misinfo. Empirical results reveal that existing detectors suffer severe performance degradation when exposed to both naturally occurring and adversarially crafted shortcuts. To address this, we propose SMF, an LLM-augmented data augmentation framework that mitigates shortcut reliance through paraphrasing, factual summarization, and sentiment normalization. SMF consistently enhances robustness across 16 benchmarks, encouraging models to rely on deeper semantic understanding rather than shortcut cues. To promote the development of misinformation detectors, we have published the resources publicly at https://github.com/whr000001/TruthOverTricks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Truth over Tricks: Measuring and Mitigating Shortcut Learning in Misinformation Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of shortcut learning in misinformation detection, where models rely on superficial cues (e.g., sentiment, style) that correlate with misinformation in training data but don't generalize to real-world, diverse misinformation. The authors introduce TRUTHOVERTRICKS, a unified evaluation framework to measure shortcut learning, categorizing it into intrinsic induction (naturally occurring shortcuts) and extrinsic injection (adversarially crafted shortcuts using LLMs). They evaluate several misinformation detectors across various benchmarks, revealing significant performance degradation when exposed to shortcuts.  To mitigate this, they propose SMF, an LLM-augmented data augmentation framework leveraging paraphrasing, factual summarization, and sentiment normalization to improve model robustness. Experiments demonstrate that SMF consistently enhances performance across benchmarks and under extrinsic shortcut injection. The authors make their resources publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the TRUTHOVERTRICKS evaluation framework and the systematic categorization of shortcut learning in misinformation detection. While previous work has explored shortcut learning, this paper provides a more comprehensive and structured approach to its measurement and mitigation in this specific domain. The use of LLMs for extrinsic shortcut injection is a clever way to simulate adversarial attacks and challenge model robustness. The datasets NQ-Misinfo and Streaming-Misinfo also add value by focusing on factual knowledge requirements. The SMF framework is a reasonable, albeit not groundbreaking, approach to data augmentation, leveraging LLMs for a specific purpose.

*   **Significance:**  The work is highly significant for the misinformation detection field. Shortcut learning is a major obstacle to building reliable and generalizable systems, and this paper directly tackles this issue with a well-defined framework and mitigation strategy. The findings highlight the limitations of existing detectors and the vulnerability to even simple adversarial manipulations. The proposed SMF framework, while not a silver bullet, provides a practical and effective method to improve model robustness. The public release of resources will encourage further research in this area. The identified reliance on topical patterns also shines light on important dataset biases that need to be addressed in future work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** TRUTHOVERTRICKS provides a well-defined taxonomy and a thorough evaluation of shortcut learning.
    *   **Realistic Adversarial Attacks:** The LLM-based shortcut injection effectively simulates real-world adversarial scenarios.
    *   **Practical Mitigation Strategy:** SMF is a practical and data-centric approach that can be easily integrated with existing detectors.
    *   **Reproducibility and Openness:** The public release of datasets and code promotes transparency and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **SMF's Limited Novelty:** The augmentation techniques within SMF, while effective, are not particularly novel. They rely on existing LLM capabilities.
    *   **Implicit Injection Weakness:** The LLM struggle with implicit injection showcases a current limitation, which could be further explored. Is it a problem of LLM fidelity, or detector sensitivity to these subtle shortcuts?
    *   **Reliance on Heuristics:** While datasets are created from QA pairs, the focus on "short" false answers relies on a heuristic that might not always capture the subtle nature of misinformation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a high potential influence on the field. It provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating misinformation detectors, encourages the development of more robust models, and highlights the importance of data-centric mitigation strategies.  The TRUTHOVERTRICKS framework could become a standard evaluation paradigm in future misinformation detection research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a strong and significant contribution to the field. While the individual components (e.g., data augmentation, LLM use) are not entirely revolutionary, their combination within the TRUTHOVERTRICKS framework and their targeted application to the shortcut learning problem in misinformation detection is novel and impactful. The systematic evaluation and the practical mitigation strategy address a critical challenge, paving the way for more reliable and generalizable systems. The release of the resources is commendable and will foster further research. However, the fact that the LM-based detection approaches used are relatively naive limits the impact slightly, since they do not involve pre-trained language models.

### Rewarding the Unlikely: Lifting GRPO Beyond Distribution Sharpening
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02355v1)
- **Authors**: Andre He, Daniel Fried, Sean Welleck
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning has emerged as an effective framework for training large language models on structured language-conditioned tasks. We identify a critical flaw of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a widely used RL algorithm in this setting. For tasks that require multi-sample performance, such as formal theorem proving, GRPO biasedly reinforces already probable solutions and neglects rare but correct proofs. This implicit bias impairs performance on pass@$N$ metrics at large sample sizes, limiting its practicality for training theorem provers. To address this, we introduce the unlikeliness reward, a straightforward method that explicitly encourages reinforcing rare correct solutions. Additionally, we find that increasing the number of PPO epochs further mitigates this bias. Our experiments confirm that incorporating the unlikeliness reward significantly improves pass@$N$ across a large range of N, outperforming standard GRPO and substantially increasing sample diversity. Applying our revised recipe to Lean, we achieve competitive performance with DeepSeek-Prover-V1.5-RL on the miniF2F-test benchmark. We release our implementation, providing a simple yet effective recipe for training formal theorem provers with RL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the limitations of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a standard reinforcement learning algorithm used to improve language model reasoning, specifically in the context of formal theorem proving. The authors identify a "rank bias" in GRPO, where the algorithm tends to reinforce already probable solutions, neglecting rare but correct ones, leading to distribution sharpening rather than solving new problems. To address this, they introduce "unlikeliness reward," which explicitly up-weights rare but correct solutions.  They demonstrate that this mitigates rank bias, improves pass@N metrics, and uncovers a link between rank bias and the number of PPO epochs. Finally, they create an open pipeline with the modifications that matches the performance of the state-of-the-art DeepSeek-Prover-V1.5-RL on the miniF2F-test benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in identifying and characterizing the "rank bias" within GRPO in the context of formal theorem proving and in introducing the "unlikeliness reward" as a simple, direct solution. While the concept of up-weighting rare events is not entirely novel in RL literature, its specific application and demonstration in mitigating GRPO's bias within the formal theorem proving domain represent a clear contribution. Discovering the implicit link to PPO epoch number is also a non-trivial empirical finding.

* **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from addressing a critical limitation of RL for reasoning in formal domains. By overcoming the distribution sharpening problem, the paper paves the way for developing more robust and versatile RL-based theorem provers. The open pipeline is a significant community contribution, allowing other researchers to build upon their results. Their approach to formal verification makes the reward structure extremely precise and is highly beneficial as noise will not contribute to model learning.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition and analysis:** The paper provides a well-defined problem statement and a rigorous analysis of GRPO's behavior, both theoretically and empirically.
    * **Simple and effective solution:** The "unlikeliness reward" is a simple yet effective method for mitigating the identified bias.
    * **Thorough experimentation:**  The paper includes comprehensive experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed solution and to analyze its behavior. The experiments are very carefully designed, with adequate controls.
    * **Practical impact:** The improved GRPO training recipe allows for competitive performance on a challenging benchmark, suggesting its practical applicability.
    * **Open source code:** The release of the open pipeline increases reproducibility and allows for further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generalization:** While the results are compelling in the formal theorem proving setting, it remains to be seen how well the unlikeliness reward generalizes to other RL domains. The precise relationship between the distribution of solutions/actions and the hyperparameter value set for rank bias in the model can also be further explored to enhance robustness across domains.
    * **Marginal improvement:** There is a marginal improvement (Table 3) between the original Deepseek model using RL and the modified version. In light of the overall effort, this could be more substantial.
    * **Incremental:** A limitation is that the unlikeliness reward is a very simple and direct intervention. There is a question of to what extent it is a completely new strategy or incremental over previously studied strategies.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in RL for reasoning, particularly in formal domains. The identification of rank bias and the proposed solution can inform the design of more effective RL algorithms and training strategies. It can also open future directions for optimizing the interplay between PPO epochs and distribution shaping in GRPO. The open pipeline will also provide a valuable resource for the community.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I believe this paper warrants a score of **7**. While the "unlikeliness reward" is a relatively simple idea, its specific application and demonstration in mitigating a critical limitation of GRPO within a challenging domain are valuable contributions. The empirical analysis, clear problem definition, and open-source release enhance the paper's impact. The score is balanced by the potentially limited generalization and relatively marginal performance improvements compared to the initial models.

### ViTNF: Leveraging Neural Fields to Boost Vision Transformers in Generalized Category Discovery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02367v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi Su, Dequan Jin
- **Abstract**: Generalized category discovery (GCD) is a highly popular task in open-world recognition, aiming to identify unknown class samples using known class data. By leveraging pre-training, meta-training, and fine-tuning, ViT achieves excellent few-shot learning capabilities. Its MLP head is a feedforward network, trained synchronously with the entire network in the same process, increasing the training cost and difficulty without fully leveraging the power of the feature extractor. This paper proposes a new architecture by replacing the MLP head with a neural field-based one. We first present a new static neural field function to describe the activity distribution of the neural field and then use two static neural field functions to build an efficient few-shot classifier. This neural field-based (NF) classifier consists of two coupled static neural fields. It stores the feature information of support samples by its elementary field, the known categories by its high-level field, and the category information of support samples by its cross-field connections. We replace the MLP head with the proposed NF classifier, resulting in a novel architecture ViTNF, and simplify the three-stage training mode by pre-training the feature extractor on source tasks and training the NF classifier with support samples in meta-testing separately, significantly reducing ViT's demand for training samples and the difficulty of model training. To enhance the model's capability in identifying new categories, we provide an effective algorithm to determine the lateral interaction scale of the elementary field. Experimental results demonstrate that our model surpasses existing state-of-the-art methods on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-100, CUB-200, and Standard Cars, achieving dramatic accuracy improvements of 19\% and 16\% in new and all classes, respectively, indicating a notable advantage in GCD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ViTNF: Leveraging Neural Fields to Boost Vision Transformers in Generalized Category Discovery":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViTNF, a novel architecture for Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) that combines Vision Transformers (ViT) with a Neural Field (NF)-based classifier. It addresses the limitations of using an MLP head as the classifier in ViT by replacing it with a static NF classifier, which efficiently stores and utilizes support sample information. The authors propose a simplified two-stage training approach: pre-training the ViT feature extractor and then training the NF classifier separately. They also present an algorithm to determine the lateral interaction scale of the elementary field, enhancing the model's ability to identify new categories. Experimental results on several benchmark datasets demonstrate that ViTNF outperforms existing state-of-the-art GCD methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of replacing the MLP head with a neural field classifier is a conceptually interesting twist on how GCD with ViTs is generally done, thus showing novelty in this paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper offers compelling gains in GCD performance across a variety of datasets. The improvements over strong baselines show that the neural field classifier is a viable and potentially more efficient alternative to standard MLP heads for GCD tasks. The simplified two-stage training process is also a notable contribution. Reducing training cost and difficulty is a positive step.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper includes extensive experimental evaluations on well-established benchmarks, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods.
    *   **Conceptual Contribution:** Introduces the use of neural fields as a classifier component within a ViT architecture, providing a fresh perspective to GCD.
    *   **Efficient Training:** The proposed two-stage training mode potentially allows for efficient training and reducing reliance on large training sets during the classifier learning stage.
    *   **Effective Algorithm:** Providing a means for determining lateral interaction scale in the field, which may enhance new category recognition.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Theoretical Analysis Depth:** While the paper presents the use of neural fields in the context of GCD, a more detailed theoretical analysis would benefit the paper.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** Despite having an algorithm to select lateral interaction scales, more rigorous descriptions of the hyperparameter selection process may allow for better reproducibility of the method.
    *   **Complexity:** Neural Fields have a reputation of being complex to work with, which has been addressed in the paper, though may warrant more justification.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   This work could influence the design of future GCD models, with a focus on utilizing neural fields or other mechanisms to improve classifier performance.
    *   The insights into efficient training strategies could lead to broader applications in few-shot learning and open-world recognition.
    *   The overall gains may be important in real-world domains where sample efficiency is very important.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a significant innovation in the design of GCD models by incorporating neural fields. The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed ViTNF architecture achieves substantial performance improvements over existing state-of-the-art methods on several challenging benchmark datasets. Furthermore, the simplified two-stage training process and the new static neural field function contribute to making ViTNF an efficient GCD model. While there is a lack of theoretical depth, the substantial experimental evaluations more than make up for it.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Score**: 7/10

### NextQuill: Causal Preference Modeling for Enhancing LLM Personalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02368v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyan Zhao, Juntao You, Yang Zhang, Wenjie Wang, Hong Cheng, Fuli Feng, See-Kiong Ng, Tat-Seng Chua
- **Abstract**: Personalizing large language models (LLMs) for individual users has become increasingly important as they are progressively integrated into real-world applications to support users' daily lives. However, existing personalization approaches often fail to distinguish which components of model predictions and training data truly reflect user preferences, leading to superficial personalization alignment. In this paper, we introduce NextQuill, a novel LLM personalization alignment framework grounded in causal preference modeling. We approach personalization from a causal perspective, treating both model predictions and ground-truth data generation as outcomes influenced by user preferences, along with other factors. We define the true preference effect as the causal impact of user history (which reflects preferences) on each token prediction or data generation instance, estimated through causal intervention techniques. Building on this insight, NextQuill introduces two complementary alignment strategies: (1) aligning model-internal causal preference effects on predictions with those reflected in ground-truth data, rather than indiscriminately fitting predictions, and (2) focusing on fitting preference-bearing tokens identified via ground-truth data preference effects, rather than treating all tokens uniformly. By integrating these strategies, NextQuill shifts the alignment process toward learning from causal preference effects, facilitating more effective and personalized adaptation. Experiments across multiple personalization benchmarks demonstrate that NextQuill significantly improves personalization quality, offering a principled, causal foundation for LLM personalization. Our codes are available on https://github.com/juntaoyou/NextQuill.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NextQuill, a novel framework for personalizing Large Language Models (LLMs) using causal preference modeling.  It addresses the shortcomings of existing LLM personalization methods, which often treat all predictions and ground truth tokens equally, failing to distinguish what truly reflects user preferences.  NextQuill approaches personalization from a causal perspective, treating both model predictions and ground truth data generation as outcomes influenced by user preferences, estimated via causal intervention.  The framework introduces two complementary alignment strategies: aligning model-internal causal preference effects with those reflected in ground truth data and focusing on preference-bearing tokens within the ground truth.  Experiments across multiple personalization benchmarks demonstrate that NextQuill significantly improves personalization quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the application of causal inference to the LLM personalization problem. While causal inference has seen increasing use in NLP, its application to *personalization* to address the issue of superficial alignment is fairly novel.  The idea of distinguishing between tokens based on their "preference-bearing" characteristics, as estimated through causal effects, is also a valuable contribution. Prior work in LLM personalization often relies on more straightforward fine-tuning or prompt engineering strategies, which this work seeks to improve upon by focusing on genuinely relevant signals.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the potential to improve the effectiveness and efficiency of LLM personalization. By moving beyond indiscriminate fine-tuning and prompt augmentation, NextQuill offers a more principled and interpretable approach. If NextQuill's approach proves generalizable and scalable, it could lead to LLMs that are more attuned to individual user preferences and needs. The performance gains reported across several datasets are promising.

**Strengths:**

*   **Principled Approach:** The paper grounds its methodology in causal inference, providing a theoretical foundation for its design choices.
*   **Targeted Alignment:** The approach intelligently selects preference-bearing signals on both model and data side to achieve better personalized adaptations.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper demonstrates significant improvements over a diverse set of baselines across multiple datasets.
*   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides clear evidence of the contributions of the two key components of NextQuill.
*   **Qualitative Analysis:** Includes qualitative results, such as the word cloud analysis and detailed example showing the benefits of personalization with their approach.
*   **Code Available:**  The availability of code is a positive aspect that enables replication and extension of the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost of Training:** The causal intervention techniques used to estimate preference effects introduces additional computational overhead during training, though it does not affect inference.
*   **Reliance on Sufficient User History:** The method relies on sufficient user history to accurately estimate causal effects, which might be a limitation in cold-start scenarios or for users with limited interaction data. This dependency limits the applicability and generalizability of the approach in situations where user data is scarce or unreliable.
*   **Single-Session Context Window:** The method currently focuses on single-session contexts and doesn't explicitly model longer-term user dynamics, which could limit its effectiveness in certain personalization settings.
*   **Limited Scalability Discussion:** While the paper mentions efficiency at test time, more detailed discussion of its behavior on very large-scale models or datasets would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Proxy Estimation for Ground Truth:**  The paper approximates causal effect computation on the data side using the initial LLM. This approximation could introduce inaccuracies and bias, which may affect the accuracy of weight assignment.

**Potential Influence:**

If the framework is proven scalable, it could become a valuable component for LLM personalization, helping other researchers to create models that are more attuned to individual user preferences. Its insights into what is most useful for preference modeling will also likely be influential.

**Overall Score:**

Taking into consideration the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, a reasonable score would be:

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant approach to LLM personalization with solid experimental results. However, the method has some limitations regarding computational cost, user history dependencies, and scalability. The insights related to causal preference modeling are impactful, and the potential for improved personalization are substantial. While there is still some room to grow (addressing some of the limitations, more extensive scalability studies), this paper is a valuable contribution to the field of LLM personalization.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reconciling Hessian-Informed Acceleration and Scalar-Only Communication for Efficient Federated Zeroth-Order Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02370v1)
- **Authors**: Zhe Li, Bicheng Ying, Zidong Liu, Chaosheng Dong, Haibo Yang
- **Abstract**: Recent dimension-free communication frameworks in Federated Learning (FL), such as DeComFL, significantly reduce per-round communication by transmitting only scalars via zeroth-order stochastic gradient descent (ZO-SGD). This method is particularly advantageous for federated fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs). Yet, the high variance in ZO gradient estimation typically leads to slow convergence. Although leveraging Hessian information is known to enhance optimization speed, integrating this into FL presents significant challenges. These include clients' restrictions on local data and the critical need to maintain the dimension-free communication property. To overcome this limitation, we first introduce a generalized scalar-only communication FL framework that decouples dimension-free communication from standard ZO-SGD, enabling the integration of more advanced optimization strategies. Building on this framework, we propose HiSo, a fast federated fine-tuning method via Hessian-informed zeroth-order optimization and Scalar-only communication. Specifically, it leverages global curvature information to accelerate convergence while preserving the same minimal communication cost per round. Theoretically, we establish convergence guarantees that are independent of the global Lipschitz constant, and further show that HiSo achieves faster rates when the global Hessian exhibits a low effective rank -- a common phenomenon in LLMs. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets and LLM fine-tuning tasks confirm that HiSo significantly outperforms existing ZO-based FL methods in both convergence speed and communication efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of efficient federated fine-tuning for Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly focusing on reducing communication costs. It highlights the limitations of existing zeroth-order optimization (ZOO)-based Federated Learning (FL) methods like DeComFL, which suffer from slow convergence despite their dimension-free communication. The paper proposes a new FL framework that decouples scalar-only communication from standard ZO-SGD, enabling the integration of Hessian-informed optimization. This leads to the HiSo algorithm, which uses global curvature information (diagonal Hessian approximation) to accelerate convergence while maintaining minimal communication cost. The paper provides theoretical convergence guarantees and demonstrates through experiments on benchmark datasets and LLM fine-tuning tasks that HiSo significantly outperforms existing ZO-based FL methods in both convergence speed and communication efficiency, even outperforming certain first-order methods in communication cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the design of HiSo, which elegantly combines Hessian-informed optimization with scalar-only communication in FL. The decoupling of communication constraints from the underlying optimization algorithm is a crucial contribution. The idea of using a diagonal Hessian approximation to guide zeroth-order gradient descent in a communication-constrained environment is also innovative. The theoretical analysis, particularly the low whitening rank assumption and its impact on convergence guarantees, is a valuable addition. However, using a diagonal Hessian approximation is well-known, so the key lies in the FL adaption.
*   **Significance:** Reducing the communication burden is a major bottleneck in federated learning, especially for large models. HiSo offers a practical solution that addresses this challenge by leveraging curvature information without incurring high communication costs. This has the potential to significantly impact the feasibility of federated fine-tuning for LLMs in real-world scenarios with limited bandwidth. The theoretical results provide a strong foundation for the method and offer insights into its convergence behavior under practical assumptions. The empirical results convincingly demonstrate the advantages of HiSo over existing methods, showcasing its potential to accelerate LLM fine-tuning while minimizing communication overhead. The savings compared to first-order methods are very significant.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a crucial challenge in FL for LLMs.
    *   **Innovative Solution:** HiSo provides a novel and practical approach.
    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical analysis offers meaningful guarantees and insights.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are well-designed and demonstrate the effectiveness of HiSo across various datasets and model sizes. It offers both breadth and depth.
    *   **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is generally well-structured and easy to follow.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Diagonal Hessian Approximation:** While practical, diagonal approximation is a simplification. Exploring more advanced (yet communication-efficient) ways to estimate curvature could be a direction for future research. The long-term benefits of the Hessian approximation over time should be further investigated.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses specifically on zeroth-order optimization in FL. The broader applicability of the proposed decoupling framework to other communication-efficient FL techniques could be explored.
    *   **Dependency on Hyperparameters:** The method introduces new hyperparameters, and while some analysis is provided, a more thorough investigation of their impact and sensitivity would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Lack of comparison to methods like FedLowRank or FedAdapt:** A comparison of the performance in relation to the communications cost of these other algorithms would increase the impact of the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:**  HiSo has the potential to become a widely adopted method for federated fine-tuning of LLMs, particularly in scenarios where communication is constrained. The paper may inspire further research into Hessian-informed optimization techniques for FL and the design of more communication-efficient algorithms. The decoupling framework itself could be a valuable contribution, influencing future work on FL algorithm design.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant advance in efficient federated fine-tuning for LLMs, addressing a critical challenge with a novel and well-supported solution. While the diagonal Hessian approximation is a simplification and future research could explore more advanced techniques, the paper's practical relevance, strong theoretical foundation, and compelling empirical results justify a high score. It combines known approximation techniques with current federated optimization literature, demonstrating strong implementation. The limitations and further research directions are clearly defined and the comparison between previous and current solutions is clearly demonstrated.

### SFBD Flow: A Continuous-Optimization Framework for Training Diffusion Models with Noisy Samples
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02371v1)
- **Authors**: Haoye Lu, Darren Lo, Yaoliang Yu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models achieve strong generative performance but often rely on large datasets that may include sensitive content. This challenge is compounded by the models' tendency to memorize training data, raising privacy concerns. SFBD (Lu et al., 2025) addresses this by training on corrupted data and using limited clean samples to capture local structure and improve convergence. However, its iterative denoising and fine-tuning loop requires manual coordination, making it burdensome to implement. We reinterpret SFBD as an alternating projection algorithm and introduce a continuous variant, SFBD flow, that removes the need for alternating steps. We further show its connection to consistency constraint-based methods, and demonstrate that its practical instantiation, Online SFBD, consistently outperforms strong baselines across benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SFBD Flow," a continuous-optimization framework for training diffusion models with noisy samples. It reinterprets the Stochastic Forward-Backward Deconvolution (SFBD) algorithm as an alternating projection method and proposes a continuous variant that eliminates the need for iterative denoising and fine-tuning.  The authors establish connections between SFBD Flow and consistency constraint-based methods. Furthermore, they present "Online SFBD," a practical instantiation of SFBD Flow, and demonstrate its superior performance compared to existing methods across various benchmarks. The core idea is to train generative models on intentionally corrupted datasets, addressing copyright and privacy concerns associated with training on large, potentially sensitive datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the continuous formulation of SFBD, moving away from the iterative denoising and fine-tuning process.  The reinterpretation as an alternating projection algorithm and the derivation of SFBD Flow provide a new perspective on the problem. The Online SFBD variant offers a practical advantage by simplifying the implementation and removing manual coordination steps. The connections made to consistency constraint-based methods are interesting but might be considered more of an observation/unification than a fundamentally new theoretical contribution.

*   **Significance:**  Training generative models on corrupted data is an important problem with significant practical implications. By removing the requirement for direct access to clean data, this research contributes to addressing privacy and copyright concerns associated with large-scale generative model training. The improved performance demonstrated by Online SFBD over baselines is a significant practical contribution. The convergence guarantees and the connection to functional gradient descent also add theoretical weight.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical grounding: The paper provides a rigorous mathematical framework for SFBD Flow.
    *   Practical improvements: Online SFBD is shown to outperform existing methods.
    *   Clear exposition: The paper presents the concepts and methods clearly.
    *   Addresses an important problem: Training generative models without directly accessing sensitive data is a relevant and timely issue.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The empirical evaluation, while demonstrating improvements, could be more extensive. Examining the computational cost compared to baseline, would strengthen the case for Online SFBD.
    *   The "connecting" CC-based methods, the level of the connection to the approach may not be as impactful as presented.
    *   The degree of improvement over existing SFBD variants could be questioned given the removal of the computationally intensive step. It is the simplicity which is improved, so is the performance increase significant or marginal?

*   **Potential Impact:** The work is likely to influence future research on privacy-preserving generative modeling. The continuous optimization framework and the Online SFBD algorithm offer a practical and efficient approach for training diffusion models on corrupted data, which can be beneficial for scenarios where access to clean data is restricted or undesirable. It also provides a new lens through which to view and potentially improve related consistency constraint-based methods.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the improvements to the framework and practical implementation and its practical implications, the paper provides an important contribution. The novel aspects, while not revolutionary, are non-trivial and valuable. However, the relatively contained performance increase and potential issues with experimental design lower its impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Explanations Improves the Robustness of In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02378v1)
- **Authors**: Ukyo Honda, Tatsushi Oka
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) has emerged as a successful paradigm for leveraging large language models (LLMs). However, it often struggles to generalize beyond the distribution of the provided demonstrations. A recent advancement in enhancing robustness is ICL with explanations (X-ICL), which improves prediction reliability by guiding LLMs to understand and articulate the reasoning behind correct labels. Building on this approach, we introduce an advanced framework that extends X-ICL by systematically exploring explanations for all possible labels (X$^2$-ICL), thereby enabling more comprehensive and robust decision-making. Experimental results on multiple natural language understanding datasets validate the effectiveness of X$^2$-ICL, demonstrating significantly improved robustness to out-of-distribution data compared to the existing ICL approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces X²-ICL, an enhancement to In-Context Learning (ICL) with explanations (X-ICL), designed to improve robustness to out-of-distribution (OOD) data.  X²-ICL extends X-ICL by systematically exploring explanations for *all* possible labels, rather than just the observed label in the demonstrations. This aims to encourage a more comprehensive exploration of potential reasoning paths, making the model less reliant on potentially spurious correlations in the demonstrations, and better able to handle distribution shifts. The authors conduct experiments on NLI and paraphrase identification tasks, demonstrating that X²-ICL outperforms both ICL and X-ICL across various OOD datasets and cutting edge LLMs. They also perform ablation studies to understand the impact of systematic reasoning exploration and instructions. Finally, qualitative analysis illustrates how X²-ICL tends to guide the model to carefully examine the details of the input, thereby facilitating reasoning grounded in confidently inferable information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of exploring explanations for *all* possible labels within ICL is a novel extension of the X-ICL framework. It's a logical next step, building directly on the established benefits of incorporating explanations, but pushes it further by explicitly considering alternative reasoning paths. The ablation studies clearly disentangle the benefit from exploration of reasoning paths versus the use of instructions, supporting the incremental gain.

*   **Significance:** Robustness in ICL is a crucial issue limiting its broader applicability. Addressing OOD performance, as this paper does, directly tackles a key challenge.  The results, demonstrating consistent improvements across multiple datasets and state-of-the-art LLMs, are compelling and suggest a genuine advance. Furthermore, the authors provide a qualitative analysis and thorough investigation of the key factors contributing to the performance of X2-ICL, strengthening the significance of the findings. The link to retrieval-based methods strengthens the claims that reasoning itself provides more robustness benefits than merely a diverse set of examples.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of OOD generalization in ICL and motivates the proposed solution.
    *   **Solid Experimental Design:** The experiments are well-designed, using multiple diverse datasets, state-of-the-art LLMs, and careful controls (multiple seeds). The comparison to existing methods (ICL, X-ICL) is direct and fair. The additional study including different prompt settings contributes to more complete understanding of the method's performance.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The ablation study and qualitative analysis provide valuable insights into the workings of X²-ICL, going beyond simply reporting performance numbers.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The method doesn't require extensive fine-tuning or distribution knowledge, making it potentially practical for real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The increased computational cost is a definite drawback.  While the authors acknowledge it and mention it as future work, a more detailed discussion of the trade-off between robustness and cost would be beneficial.
    *   **In-Distribution Performance Trade-off:** The degradation of performance on in-distribution data also needs careful consideration and may affect broader adoption without further improvements.
    *   **Reliance on Strong LLMs:** The experiments suggest that X²-ICL relies on strong reasoning capabilities of the LLM. This might limit its effectiveness with less powerful models.
    *   **Limited Qualitative Insight:** While the qualitative analysis provided is useful, in order to strengthen the claims, more quantitative metrics over the collected outputs (e.g. length of answer, correlation between certain content and labels) may provide further support to the claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of research on robust ICL, encouraging the development of methods that explicitly explore and leverage reasoning. It might also spur further investigation into the trade-offs between robustness, computational cost, and in-distribution performance. Furthermore, findings regarding the effectiveness of explanation-based approaches compared to retrieval based may encourage a broader change within the ICL robustness community.

*   **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of ICL, addressing a key problem (OOD robustness) with a novel and well-validated approach. While there are limitations related to computational cost and in-distribution performance, the clear improvements demonstrated in OOD settings, combined with insightful analysis, warrant a high score. The paper builds upon existing research in a meaningful way and offers a promising direction for future work.

### Univariate to Multivariate: LLMs as Zero-Shot Predictors for Time-Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02389v1)
- **Authors**: Chamara Madarasingha, Nasrin Sohrabi, Zahir Tari
- **Abstract**: Time-series prediction or forecasting is critical across many real-world dynamic systems, and recent studies have proposed using Large Language Models (LLMs) for this task due to their strong generalization capabilities and ability to perform well without extensive pre-training. However, their effectiveness in handling complex, noisy, and multivariate time-series data remains underexplored. To address this, we propose LLMPred which enhances LLM-based time-series prediction by converting time-series sequences into text and feeding them to LLMs for zero shot prediction along with two main data pre-processing techniques. First, we apply time-series sequence decomposition to facilitate accurate prediction on complex and noisy univariate sequences. Second, we extend this univariate prediction capability to multivariate data using a lightweight prompt-processing strategy. Extensive experiments with smaller LLMs such as Llama 2 7B, Llama 3.2 3B, GPT-4o-mini, and DeepSeek 7B demonstrate that LLMPred achieves competitive or superior performance compared to state-of-the-art baselines. Additionally, a thorough ablation study highlights the importance of the key components proposed in LLMPred.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes LLMPred, a novel framework for time-series forecasting using Large Language Models (LLMs).  LLMPred enhances LLM-based prediction by converting time-series data into text prompts.  It utilizes two main techniques: (1) time-series sequence decomposition to separate low- and high-frequency components for improved univariate prediction, and (2) a lightweight prompt-processing strategy to extend univariate prediction to handle multivariate data. The framework demonstrates competitive or superior performance against state-of-the-art baselines using smaller LLMs like Llama 2 7B, Llama 3.2 3B, GPT-40-mini, and DeepSeek 7B. An ablation study highlights the importance of each component within LLMPred.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing ideas, but the specific *integration* and *adaptations* for time-series forecasting offer some novelty. The decomposition of time series for LLM input is inspired by existing time series analysis techniques, but the *application* to LLMs for *zero-shot* forecasting is a valuable exploration. The prompt engineering for multivariate data is another area of novel application, even if the underlying prompt strategies are not entirely new in the broader context of LLMs. The idea of using smaller LLMs is also notable since most LLM time series studies have focused on larger models.
*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it addresses the underexplored area of using LLMs for complex, noisy, and multivariate time-series data. The fact that they show the benefits of smaller LLMs makes the approach accessible. Showing competitive results *without* fine-tuning is a key strength, highlighting the potential of LLMs as zero-shot learners.  The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of each component. However, the paper lacks a thorough comparison to *specialized* time series models. While it compares to transformer-based approaches, it neglects proven time series techniques, which weakens the claim of state-of-the-art performance. Also, the data sets chosen seem to be well-understood to existing techniques, but a key selling point of the use of LLMs for time series is on problems that are difficult using standard techniques.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Effective use of zero-shot learning with LLMs for time-series.
    *   Clear explanation of the components (decomposition, prompt engineering, post-processing).
    *   Strong ablation study demonstrating component contributions.
    *   Focus on smaller LLMs, improving accessibility.
    *  Demonstrated improvement over univariate approach from multi-variate LLMPred, showing the benefit of feature interrelation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited novelty of individual components; primarily novel integration.
    *   Lack of comparison with *dedicated* time-series models (e.g., ARIMA variants, ETS models).  This weakens the "state-of-the-art" claim.
    *  No demonstrations on any real-world problem that is challenging with existing techniques.
    * The context length of LLMs causes significant limitations when dealing with multivariate data or high amounts of data which weakens the significance of the improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper can encourage further research into using LLMs for time-series forecasting, particularly in exploring prompt engineering strategies and adaptation techniques for diverse data characteristics. It could also promote the use of smaller LLMs, which is crucial for resource-constrained environments. The sequence decomposition may inspire novel methodologies for transforming numeric data for LLM consumption.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has interesting results and the zero-shot approach is notable, the lack of comprehensive comparison to *specialized* time-series methods is a major weakness. The individual components lack significant novelty, and the societal impacts do not appear to be well-considered. Given these considerations, the work does not present a truly groundbreaking contribution to the field, but it provides important insights on the effectiveness of existing methods.

### Consultant Decoding: Yet Another Synergistic Mechanism
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02391v1)
- **Authors**: Chuanghao Ding, Jiaping Wang, Ziqing Yang, Xiaoliang Wang, Dahua Lin, Cam-Tu Nguyen, Fei Tan
- **Abstract**: The synergistic mechanism based on Speculative Decoding (SD) has garnered considerable attention as a simple yet effective approach for accelerating the inference of large language models (LLMs). Nonetheless, the high rejection rates require repeated LLMs calls to validate draft tokens, undermining the overall efficiency gain of SD. In this work, we revisit existing verification mechanisms and propose a novel synergetic mechanism Consultant Decoding (CD). Unlike SD, which relies on a metric derived from importance sampling for verification, CD verifies candidate drafts using token-level likelihoods computed solely by the LLM. CD achieves up to a 2.5-fold increase in inference speed compared to the target model, while maintaining comparable generation quality (around 100% of the target model's performance). Interestingly, this is achieved by combining models whose parameter sizes differ by two orders of magnitude. In addition, CD reduces the call frequency of the large target model to below 10%, particularly in more demanding tasks. CD's performance was even found to surpass that of the large target model, which theoretically represents the upper bound for speculative decoding.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can do that. Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Consultant Decoding: Yet Another Synergistic Mechanism".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Consultant Decoding" (CD), a novel synergistic mechanism aimed at improving the inference speed of large language models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of Speculative Decoding (SD), particularly the high rejection rates that necessitate frequent calls to the large target model. Unlike SD, which relies on likelihood ratios derived from importance sampling for verification, CD directly assesses the correctness of candidate draft tokens using the negative log-likelihood (NLL) computed by the target model itself. The authors claim that CD achieves a significant increase in inference speed (up to 2.5x compared to the target model) while maintaining comparable generation quality. The method is particularly effective when combining models with parameter sizes differing by two orders of magnitude. They also demonstrate that CD reduces the call frequency of the large target model and can even surpass the performance of the target model alone.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its verification approach. Moving away from the importance-sampling-based likelihood ratio in SD to a direct NLL-based evaluation is a relatively simple but potentially effective change. It leverages the LLM's learned distribution directly, rather than comparing it to a smaller, potentially less accurate draft model.

*   **Significance:** Improving the inference speed of LLMs is a highly significant problem. SD has already made inroads into this area, and this paper builds upon that foundation. If the claims of a 2.5x speedup with comparable quality hold up, this could be a valuable contribution. The finding that performance *exceeds* the target model is interesting and, if broadly replicable, could have substantial implications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** CD's verification method appears straightforward to implement and understand.
    *   **Performance:** The reported speedup and quality results are promising. The claim of exceeding target model performance, while theoretically interesting, needs further validation in different settings.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The authors provide a rationale for why the generic threshold preserves accuracy, relating it to the convergence loss during training and Top-P sampling.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes experiments on various tasks (mathematical reasoning, code generation, dialogue, and instruction following) and datasets.
    *   **Scalability:** Claims about better scalability with increasing draft length and model size are supported by analysis of the Average Acceptance Length.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the verification method is different, the overall framework remains within the SD paradigm. The innovation feels somewhat incremental, rather than a radical departure.
    *   **Generic Threshold Assumption:** While the authors argue for a generic threshold related to convergence loss, it's unclear how robust this is across different LLM architectures, training datasets, and fine-tuning procedures. The accuracy with the proposed method has the chance to go beyond that of the target LLM needs further validation.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** The ablation study explores the impact of the threshold, but more detailed ablation of the EMA smoothing factor would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** The experiments utilize "Speed-Optimal" settings with tuned parameters, which makes the results less directly comparable to SD with its "Generic" setting. A more apples-to-apples comparison with both methods using tuned parameters would be beneficial.
    *   **Case Study Details:** The case study comparing SD and CD is helpful, but providing a more in-depth explanation of why the CD method led to a correct solution might be useful.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper provides results on a variety of tasks, it is worth noting that the analysis of the errors leading to the outperformance of the target model is limited to a few cases.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the method proves to be generally effective, it could be adopted as a simple optimization technique for LLM inference, particularly when using models of vastly different sizes. The demonstration of performance beyond greedy decoding of the target LLM is a potential selling point and could open up new directions.

*   **Justification:**
    The approach is straightforward but reasonable and effective. The experimental results are solid and demonstrate a significant improvement over current state-of-the-art techniques. The proposed method preserves accuracy across various LLMs and tasks. However, the innovation seems incremental. The idea itself is not completely new and can be viewed as an improvement to a method that is already known. More theoretical and error analysis should be added to further strengthen the claims made by the authors.

**Score: 7**

While the work is incremental, the results are compelling enough to justify a positive rating. The potential for exceeding target model performance, the scalability advantages, and the simplicity of the approach make it a valuable contribution to the field of LLM inference optimization. The authors should address the weaknesses mentioned above to further solidify the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Generalization of Neural Combinatorial Optimization for Vehicle Routing Problems via Test-Time Projection Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02392v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanyao Chen, Rongsheng Chen, Fu Luo, Zhenkun Wang
- **Abstract**: Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO) has emerged as a promising learning-based paradigm for addressing Vehicle Routing Problems (VRPs) by minimizing the need for extensive manual engineering. While existing NCO methods, trained on small-scale instances (e.g., 100 nodes), have demonstrated considerable success on problems of similar scale, their performance significantly degrades when applied to large-scale scenarios. This degradation arises from the distributional shift between training and testing data, rendering policies learned on small instances ineffective for larger problems. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a novel learning framework driven by Large Language Models (LLMs). This framework learns a projection between the training and testing distributions, which is then deployed to enhance the scalability of the NCO model. Notably, unlike prevailing techniques that necessitate joint training with the neural network, our approach operates exclusively during the inference phase, obviating the need for model retraining. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method enables a backbone model (trained on 100-node instances) to achieve superior performance on large-scale Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) of up to 100K nodes from diverse distributions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of poor generalization in neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) methods applied to vehicle routing problems (VRPs).  NCO models trained on small problem instances often struggle to scale to larger, more realistic scenarios due to distributional shifts between training and testing data.  To overcome this limitation, the authors propose a novel framework called Test-Time Projection Learning (TTPL). TTPL leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to learn a projection strategy that transforms the input distribution of large-scale VRP instances to resemble the distribution seen during training on smaller instances. Crucially, this projection is applied *only during inference*, avoiding the need to retrain the NCO model. Furthermore, they introduce a Multi-View Decision Fusion (MVDF) module to enhance robustness by generating and aggregating multiple views of the projected subgraph.  Experiments on TSP and CVRP benchmarks (synthetic and real-world) demonstrate that TTPL allows a base NCO model, pre-trained on small instances, to achieve significantly improved performance on larger, more complex problems without additional fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to learn a projection strategy *specifically at test-time* is a significant and innovative aspect of this work.  Traditional distribution adaptation techniques often require integration into the model training process, limiting flexibility and increasing computational burden.  The separation of learning the projection and deploying it during inference is a key strength. The incorporation of Multi-View Decision Fusion to address local density variations provides an additional layer of robustness and novelty.

*   **Significance:** The problem of poor generalization in NCO methods for VRPs is well-established and significantly hinders their real-world applicability. TTPL offers a promising solution that can substantially improve the scalability of existing NCO models. The ability to leverage pre-trained models without retraining is valuable because it reduces computational costs and allows practitioners to quickly adapt solutions to new problem scales. The impressive results on large-scale TSP and CVRP instances, especially those with non-uniform distributions, demonstrate the practical impact of this approach. The experiments using real-world data from TSPLIB and CVRPLIB further highlight the solution's value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Elegant Solution:** TTPL provides an elegant way to address distributional shift issues without requiring costly retraining.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper includes comprehensive experiments on various benchmark datasets, demonstrating significant performance gains over state-of-the-art methods.
    *   **Real-World Relevance:**  The experiments on TSPLIB and CVRPLIB datasets increase the practical significance of the work.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the importance of both the LLM-based projection strategy and the MVDF module.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:** The paper provides a sufficient description of the approach allowing for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost of LLM at Inference:**  While avoiding retraining is beneficial, the paper should more explicitly address the computational overhead introduced by using the LLM to generate projection strategies during inference.  Although the LLM is used "offline" to design the projection, the computational cost of running the best-found projection strategy at inference is a crucial consideration and should be discussed in more detail. Are the inference times competitive even when accounting for the extra compute? A comparison of the per-instance compute of TTPL projection strategy versus fixed-projection baselines should be included.
    *   **Black-Box Nature of LLM:** The LLM contributes significantly to the core component of the solution, yet its inner workings and specifically *why* certain generated projection strategies work better than others remains opaque. While leveraging powerful pre-trained models is a common and often effective strategy, a greater understanding of the LLM's decision-making process in this context would improve the scientific contribution. Providing more analysis or intuition into what *types* of projection strategies are favored by the LLM and why they generalize better is important.
    *   **Reliance on Specific Base Model:** The evaluation primarily focuses on LEHD as the base model, but a more comprehensive investigation into the transferability to a wider variety of backbone architectures is needed. An ablation study testing TTPL with other NCO base models would strengthen the claims of general applicability.
    *   **Practical Deployment Complexities:** The TTPL framework involves an evolutionary process guided by an LLM that optimizes the projection strategy for the target problem. Although this only takes place at the beginning of inference, the optimization process itself might be lengthy and complex for practitioners. The paper needs to better address the complexities involved in the deployment of LLM to design the projection strategy for specific types of problem scales and distributions.

*   **Potential Influence:** TTPL has the potential to significantly influence the field of NCO by providing a practical and effective method for improving generalization and scaling to larger, real-world problems. The work has the potential to inspire future research into using LLMs for distribution adaptation in other areas of machine learning.

**Rigorous Rationale:** The paper tackles an important problem, offers an innovative solution with sound methodology, and provides substantial empirical support. The weaknesses identified above represent areas for improvement and future research, but do not detract significantly from the paper's overall contribution. Given the identified novelty, significance and strong results, but also considering the limitations around per-instance compute, reliance on LEHD, and deployment of LLM for designing projection strategy, a score of 7 is warranted.

### Joint Modeling for Learning Decision-Making Dynamics in Behavioral Experiments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02394v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Bian, Xingche Guo, Yuanjia Wang
- **Abstract**: Major depressive disorder (MDD), a leading cause of disability and mortality, is associated with reward-processing abnormalities and concentration issues. Motivated by the probabilistic reward task from the Establishing Moderators and Biosignatures of Antidepressant Response in Clinical Care (EMBARC) study, we propose a novel framework that integrates the reinforcement learning (RL) model and drift-diffusion model (DDM) to jointly analyze reward-based decision-making with response times. To account for emerging evidence suggesting that decision-making may alternate between multiple interleaved strategies, we model latent state switching using a hidden Markov model (HMM). In the ''engaged'' state, decisions follow an RL-DDM, simultaneously capturing reward processing, decision dynamics, and temporal structure. In contrast, in the ''lapsed'' state, decision-making is modeled using a simplified DDM, where specific parameters are fixed to approximate random guessing with equal probability. The proposed method is implemented using a computationally efficient generalized expectation-maximization algorithm with forward-backward procedures. Through extensive numerical studies, we demonstrate that our proposed method outperforms competing approaches under various reward-generating distributions, both with and without strategy switching. When applied to the EMBARC study, our framework reveals that MDD patients exhibit lower overall engagement than healthy controls and experience longer decision times when they do engage. Additionally, we show that neuroimaging measures of brain activities are associated with decision-making characteristics in the ''engaged'' state but not in the ''lapsed'' state, providing evidence of brain-behavioral association specific to the ''engaged'' state.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework, RL-HMM-DDM, to model decision-making dynamics in behavioral experiments, particularly focusing on reward-based learning. This framework integrates Reinforcement Learning (RL), a Hidden Markov Model (HMM), and a Drift-Diffusion Model (DDM) to account for strategy switching and response times in decision-making.  The HMM allows for two latent states: an "engaged" state where decisions are guided by RL-DDM and a "lapsed" state representing random guessing using a simplified DDM. The method is implemented using a computationally efficient EM algorithm with forward-backward procedures.  The authors demonstrate the method's performance through simulations and apply it to the EMBARC study of Major Depressive Disorder (MDD), finding that MDD patients exhibit lower engagement and longer decision times when engaged. They also show associations between brain activity and decision-making characteristics, specifically within the "engaged" state.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The integration of RL, HMM, and DDM into a single framework for modeling decision-making is a significant step forward. Previous work often considered RL and DDM separately or combined them without accounting for potential shifts in strategy. The incorporation of an HMM to model state switching is a valuable addition that better reflects real-world decision-making processes, where individuals don't always apply a consistent strategy. The ability to simultaneously model learning, decision dynamics, and temporal structure is a clear advantage.
*   **Weakness:** While the individual components (RL, HMM, DDM) are well-established, the novelty lies in their specific combination and application to the problem of modeling behavioral data in mental health research. The RL-DDM part is not new.
**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** The potential impact on understanding cognitive deficits in MDD and other mental health disorders is substantial. The method offers a more nuanced analysis of decision-making than traditional approaches, allowing researchers to disentangle different aspects of the decision process (e.g., reward processing, decision threshold, non-decision time). The ability to link these aspects to neuroimaging data provides valuable insights into the brain-behavior relationship. The finding that MDD patients have lower overall engagement and slower decision times when engaged aligns with clinical observations and opens new avenues for investigating the neural mechanisms underlying these deficits.
*   **Weakness:** The analysis is limited to the EMBARC study. Although promising, it requires validation with different datasets and experimental paradigms. Furthermore, the interpretation of the "lapsed" state as pure random guessing might be overly simplistic, and future research could explore more complex models for this state. The current study primarily demonstrates correlation with brain measures. Establishing causal relationships between brain activity and decision-making parameters would significantly strengthen the findings. Further exploration of alternative RL-DDM formulations or extending the action space (actions of more than 2 options) will make the paper more significant.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant methodological advance with clear potential for impacting the field of computational psychiatry and cognitive neuroscience. The integration of RL, HMM, and DDM is a clever approach to capture complex decision-making dynamics. The application to MDD provides clinically relevant insights. The method is technically sound, and the simulation studies support its efficacy. However, the novelty is primarily in the specific combination of existing techniques and the application to this particular problem, rather than groundbreaking theoretical developments. The reliance on correlational analyses and the limited validation also constrain the impact.
