The primary reservation in giving it a higher score stems from its dependency on synthetically generated data which might affect the generalizability of LLMs. Further experiments that measure the correlation between the accuracy of FINCHAIN models and the accuracy of these models on real-world datasets would be required to increase the score.*

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think Twice, Act Once: A Co-Evolution Framework of LLM and RL for Large-Scale Decision Making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02522v1)
- **Authors**: Xu Wan, Wenyue Xu, Chao Yang, Mingyang Sun
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) have shown significant promise in decision-making tasks. Nevertheless, for large-scale industrial decision problems, both approaches face distinct challenges: LLMs lack real-time long-sequence decision-making capabilities, while RL struggles with sample efficiency in vast action spaces. To bridge this gap, we propose Agents Co-Evolution (ACE), a synergistic framework between LLMs and RL agents for large-scale decision-making scenarios. ACE introduces a dual-role trajectory refinement mechanism where LLMs act as both Policy Actor and Value Critic during RL's training: the Actor refines suboptimal actions via multi-step reasoning and environment validation, while the Critic performs temporal credit assignment through trajectory-level reward shaping. Concurrently, RL agent enhances LLMs' task-specific decision-making with high-quality fine-tuning datasets generated via prioritized experience replay. Through extensive experiments across multiple power grid operation challenges with action spaces exceeding 60K discrete actions, ACE demonstrates superior performance over existing RL methods and LLM-based methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think Twice, Act Once: A Co-Evolution Framework of LLM and RL for Large-Scale Decision Making":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Agents Co-Evolution (ACE), a novel framework that synergistically combines Large Language Models (LLMs) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) for addressing complex, large-scale decision-making problems, particularly in industrial control scenarios like power grid operation.  ACE addresses the limitations of both LLMs (struggling with long-sequence decision making and real-time performance) and RL (sample inefficiency in large action spaces). The key idea is a dual-role trajectory refinement mechanism. LLMs act as both Policy Actors, refining suboptimal RL actions through multi-step reasoning and environment validation, and as Value Critics, performing temporal credit assignment via trajectory-level reward shaping.  Simultaneously, the RL agent enhances the LLM's task-specific decision-making by generating high-quality fine-tuning datasets using prioritized experience replay. The framework is evaluated on power grid operation challenges with action spaces exceeding 60K discrete actions, demonstrating superior performance over existing RL and LLM-based methods.  ACE separates LLM reasoning and RL execution, enabling offline training and online deployment, facilitating both effective learning and real-time decision-making in industrial contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and executed approach to integrating LLMs and RL. The ACE framework addresses a critical gap in applying these technologies to complex industrial control problems. Here's a detailed breakdown of the strengths, weaknesses, and significance of the work:

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Relevance:** The paper targets a significant and practical problem: large-scale decision-making in complex physical systems. Power grid operation is a prime example where AI-driven automation can have a major impact.
*   **Clear Motivation:** The limitations of both LLMs and RL when applied independently to these problems are clearly articulated. The authors make a compelling case for a combined approach.
*   **Novelty of Approach:**  The dual-role trajectory refinement mechanism (LLM as Actor *and* Critic) is a novel contribution. It effectively leverages the strengths of LLMs (reasoning, knowledge) and RL (optimization, real-time execution). Separating the LLM from real-time interaction, as in ACE, directly addresses the latency issues inherent to autoregressive LLMs in such time-sensitive applications.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The framework is thoroughly evaluated across multiple L2RPN competition environments, providing strong empirical evidence of its effectiveness. The comparisons to existing expert-guided RL methods and LLM-based approaches are crucial.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies effectively isolate the contribution of each component of the ACE framework (Actor, Critic, reasoning strategies), offering valuable insights into its inner workings.
*   **Sample Efficiency:** The experiments highlight the significant improvement in sample efficiency, which is a key advantage in RL for real-world applications.
*   **Practical Considerations:** The paper addresses practical constraints, such as memory management and computational overhead, making the framework more relevant for real-world deployment.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **LLM Prompt Engineering Details:** While the paper describes the structure of the prompts for the LLM, providing more details on prompt engineering would improve the reproducibility and understanding of the approach. Examples of specific prompts used could be beneficial.
*   **Limited LLM Evaluation:** A more extensive evaluation of different LLMs with varying sizes and architectures could have strengthened the robustness of the conclusions. Although Qwen2-7B and GPT-4 are used, further exploration would be beneficial.
*   **Domain Specificity:** The evaluation focuses exclusively on power grid operation. While this provides a strong demonstration in a relevant domain, it leaves open the question of how well ACE generalizes to other complex control systems with different characteristics.
*  **Parameter Sensitivity**: The paper lacks a comprehensive discussion on the sensitivity of ACE to different hyperparameter settings and its potential impact on performance.

**Significance:**

The ACE framework represents a significant step forward in integrating LLMs and RL for complex decision-making. Its ability to achieve superior performance with improved sample efficiency and real-time responsiveness has important implications for industrial automation and other real-world applications. The novel dual-role trajectory refinement mechanism and the clear separation of LLM reasoning and RL execution provide valuable insights for future research in this area. The work encourages further investigation into how LLMs can be effectively used to guide and enhance RL in challenging control scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper exhibits high problem relevance, a significant degree of novelty in the approach, and a thorough experimental evaluation. The identified weaknesses, primarily concerning the depth of the LLM evaluation and the breadth of the domain validation, do not substantially detract from the overall contribution. ACE offers a practical and effective way to combine the strengths of LLMs and RL for large-scale industrial control, a problem of growing importance. The separation of LLM from real time interaction allows the use of stronger LLMs as long as it doesn't influence real-time performance.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Hardware-Centric Analysis of DeepSeek's Multi-Head Latent Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02523v1)
- **Authors**: Robin Geens, Marian Verhelst
- **Abstract**: Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA), introduced in DeepSeek-V2, improves the efficiency of large language models by projecting query, key, and value tensors into a compact latent space. This architectural change reduces the KV-cache size and significantly lowers memory bandwidth demands, particularly in the autoregressive decode phase. This letter presents the first hardware-centric analysis of MLA, comparing it to conventional Multi-Head Attention (MHA) and evaluating its implications for accelerator performance. We identify two alternative execution schemes of MLA--reusing, resp. recomputing latent projection matrices--which offer distinct trade-offs between compute and memory access. Using the Stream design space exploration framework, we model their throughput and energy cost across a range of hardware platforms and find that MLA can shift attention workloads toward the compute-bound regime. Our results show that MLA not only reduces bandwidth usage but also enables adaptable execution strategies aligned with hardware constraints. Compared to MHA, it provides more stable and efficient performance, particularly on bandwidth-limited hardware platforms. These findings emphasize MLA's relevance as a co-design opportunity for future AI accelerators.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a hardware-centric analysis of DeepSeek's Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA), a key architectural innovation in the DeepSeek-V3 large language model that reduces memory bandwidth requirements, especially during inference.  The authors compare MLA to traditional Multi-Head Attention (MHA), exploring different computation orders (recomputing vs. reusing latent projection matrices) and their implications for accelerator performance. Using the Stream design space exploration framework, they model the performance and energy consumption across various hardware platforms, demonstrating that MLA can shift attention workloads towards a compute-bound regime. The results indicate that MLA offers adaptable execution strategies that are well-suited for bandwidth-limited hardware and more consistent performance compared to MHA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in providing the first dedicated hardware-centric analysis of DeepSeek's MLA.  While previous works have described the algorithmic advantages of MLA, this paper goes further by quantifying the computational footprint, analyzing trade-offs in execution strategies, and modeling performance on different hardware platforms.  The comparison with MHA and exploration of different MLA execution orders (MLArc vs MLAru) contributes to a better understanding of MLA's practical implications for hardware acceleration. Introducing two variants of MHA (one with the same dimensions, one with the same number of parameters) helps to provide a stronger and fairer comparison.
*   **Significance:** The findings of this paper are significant for AI accelerator design. The analysis shows that MLA can significantly reduce memory bandwidth demands, which is a major bottleneck in deploying large language models. Demonstrating that MLA enables adaptable execution strategies based on hardware constraints is crucial. The Stream-based modeling provides valuable insights for optimizing AI accelerator architectures for MLA-based models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-structured analysis of MLA.
    *   The use of Stream for hardware modeling is appropriate and allows for quantitative evaluation.
    *   The comparison with MHA is thorough, and the inclusion of two MHA variants provides a more nuanced understanding.
    *   The identification of different MLA execution strategies and their trade-offs is insightful.
    *   The energy analysis adds another dimension to the evaluation, showing the energy efficiency trade-offs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the Stream framework is used, the specific details of the modeled AI accelerator architecture are somewhat generic. More information regarding the hardware parameters and the mapping of kernels could strengthen the analysis.
    *   The analysis focuses primarily on the decode phase. While this is the bottleneck for many LLMs, an analysis of the prefill stage would offer a more complete picture.
    *   It would have been beneficial to include a discussion of area implications, in addition to power and throughput. How do the different execution strategies affect the hardware area requirements for the accelerator?
    *   The paper does not address the memory hierarchy in detail. Real hardware platforms often contain multiple levels of cache, and their effects on performance are not considered here.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the design of future AI accelerators by providing a concrete understanding of the hardware requirements for MLA-based models. The findings can guide architects in making informed decisions about memory bandwidth, compute resources, and execution strategies.  The paper provides a valuable case study for hardware-algorithm co-design.

**Overall:** This is a solid paper that presents a valuable hardware-centric analysis of MLA. It fills a gap in the literature by providing quantitative insights into the computational footprint and performance implications of MLA on different hardware platforms.  While there are some weaknesses, the paper's strengths outweigh them, and its findings are relevant for AI accelerator designers.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of AI accelerator design by providing a detailed hardware analysis of DeepSeek's MLA. The novelty lies in its hardware-focused approach, and the results provide insights for optimizing accelerator architectures. The weaknesses, such as the lack of detailed architectural description and the limited scope of analysis, prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. However, the paper's clarity, thorough comparison, and potential influence justify a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### RelationAdapter: Learning and Transferring Visual Relation with Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02528v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Gong, Yiren Song, Yicheng Li, Chenglin Li, Yin Zhang
- **Abstract**: Inspired by the in-context learning mechanism of large language models (LLMs), a new paradigm of generalizable visual prompt-based image editing is emerging. Existing single-reference methods typically focus on style or appearance adjustments and struggle with non-rigid transformations. To address these limitations, we propose leveraging source-target image pairs to extract and transfer content-aware editing intent to novel query images. To this end, we introduce RelationAdapter, a lightweight module that enables Diffusion Transformer (DiT) based models to effectively capture and apply visual transformations from minimal examples. We also introduce Relation252K, a comprehensive dataset comprising 218 diverse editing tasks, to evaluate model generalization and adaptability in visual prompt-driven scenarios. Experiments on Relation252K show that RelationAdapter significantly improves the model's ability to understand and transfer editing intent, leading to notable gains in generation quality and overall editing performance.
- **Summary**: The paper "RelationAdapter: Learning and Transferring Visual Relation with Diffusion Transformers" introduces a new approach to visual prompt-based image editing using diffusion transformers. The core idea is to leverage pairs of source-target images to extract and transfer content-aware editing intent to novel query images. The authors propose RelationAdapter, a lightweight module for DiT-based models, designed to capture and apply visual transformations from limited examples. They also introduce Relation252K, a large-scale dataset of diverse editing tasks for evaluating model generalization in visual prompt-driven scenarios. The experiments demonstrate improvements in generation quality and editing performance using RelationAdapter.

Critical Evaluation:

Novelty: The paper's novelty lies in its explicit modeling of visual relationships between image pairs within a diffusion transformer framework. While in-context learning and visual prompting are not entirely new, the authors' specific approach of using a dual-branch adapter (RelationAdapter) to decouple the extraction and application of editing intent is a distinguishing feature.  The introduction of the Relation252K dataset is also a notable contribution, addressing the need for a more comprehensive evaluation benchmark for in-context image editing. However, the individual components are not entirely groundbreaking; the core is still built on pre-existing DiT architectures and borrows inspiration from other adapter-based approaches. The degree of innovation might be considered incremental rather than transformative.

Significance: The paper addresses a key challenge in visual prompt-based image editing:  generalizing beyond simple style or appearance adjustments to handle more complex, non-rigid transformations. By explicitly modeling the visual relationships between image pairs, the authors aim to enable more robust and controllable editing. The Relation252K dataset is significant because it offers a valuable resource for training and evaluating future image-pair editing models. However, the improvement of RelationAdapter is somewhat limited. While it demonstrates quantitative and qualitative gains, the degree of improvement might not be large enough to shift the paradigm of visual editing. Furthermore, the paper acknowledges some limitations, such as difficulties in rendering text details accurately.

Strengths:

*   The RelationAdapter module is a lightweight and efficient way to inject editing intent into DiT models.
*   The In-Context Editor facilitates high-fidelity, semantically aligned image editing.
*   The Relation252K dataset provides a valuable resource for research in visual prompt-based image editing.
*   The experimental results demonstrate improvements over existing baseline methods.
*   Decoupling visual features improves computational efficiency

Weaknesses:

*   The method struggles to accurately render textual details, a known limitation of diffusion models.
*   Performance may vary on rare or unseen tasks, suggesting sensitivity to task-specific nuances.
*   While the results are positive, the incremental nature of some of the components (especially considering existing adapter techniques) slightly limits the overall impact.
*   The method's reliance on computationally intensive pre-trained models still contributes to the computational complexity.

Potential Influence: The paper has the potential to influence future research in visual prompt-based image editing by providing a more robust and generalizable framework for learning from image pairs. The Relation252K dataset will likely be used by other researchers in the field. The code release of the proposed model will also enable more future research.

Rigorous Rationale: The paper presents a clear and well-motivated approach to visual prompt-based image editing. The strengths lie in the novel architecture and the introduction of a new comprehensive dataset. The weaknesses mainly come from the limited improvement and several known diffusion model limitations.

Score: 7

The contribution is significant, introducing a well-designed approach to visual prompt-based image editing with a new comprehensive dataset, addressing a real problem in the field. However, due to the incremental nature of some of the architecture, it has to be assigned with a score less than 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Web Application Testing: End-to-End Test Case Generation with Large Language Models and Screen Transition Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02529v1)
- **Authors**: Nguyen-Khang Le, Quan Minh Bui, Minh Ngoc Nguyen, Hiep Nguyen, Trung Vo, Son T. Luu, Shoshin Nomura, Minh Le Nguyen
- **Abstract**: Web applications are critical to modern software ecosystems, yet ensuring their reliability remains challenging due to the complexity and dynamic nature of web interfaces. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in automating complex tasks, but limitations persist in handling dynamic navigation flows and complex form interactions. This paper presents an automated system for generating test cases for two key aspects of web application testing: site navigation and form filling. For site navigation, the system employs screen transition graphs and LLMs to model navigation flows and generate test scenarios. For form filling, it uses state graphs to handle conditional forms and automates Selenium script generation. Key contributions include: (1) a novel integration of graph structures and LLMs for site navigation testing, (2) a state graph-based approach for automating form-filling test cases, and (3) a comprehensive dataset for evaluating form-interaction testing. Experimental results demonstrate the system's effectiveness in improving test coverage and robustness, advancing the state of web application testing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an automated system for generating end-to-end test cases for web applications, focusing on two key aspects: site navigation and form filling. The approach uses large language models (LLMs) and graph structures. For site navigation, the system constructs screen transition graphs and leverages LLMs to model navigation flows and generate test scenarios. For form filling, the system employs state graphs to handle conditional forms and automates Selenium script generation. The authors contribute a novel integration of graph structures and LLMs for site navigation, a state graph-based approach for form filling, and a comprehensive dataset for evaluating form-interaction testing. The experimental results show improvements in test coverage and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The integration of graph structures (screen transition graphs and state graphs) with LLMs for both site navigation and form filling has some novelty. While LLMs have been used for web automation before, the explicit use of graphs to structure the problem and guide the LLM is a differentiating factor. The creation of a dedicated dataset for form-interaction testing is also a useful contribution, as it provides a standardized benchmark for future research. However, the individual components (LLMs for task automation, Selenium for web interaction, graph structures for state representation) are not entirely new in the context of web application testing.  The key innovation is in *how* these components are orchestrated.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to reduce the manual effort required for web application testing and improve test coverage. Automating the generation of test cases can lead to more robust and reliable web applications. The proposed system addresses important challenges in web testing, such as handling dynamic navigation flows and complex form interactions. The comprehensive dataset for form-interaction testing further enhances the significance of the paper by providing a valuable resource for the community.  The paper's reliance on Selenium, while practical, is a potential weakness, as it ties the system to a specific web automation framework.  Furthermore, while the results demonstrate improvement, it is essential to consider the complexity and cost of integrating LLMs into the testing process, particularly for open-source and resource-constrained projects.  The results are also somewhat limited in scope, focusing primarily on coverage and accuracy. A more detailed evaluation of the types of bugs the system can detect, as well as its limitations, would strengthen the paper.

**Strengths:**

*   Addresses a practical and important problem: Automated web application testing.
*   Presents a well-defined and structured approach.
*   Combines graph structures and LLMs effectively.
*   Contributes a comprehensive dataset for form-interaction testing.
*   Demonstrates improved test coverage and robustness.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The individual components (LLMs, Selenium, graph structures) are not entirely novel.
*   Reliance on Selenium introduces dependency on a specific framework.
*   Results primarily focus on coverage and accuracy; lacks detailed analysis of the types of bugs detected.
*   Limited discussion on the cost and complexity of integrating LLMs into the testing process.
*   Evaluation could be expanded to include more diverse and complex web applications.
*   The comparison against existing LLM based automated web testing tools is not extensive.
**Overall:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of automated web application testing by effectively combining graph structures and LLMs. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the integration is well-executed and addresses practical challenges. The creation of a comprehensive dataset for form-interaction testing is a valuable contribution to the community. However, the paper could be strengthened by a more detailed analysis of the system's capabilities, limitations, and cost, as well as a more extensive comparison with other LLM based automated web testing approaches.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's clear contribution in integrating graph structures and LLMs for automated web application testing. The creation of the dataset and the demonstrated improvements in test coverage and robustness warrant a strong score. However, the lack of substantial novelty in the individual components, the reliance on Selenium, and the limited analysis of the system's limitations and costs prevent it from achieving a higher score. The work is a solid step forward, but further refinements and more detailed evaluation are needed to fully realize its potential.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Answer Convergence as a Signal for Early Stopping in Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02536v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Liu, Lu Wang
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting enhances reasoning in large language models (LLMs) but often leads to verbose and redundant outputs, thus increasing inference cost. We hypothesize that many reasoning steps are unnecessary for producing correct answers. To investigate this, we start with a systematic study to examine what is the minimum reasoning required for a model to reach a stable decision. We find that on math reasoning tasks like math, models typically converge to their final answers after 60\% of the reasoning steps, suggesting substantial redundancy in the remaining content. Based on these insights, we propose three inference-time strategies to improve efficiency: (1) early stopping via answer consistency, (2) boosting the probability of generating end-of-reasoning signals, and (3) a supervised method that learns when to stop based on internal activations. Experiments across five benchmarks and five open-weights LLMs show that our methods significantly reduce token usage with little or no accuracy drop. In particular, on NaturalQuestions, Answer Consistency reduces tokens by over 40\% while further improving accuracy. Our work underscores the importance of cost-effective reasoning methods that operate at inference time, offering practical benefits for real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Answer Convergence as a Signal for Early Stopping in Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of verbose and redundant reasoning traces generated by large language models (LLMs) when using chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting.  It proposes the concept of "answer convergence," suggesting that LLMs often internally arrive at a stable answer before completing the full reasoning trace.  The paper presents a systematic study showing this phenomenon exists across various datasets.  Based on this observation, it introduces three inference-time strategies for early stopping: (1) answer consistency, (2) think token adjustment (boosting end-of-reasoning token probability), and (3) a supervised learning approach (Learn-to-Stop) using internal activations to predict optimal stopping points.  Experiments on multiple benchmarks and LLMs demonstrate that these methods reduce token usage significantly with little or no accuracy drop, and in some cases, even improve accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of leveraging answer convergence for early stopping in CoT is reasonably novel, although not completely groundbreaking. Prior work has explored techniques to shorten reasoning chains, but this paper focuses on the specific signal of answer stability during inference. Boosting the `</think>` token is a straightforward, but practical solution. The supervised approach, Learn-to-Stop, is a more sophisticated contribution, utilizing internal activations, but activation-based techniques have been used in LLMs before. The *combination* of these three approaches and the systematic empirical study adds significant value.

*   **Significance:**  The paper has significant practical implications. Reducing the inference cost of LLMs is a crucial area, especially for real-time and resource-constrained applications. The methods proposed are model-agnostic and do not require retraining, making them easily adaptable. The improvement in accuracy on some simpler datasets is an unexpected and interesting finding that deserves further exploration, although the authors acknowledge the potential for increased unfaithfulness. The demonstration that the methods work across multiple datasets and open-weights LLMs strengthens the argument for its broad applicability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Study:** The initial study of answer convergence is well-designed and provides empirical support for the hypothesis.
    *   **Multiple Methods:** Offering three different early stopping strategies provides flexibility and allows for trade-offs between complexity and performance.
    *   **Model-Agnosticism:** The methods are model-agnostic, allowing for easy adoption without retraining, which is valuable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and LLMs provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed techniques.
    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** Reduces the computationally intensity of LLM inference, which is important for real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Faithfulness Analysis:** While the paper addresses faithfulness, a more in-depth analysis of how early stopping impacts the logical consistency and support of the reasoning chain would be beneficial. The manual analysis is a good start, but more comprehensive automatic metrics or qualitative evaluations are needed.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** Although the parameters are validated using the validation sets, a detail discussion how sensitive these parameters are may add more value.
    *   **Oversimplification:** The assumption that answer convergence *always* indicates sufficient reasoning is an oversimplification. The paper could benefit from discussing scenarios where answer convergence might be premature or misleading (e.g., due to biases or superficial patterns).
    *   **Limited Comparison to Baseline:** The paper compares the performance with the Original and CCoT, but lacks a more sophisticated baseline with similar objective.
    *   **Generalization Beyond Datasets:** The paper may not generalize to the open-ended tasks.
    *   **Minor Presentation Issue:** Some text are hard to read.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Encouraging further research into inference-time optimization techniques for LLMs.
    *   Providing practical tools for reducing the cost of deploying LLMs in real-world applications.
    *   Highlighting the importance of balancing efficiency and faithfulness in reasoning models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a useful contribution to the field of LLM efficiency by introducing and validating the concept of answer convergence for early stopping. The methods are practical, model-agnostic, and demonstrated to be effective across various benchmarks. While the novelty of individual components is not exceptionally high, the combination of techniques and the comprehensive empirical evaluation add significant value. The main weakness lies in the need for a more in-depth analysis of faithfulness and a more nuanced discussion of the limitations of answer convergence as a sole indicator of sufficient reasoning.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### VisuRiddles: Fine-grained Perception is a Primary Bottleneck for Multimodal Large Language Models in Abstract Visual Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02537v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Yan, Handong Zheng, Hao Wang, Liang Yin, Xingchen Liu, Zhenbiao Cao, Xinxing Su, Zihao Chen, Jihao Wu, Minghui Liao, Chao Weng, Wei Chen, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Bai
- **Abstract**: Recent strides in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have significantly advanced their performance in many reasoning tasks. However, Abstract Visual Reasoning (AVR) remains a critical challenge, primarily due to limitations in perceiving abstract graphics. To tackle this issue, we investigate the bottlenecks in current MLLMs and synthesize training data to improve their abstract visual perception. First, we propose VisuRiddles, a benchmark for AVR, featuring tasks meticulously constructed to assess models' reasoning capacities across five core dimensions and two high-level reasoning categories. Second, we introduce the Perceptual Riddle Synthesizer (PRS), an automated framework for generating riddles with fine-grained perceptual descriptions. PRS not only generates valuable training data for abstract graphics but also provides fine-grained perceptual description, crucially allowing for supervision over intermediate reasoning stages and thereby improving both training efficacy and model interpretability. Our extensive experimental results on VisuRiddles empirically validate that fine-grained visual perception is the principal bottleneck and our synthesis framework markedly enhances the performance of contemporary MLLMs on these challenging tasks. Our code and dataset will be released at https://github.com/yh-hust/VisuRiddles
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "VisuRiddles: Fine-grained Perception is a Primary Bottleneck for Multimodal Large Language Models in Abstract Visual Reasoning" addresses the challenge of Abstract Visual Reasoning (AVR) for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The authors argue that a key bottleneck is the *fine-grained visual perception* of abstract graphics, rather than reasoning abilities alone. They introduce VisuRiddles, a new benchmark designed to evaluate AVR across five core dimensions (Numerosity, Attributes, Style, Position, Spatiality) and two high-level reasoning categories (Raven and Sudoku). They also present the Perceptual Riddle Synthesizer (PRS), an automated framework for generating riddles with fine-grained perceptual descriptions. Experimental results demonstrate that current MLLMs struggle on VisuRiddles, and that training with PRS-generated data significantly improves performance by enhancing perceptual abstraction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focused approach to AVR.  While benchmarks for MLLMs exist, VisuRiddles is specifically designed to isolate and evaluate perceptual abilities within the context of abstract visual reasoning. The PRS framework is also a novel contribution. The idea of automated generation of perceptually described training data, enabling intermediate supervision, is a significant step beyond simple question-answer datasets.  The dissection of AVR into the five dimensions is thoughtful and helpful for analysis.

* **Significance:** The findings are significant because they challenge the conventional wisdom that MLLM limitations in visual tasks are solely due to reasoning deficits. The paper convincingly demonstrates that fine-grained perception is a major impediment. The ability to generate training data that improves perceptual abstraction has the potential to benefit a broad range of visual reasoning tasks beyond the specific benchmark.  The emphasis on interpretability through perceptual descriptions is also a positive direction.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of AVR and convincingly argues for the importance of fine-grained visual perception.
    * **Well-Designed Benchmark:** VisuRiddles appears to be a well-designed and comprehensive benchmark, explicitly targeting different dimensions of AVR.
    * **Effective Data Synthesis Framework:** The PRS framework is a strong contribution, enabling the generation of valuable training data. The framework's modularity based on rules provides good control over the generation process. The structured perceptual descriptions are a major advantage.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, showing significant performance improvements with PRS-generated data.
    * **Clear Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Synthetic Data Focus:** While the PRS framework is a major strength, relying heavily on synthetic data can raise concerns about generalizability to more complex real-world scenarios or data distributions beyond what the simulator captures. The paper should more explicitly address potential limitations of the synthesized data and discuss strategies to bridge the gap to real-world scenarios. There appears to be no discussion or empirical evaluation of how performance on VisuRiddles relates to performance on other existing, real-world datasets, even if only weakly correlated.
    * **Limited Model Evaluation:** While the paper evaluates a decent range of MLLMs, some of these models are outdated and/or superseded. It would be strengthened by including very recent state-of-the-art models (from early 2025) for a truly rigorous benchmark comparison.
    * **Over-claimed contribution** The claim that VisuRiddles is a "Primary Bottleneck" for AVR is debatable and perhaps overstated. While perceptual understanding is important, so too is effective reasoning and planning. The study focuses heavily on improving the former, and as a result the experiments do not fully demonstrate to what extent fine-grained perception limits broader AVR.
    * **Limited error analysis**: The paper analyzes a single case and some aggregate performance metrics, but a more systematic analysis of failure modes and the types of errors models make would further strengthen the findings.  For example, is there a correlation between specific perceptual dimensions and specific types of errors?

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by shifting the focus towards perceptual abstraction as a critical area for improvement in MLLMs. The VisuRiddles benchmark and the PRS framework could become valuable tools for researchers working on visual reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper makes a significant contribution to understanding and addressing limitations in AVR for MLLMs, several weaknesses prevent it from achieving a higher score. The reliance on synthetic data raises concerns about generalizability and requires further investigation. There is no link with existing benchmarks either in its use or discussion. The model evaluation could have been more up-to-date. A stronger error analysis could also reinforce the key findings. Nevertheless, the novelty of the approach (especially the PRS framework) and the clear empirical validation justify a good score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### CoRe-MMRAG: Cross-Source Knowledge Reconciliation for Multimodal RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02544v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Tian, Fan Liu, Jingyuan Zhang, Victoria W., Yupeng Hu, Liqiang Nie
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MMRAG) has been introduced to enhance Multimodal Large Language Models by incorporating externally retrieved multimodal knowledge, but it introduces two challenges: Parametric-Retrieved Knowledge Inconsistency (PRKI), where discrepancies between parametric and retrieved knowledge create uncertainty in determining reliability, and Visual-Textual Knowledge Inconsistency (VTKI), where misalignment between visual and textual sources disrupts entity representation. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{C}r\textbf{o}ss-source knowledge \textbf{Re}conciliation for \textbf{M}ulti\textbf{M}odal \textbf{RAG} (CoRe-MMRAG), a novel end-to-end framework that effectively reconciles inconsistencies across knowledge sources. CoRe-MMRAG follows a four-stage pipeline: it first generates an internal response from parametric knowledge, then selects the most relevant multimodal evidence via joint similarity assessment, generates an external response, and finally integrates both to produce a reliable answer. Additionally, a specialized training paradigm enhances knowledge source discrimination, multimodal integration, and unified answer generation. Experiments on KB-VQA benchmarks show that CoRe-MMRAG achieves substantial improvements over baseline methods, achieving 5.6\% and 9.3\% performance gains on InfoSeek and Encyclopedic-VQA, respectively. We release code and data at \href{https://github.com/TyangJN/CoRe-MMRAG}{https://github.com/TyangJN/CoRe-MMRAG}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoRe-MMRAG, a novel framework designed to improve Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MMRAG). MMRAG enhances Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) by incorporating retrieved textual and visual knowledge. However, MMRAG systems face two key challenges: Parametric-Retrieved Knowledge Inconsistency (PRKI), where internal model knowledge conflicts with retrieved information, and Visual-Textual Knowledge Inconsistency (VTKI), arising from misalignments between visual and textual sources in the retrieved content. CoRe-MMRAG addresses these issues with a four-stage pipeline: generating an initial response from the model's internal knowledge, selecting the most relevant multimodal evidence via joint similarity assessment, generating an external response based on the retrieved content, and integrating both responses to produce a reliable answer.  The paper also presents a specialized training paradigm to improve knowledge source discrimination, multimodal integration, and unified answer generation. Experiments on KB-VQA benchmarks (InfoSeek and Encyclopedic-VQA) demonstrate significant improvements over baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper effectively identifies and formalizes two critical challenges (PRKI and VTKI) in multimodal RAG systems, highlighting the limitations of current approaches.
    *   **Well-Structured Framework:** CoRe-MMRAG offers a logical and well-defined pipeline for addressing the identified inconsistencies. The four-stage process provides a structured way to reconcile different knowledge sources.
    *   **Targeted Training Paradigm:** The specialized training paradigm, with its objectives of knowledge source discrimination, multimodal integration, and unified answer generation, appears well-suited to improve model performance in the context of knowledge inconsistency.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments on KB-VQA benchmarks demonstrate substantial performance gains compared to existing methods.  The results on InfoSeek and Encyclopedic-VQA provide empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the framework and the training objectives, increasing the credibility of the results.
* **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Initial Retrieval Quality:** The paper acknowledges that the framework's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of initial retrieval using EVA-CLIP-8B. This reliance on an external component could limit the overall system performance, and potentially makes improvements in this area, a bottleneck.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The four-stage process and multiple training objectives require substantial computational resources, which could limit the practical deployment in resource-constrained environments. The paper could be improved by adding a runtime analysis.
    *   **Dataset Coverage and Generalization:** The paper demonstrates strong performance on two specific KB-VQA benchmarks, but the effectiveness of the approach on other types of multimodal tasks or real-world scenarios remains unexplored. The authors themselves acknowledge that performance on rare entities or edge cases may be suboptimal.
    *  **Incremental Novelty:**  While the combination of the four-stage pipeline and the inconsistency-aware training paradigm is presented as novel, the individual components have roots in prior work.  The novelty lies in the specific integration of these elements within the context of multimodal RAG.
    * **Limited Novelty in Case of Failure** In case the model fails, there is no recovery process described in the paper.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal learning by specifically addressing the challenges of knowledge inconsistency in MMRAG systems. While individual components of the framework have precedents in prior research, the specific combination of a four-stage pipeline with an inconsistency-aware training paradigm, targeting PRKI and VTKI, represents a novel approach.  The empirical results demonstrate the potential of this approach to improve the factual accuracy and reliability of MLLMs. The work is significant because it points towards addressing a crucial bottleneck in achieving robust and trustworthy multimodal reasoning. The analysis of PRKI and VTKI contributes to a deeper understanding of the challenges inherent in integrating external knowledge into MLLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by explicitly addressing critical challenges in multimodal RAG, proposing a well-structured framework, and demonstrating improved performance on benchmark datasets. The limitations related to retrieval dependence, computational cost, and dataset coverage are valid points of concern, indicating areas for future work. However, the clear problem formulation, well-designed approach, and solid empirical results justify a positive assessment.  The incremental novelty, while a factor, is offset by the significant performance improvements and the valuable insights gained into the nature of knowledge inconsistency in MLLMs.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Response-Level Rewards Are All You Need for Online Reinforcement Learning in LLMs: A Mathematical Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02553v1)
- **Authors**: Shenghua He, Tian Xia, Xuan Zhou, Hui Wei
- **Abstract**: We study a common challenge in reinforcement learning for large language models (LLMs): the Zero-Reward Assumption, where non-terminal actions (i.e., intermediate token generations) receive zero task-specific immediate reward, while only the final token receives a reward for the entire response. This assumption arises frequently in practice, as precise token-level rewards are often difficult or infeasible to obtain in LLM applications. In this work, we provide a unifying theoretical perspective. We introduce the Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem, which shows that the policy gradient based on true, unknown token-level rewards can be unbiasedly estimated using only a response-level reward model, regardless of whether the Zero-Reward Assumption holds or not, for algorithms in the REINFORCE and Actor-Critic families. This result reveals that widely used methods such as PPO, GRPO, ReMax, and RLOO inherently possess the capacity to model token-level reward signals, offering a theoretical justification for response-level reward approaches. Our findings pave the way for more practical, efficient LLM fine-tuning, allowing developers to treat training algorithms as black boxes and focus on improving the response-level reward model with auxiliary sub-models. We also offer a detailed analysis of popular RL and non-RL methods, comparing their theoretical foundations and practical advantages across common LLM tasks. Finally, we propose a new algorithm: Token-Reinforced Policy Optimization (TRePO), a theoretically grounded method that is simpler than PPO, matches GRPO in memory efficiency, and holds promise for broad applicability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of the Zero-Reward Assumption in reinforcement learning for large language models (LLMs), where only the final token receives a reward, making it difficult to assign credit to individual token generations. The authors introduce the Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem, demonstrating that a response-level reward model can unbiasedly estimate the policy gradient, even with the Zero-Reward Assumption.  They analyze popular RL methods (PPO, GRPO, etc.) based on this theorem, showing they inherently possess token-level modeling capacity. Finally, they propose Token-Reinforced Policy Optimization (TRePO), a new algorithm grounded in their theorem, aiming for simplicity and memory efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem is the paper's most significant contribution. It provides a theoretical justification for response-level rewards and explains the capabilities of existing RL algorithms. The theoretical connections that is made to existing methods, which might be known in parts within the community, and putting them under the same lense of Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem makes it highly novel. TRePO is novel in its design but its theoretical and practical capabilities stem directly from the presented theorem.
* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to simplify LLM fine-tuning. By showing that existing methods can handle token-level reward signals despite using response-level rewards, developers can focus on improving reward models rather than modifying RL algorithms. The new TRePO algorithm aims to be more memory efficient, which is an important practical advantage. However, the experimental result and comparisons are missing which makes the potential advantages of TREPO hard to evaluate.
* **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical foundation: The Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem provides a unifying perspective and a solid basis for further research.
    *   Practical implications: The paper offers practical guidance for developing RL algorithms for LLMs.
    *   Comprehensive analysis: The analysis of existing RL methods provides valuable insights into their capabilities and limitations.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   Lack of experimental validation: The paper would be stronger with experimental results comparing TRePO to existing methods. Right now TREPO is entirely on theoretical grounds.
    *   The implementation details for the sampling of trajectories in TRePO and the subsequent compute cost.
    *   Limited focus on reward model design: While the paper emphasizes the importance of reward models, it does not provide detailed guidance on how to design effective ones.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more practical and efficient LLM fine-tuning methods. The Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem could inspire new RL algorithms and reward model designs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the theory of reinforcement learning for LLMs. The Trajectory Policy Gradient Theorem offers a valuable theoretical insight and potentially simplifies algorithm design. However, the lack of experimental validation is a significant weakness. While the theoretical arguments are compelling, the paper is not a fully revolutionary piece.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### HiLO: High-Level Object Fusion for Autonomous Driving using Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02554v1)
- **Authors**: Timo Osterburg, Franz Albers, Christopher Diehl, Rajesh Pushparaj, Torsten Bertram
- **Abstract**: The fusion of sensor data is essential for a robust perception of the environment in autonomous driving. Learning-based fusion approaches mainly use feature-level fusion to achieve high performance, but their complexity and hardware requirements limit their applicability in near-production vehicles. High-level fusion methods offer robustness with lower computational requirements. Traditional methods, such as the Kalman filter, dominate this area. This paper modifies the Adapted Kalman Filter (AKF) and proposes a novel transformer-based high-level object fusion method called HiLO. Experimental results demonstrate improvements of $25.9$ percentage points in $\textrm{F}_1$ score and $6.1$ percentage points in mean IoU. Evaluation on a new large-scale real-world dataset demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed approaches. Their generalizability is further validated by cross-domain evaluation between urban and highway scenarios. Code, data, and models are available at https://github.com/rst-tu-dortmund/HiLO .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HiLO: High-Level Object Fusion for Autonomous Driving using Transformers" introduces a transformer-based high-level object fusion method, HiLO, designed for autonomous driving applications. It aims to improve robustness and generalizability in multi-sensor fusion while maintaining computational efficiency suitable for near-production vehicles. HiLO is compared against a modified Adapted Kalman Filter (AKF) baseline. The evaluation uses a large-scale real-world dataset, focusing on cross-domain performance between highway and urban driving scenarios. The paper demonstrates improvements in F1 score and mean IoU compared to the baselines and provides runtime analysis supporting its efficiency. The authors release code, data, and models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of a Transformer architecture for *high-level* object fusion in autonomous driving. Most prior work focuses on feature-level fusion. This is a worthwhile exploration, as it potentially offers advantages in robustness to sensor failures and reduced computational demands compared to feature-level approaches. The modification to the AKF by adding a measurement covariance term per sensor (AKFA) is a smaller, but still notable, contribution to the classical fusion space.

*   **Significance:** The significance comes from addressing a critical need in autonomous driving: robust and efficient sensor fusion. The paper explicitly targets near-production vehicle constraints, acknowledging that high-performing but computationally expensive methods are not always practical. The extensive cross-domain evaluation (highway vs. urban) is valuable and highlights the generalizability (or lack thereof) of different fusion approaches. This aspect contributes significantly to the real-world applicability of the research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-scale Dataset:** The evaluation on a dataset of approximately two million frames is a major strength. It provides a solid foundation for assessing the performance and generalizability of the proposed methods.
    *   **Cross-Domain Evaluation:** The rigorous cross-domain analysis is a key contribution. It directly addresses the issue of distribution shift, which is a significant challenge in real-world autonomous driving.
    *   **Runtime Analysis:** The paper provides runtime measurements, demonstrating HiLO's feasibility for near-production vehicles.
    *   **Open Source:** Releasing code, data, and models promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research.
    *   The paper clearly outlines the limitations of the current study and points towards future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are promising, the improvement of HiLO over the AKFA baseline in certain cross-domain settings might not be overwhelmingly dramatic.  The paper relies on significant gains over standard classical approaches, but the benefits in generalizability could be emphasized more.
    *   **Annotation Quality:** The paper acknowledges limitations in the quality of the automatically generated annotations. While filtering is applied, this remains a potential source of error. While automatic annotation generation is necessary to create a large dataset, addressing the quality of annotations is crucial. A comparison to manually labelled results would be helpful.
    *   **MOT Omission:** The paper mentions that Multi-Object Tracking (MOT) is left for future work, while the standard approach implements the tracking. This limits HiLO's ability to deal with occlusions and faulty detections by accumulating information over time.  The omission of tracking may affect the performance, and its impact on the results should be clearly stated.
    *   The description of how HiLO deals with occluded objects is not explicitly addressed. It's important to acknowledge if HiLO is unable to manage with occluded objects or not.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of sensor fusion for autonomous driving. The use of Transformers for high-level fusion is novel, and the comprehensive evaluation, particularly the cross-domain analysis, adds significant value. However, the annotation quality limitations and the absence of Multi-Object Tracking (MOT) slightly diminish the impact. The gains, while present, are not consistently overwhelming in all scenarios, particularly in cross-domain settings.  The strengths (dataset size, cross-domain evaluation) outweigh the weaknesses, but the annotation issue is important.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Kernel-based Unsupervised Embedding Alignment for Enhanced Visual Representation in Vision-language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02557v1)
- **Authors**: Shizhan Gong, Yankai Jiang, Qi Dou, Farzan Farnia
- **Abstract**: Vision-language models, such as CLIP, have achieved significant success in aligning visual and textual representations, becoming essential components of many multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) like LLaVA and OpenFlamingo. However, numerous studies have identified CLIP's limited fine-grained perception as a critical drawback, leading to substantial failures in downstream MLLMs. In contrast, vision-centric foundation models like DINOv2 demonstrate remarkable capabilities in capturing fine details from images. In this work, we propose a novel kernel-based method to align CLIP's visual representation with that of DINOv2, ensuring that the resulting embeddings maintain compatibility with text embeddings while enhancing perceptual capabilities. Our alignment objective is designed for efficient stochastic optimization. Following this image-only alignment fine-tuning, the visual encoder retains compatibility with the frozen text encoder and exhibits significant improvements in zero-shot object recognition, fine-grained spatial reasoning, and localization. By integrating the aligned visual encoder, downstream MLLMs also demonstrate enhanced performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitation of CLIP's visual encoder in capturing fine-grained details, which can hinder the performance of downstream Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The authors propose a novel kernel-based method to align CLIP's visual representations with those of DINOv2, a vision-centric foundation model known for its strong capabilities in capturing fine details. The alignment process is performed using image-only data and a custom kernel-based objective function designed to maintain compatibility with CLIP's text embeddings. The authors show that this approach enhances CLIP's zero-shot performance on object recognition, spatial reasoning, and localization tasks. Furthermore, they demonstrate that integrating the aligned visual encoder into MLLMs such as LLaVA and OpenFlamingo results in enhanced performance on visual question answering benchmarks, without requiring fine-tuning of the LLM component.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the specific approach of using kernel-based alignment to transfer the fine-grained visual perception capabilities of DINOv2 to CLIP while preserving compatibility with CLIP's text embeddings. While aligning representations and using kernel methods are established techniques, their specific combination and application in this context is novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the visual understanding capabilities of existing vision-language models without requiring extensive retraining or architectural changes. This is particularly valuable for resource-constrained settings where full retraining is not feasible.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is computationally efficient, requiring only image-only fine-tuning.
    *   The alignment preserves compatibility with existing text encoders and downstream MLLMs, eliminating the need for full retraining.
    *   The results demonstrate significant improvements on a variety of benchmarks, indicating strong generalizability.
    *   The kernel-based alignment provides a flexible way of transferring the visual knowledge.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance may be bounded by the limitations of the original CLIP architecture. A more advanced architecture may benefit better with this approach.
    *   The approach relies on having a pre-trained target model (DINOv2). If the target model is lacking, the transferred capabilities would be lacking.
    *   While the computational cost is less than full retraining, the alignment fine-tuning step still requires resources.
    *   The improvements depend heavily on the design and parameter tuning of the kernel function. More explanation on Kernel function choices will be helpful for reproduction.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging the development of more efficient transfer learning techniques for vision-language models. The kernel-based alignment approach could be adapted for other settings where preserving compatibility with existing components is crucial.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.

*   The paper presents a novel approach to address a well-defined problem in the field of vision-language models, specifically the limitation of CLIP's fine-grained visual perception. The combination of kernel alignment and transfer learning from a vision-centric model is innovative.
*   The empirical results are strong, demonstrating significant improvements on a variety of benchmarks and downstream tasks. This reinforces the potential impact of the method.
*   The computational efficiency of the approach is a significant advantage, making it more accessible and practical.
*   However, the novelty is incremental, building on existing techniques. Also the paper relies on a pretrained target model, if the target model is lacking in capacity, then this alignment based framework may also be limited. More description about the kernel selection can improve the reproducibility of this paper.
*   The contribution is significant for addressing a specific problem of CLIP, but also has broader potential in many VLMs in the future.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DCI: Dual-Conditional Inversion for Boosting Diffusion-Based Image Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02560v1)
- **Authors**: Zixiang Li, Haoyu Wang, Wei Wang, Chuangchuang Tan, Yunchao Wei, Yao Zhao
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in image generation and editing tasks. Inversion within these models aims to recover the latent noise representation for a real or generated image, enabling reconstruction, editing, and other downstream tasks. However, to date, most inversion approaches suffer from an intrinsic trade-off between reconstruction accuracy and editing flexibility. This limitation arises from the difficulty of maintaining both semantic alignment and structural consistency during the inversion process. In this work, we introduce Dual-Conditional Inversion (DCI), a novel framework that jointly conditions on the source prompt and reference image to guide the inversion process. Specifically, DCI formulates the inversion process as a dual-condition fixed-point optimization problem, minimizing both the latent noise gap and the reconstruction error under the joint guidance. This design anchors the inversion trajectory in both semantic and visual space, leading to more accurate and editable latent representations. Our novel setup brings new understanding to the inversion process. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DCI achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple editing tasks, significantly improving both reconstruction quality and editing precision. Furthermore, we also demonstrate that our method achieves strong results in reconstruction tasks, implying a degree of robustness and generalizability approaching the ultimate goal of the inversion process.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dual-Conditional Inversion (DCI), a novel framework for improving image editing using diffusion models. DCI addresses the trade-off between reconstruction accuracy and editing flexibility in existing inversion methods. It achieves this by jointly conditioning the inversion process on both the source prompt and a reference image, minimizing the latent noise gap and reconstruction error through a dual-conditional fixed-point optimization.  DCI consists of two key stages: Reference-Guided Noise Correction, which aligns predicted noise with the source image, and Fixed-Point Latent Refinement, which ensures self-consistency of the generative process. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across various editing tasks, showing improvements in both reconstruction quality and editing precision. The framework can be plugged-and-played into different diffusion models without requiring retraining or modification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the dual-conditional approach for inversion. Existing methods often focus on either the text prompt or the image itself, but DCI simultaneously leverages both through a fixed-point optimization problem. The Reference-Guided Noise Correction is a simple, yet effective way to anchor the inversion to the source image, which significantly reduces the latent gap. The Fixed-Point Latent Refinement also promotes self-consistency. Overall, this integrated approach presents a novel perspective on the diffusion-based image editing paradigm.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant limitation in diffusion-based image editing: the inversion process's trade-off between accuracy and flexibility. By demonstrably improving both, DCI unlocks more reliable and controllable image editing. The plug-and-play nature of the method enhances its potential impact, as it can be easily adopted by researchers and practitioners already working with diffusion models. The comprehensive experiments across multiple editing tasks further strengthens the significance of the paper. The increased fidelity during editing and reconstruction has a direct impact on the quality of the resulting edits and reduces the prevalence of artifacts.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problems associated with existing inversion methods and motivates the need for DCI.

    *   **Technically Sound Approach:** The proposed DCI framework is well-designed and theoretically grounded. The dual-conditioning mechanism and fixed-point optimization are logically sound.

    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Extensive experiments across various editing tasks and datasets demonstrate the superiority of DCI over existing methods. Quantitative metrics and qualitative results support the claims.

    *   **Plug-and-Play Capability:** The method's ability to be integrated into existing diffusion models without retraining or modification makes it practical and widely applicable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Ablation Study:** While an ablation study is provided, a more comprehensive exploration of the contribution of each component within DCI (the reference-guided noise correction *vs.* the fixed-point latent refinement) would further strengthen the results. The analysis primarily focused on hyperparameter variations.

    *   **Potential for Increased Computational Cost:** While the paper claims computational efficiency, the iterative nature of the Fixed-Point Latent Refinement might introduce additional computational overhead, depending on the number of iterations required for convergence. Although the paper claims quick convergence, a comparison of actual inference times against baselines would be beneficial.

    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Encoder:** The reliance on a pre-trained VAE encoder (`E`) for extracting the reference noise might introduce biases or limitations based on the encoder's performance. A discussion on the sensitivity of DCI to different encoder architectures would be useful.

*   **Potential Influence:** DCI is likely to influence future research in diffusion-based image editing by providing a more accurate and controllable inversion process. The dual-conditioning approach could be extended and adapted for other downstream tasks in generative modeling. It can serve as a solid foundation for more research on improving the fidelity and controllability of diffusion-based editing.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

DCI presents a novel and effective approach to improving inversion for diffusion-based image editing, addressing a critical trade-off between reconstruction accuracy and editing flexibility. While the ablation study could be slightly more detailed, and the computational cost requires more explicit evaluation, the strengths of the paper, including its clear problem definition, technically sound approach, and comprehensive experimental results, outweigh these minor weaknesses. The plug-and-play capability and potential influence on future research warrant a score of 8. It represents a significant and impactful contribution to the field. The improvements in both reconstruction quality and editability are substantial and well-demonstrated. Therefore, a high score is justified.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Pruning General Large Language Models into Customized Expert Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02561v1)
- **Authors**: Yirao Zhao, Guizhen Chen, Kenji Kawaguchi, Lidong Bing, Wenxuan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have revolutionized natural language processing, yet their substantial model sizes often require substantial computational resources. To preserve computing resources and accelerate inference speed, it is crucial to prune redundant parameters, especially for experienced users who often need compact expert models tailored to specific downstream scenarios. However, most existing pruning methods focus on preserving the model's general capabilities, often requiring extensive post-training or suffering from degraded performance due to coarse-grained pruning. In this work, we design a $\underline{Cus}$tom $\underline{Prun}$ing method ($\texttt{Cus-Prun}$) to prune a large general model into a smaller lightweight expert model, which is positioned along the "language", "domain" and "task" dimensions. By identifying and pruning irrelevant neurons of each dimension, $\texttt{Cus-Prun}$ creates expert models without any post-training. Our experiments demonstrate that $\texttt{Cus-Prun}$ consistently outperforms other methods, achieving minimal loss in both expert and general capabilities across various models from different model families and sizes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Custom Pruning (Cus-Prun), a novel method for pruning large language models (LLMs) into smaller, specialized expert models.  Unlike existing pruning techniques that primarily focus on maintaining general capabilities, Cus-Prun aims to create models tailored to specific needs along three dimensions: language, domain, and task. The method identifies and prunes irrelevant neurons for each dimension by analyzing their impact on the output of corresponding corpora. Cus-Prun can create expert models without the need for post-training.  The paper presents comprehensive experiments across various model families and sizes, demonstrating that Cus-Prun outperforms other pruning methods, achieving minimal loss in both expert and general capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the targeted approach to pruning based on language, domain, and task. Existing pruning methods often treat the model as a black box, focusing on preserving general knowledge or reducing parameters without explicitly considering the intended use case. By identifying irrelevant neurons specific to each dimension and pruning the intersections, Cus-Prun offers a more fine-grained and customizable approach.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical need for smaller, efficient LLMs tailored to specific applications. The high computational cost of large general-purpose LLMs limits their deployment in many real-world scenarios. Cus-Prun addresses this by providing a way to create compact expert models that maintain high performance in their intended area of expertise while reducing the overall model size. This has implications for edge computing, resource-constrained environments, and applications where efficiency is paramount.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-defined methodology.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive and demonstrate the effectiveness of Cus-Prun across various models and settings.
    *   The approach is adaptable to different granularity levels, allowing users to customize expert models based on one, two, or three dimensions.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The construction of corpora specific to each dimension requires manual effort or reliance on other models. The paper could benefit from exploring automated or more efficient corpus generation techniques. While the generated corpora are said to be easily constructed from "relevant plain text documents," it's not clear how the model ensures that these documents are representative of the dimension or how the models ensure that the quality of these generated documents do not impact pruning.
    *   The paper assumes that there is a clear and distinct boundary between a relevant vs irrelevant neuron, but the language model weights and activation values are on a spectrum, and it might be more valuable to develop a method to keep neurons that contribute more than others.
    *   The paper lacks a thorough discussion of the limitations. While the paper notes that it does not capture query format or input structures, this seems to be a fairly critical factor. The assumption seems to be that one corpus could represent the dimension, but more details could be given on how these documents are chosen or constructed and why they are truly representative.
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on accuracy and Rouge-L scores. A more in-depth analysis of the generated text, including metrics like coherence, fluency, and informativeness, would further strengthen the evaluation.
    *   The improvement is still relatively low, roughly less than 2%, with significantly more complexity in implementation and more dependence on hyperparameters.

*   **Potential Influence:** Cus-Prun has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and specialized LLMs. Its targeted pruning approach could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners to create custom models for specific tasks and applications. The method also opens up new avenues for research in model compression and adaptation.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM pruning by introducing a novel and practical approach for creating specialized expert models. While the method has some limitations, its clear advantages and the comprehensive experimental results justify a relatively high score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MLaGA: Multimodal Large Language and Graph Assistant
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02568v1)
- **Authors**: Dongzhe Fan, Yi Fang, Jiajin Liu, Djellel Difallah, Qiaoyu Tan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated substantial efficacy in advancing graph-structured data analysis. Prevailing LLM-based graph methods excel in adapting LLMs to text-rich graphs, wherein node attributes are text descriptions. However, their applications to multimodal graphs--where nodes are associated with diverse attribute types, such as texts and images--remain underexplored, despite their ubiquity in real-world scenarios. To bridge the gap, we introduce the Multimodal Large Language and Graph Assistant (MLaGA), an innovative model that adeptly extends LLM capabilities to facilitate reasoning over complex graph structures and multimodal attributes. We first design a structure-aware multimodal encoder to align textual and visual attributes within a unified space through a joint graph pre-training objective. Subsequently, we implement a multimodal instruction-tuning approach to seamlessly integrate multimodal features and graph structures into the LLM through lightweight projectors. Extensive experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of MLaGA compared to leading baseline methods, achieving superior performance in diverse graph learning tasks under both supervised and transfer learning scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MLaGA (Multimodal Large Language and Graph Assistant), a novel framework that extends the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) to reason over complex graph structures with multimodal node attributes (text and images).  MLaGA addresses the limitation of existing graph LLMs, which primarily focus on text-rich graphs and struggle with multimodal graphs.
