The justifications for claiming Mamba is uniquely suited and superior, lack empirical rigor and must be meticulously supported. This architecture novelty, while present, is largely incremental and lacks profound architectural advancement. More exhaustive comparisons against alternatives and in-depth analyses are required to elevate the value and impact of this contribution, as is more careful consideration of the trade-offs in perceptual versus distortion. While combining two state-of-the-art architectures and reporting better results is good, further effort is needed to justify the choice of architectures as the best choices. A lower-complexity and/or equally-performing CNN-based model would undermine the claim.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### KVCache Cache in the Wild: Characterizing and Optimizing KVCache Cache at a Large Cloud Provider
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02634v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Wang, Jinbo Han, Xingda Wei, Sijie Shen, Dingyan Zhang, Chenguang Fang, Rong Chen, Wenyuan Yu, Haibo Chen
- **Abstract**: Serving large language models (LLMs) is important for cloud providers, and caching intermediate results (KV\$) after processing each request substantially improves serving throughput and latency. However, there is limited understanding of how LLM serving benefits from KV\$ caching, where system design decisions like cache eviction policies are highly workload-dependent. In this paper, we present the first systematic characterization of the KV\$ workload patterns from one of the leading LLM service providers. We draw observations that were not covered by previous studies focusing on synthetic workloads, including: KV\$ reuses are skewed across requests, where reuses between single-turn requests are equally important as multi-turn requests; the reuse time and probability are diverse considering all requests, but for a specific request category, the pattern tends to be predictable; and the overall cache size required for an ideal cache hit ratio is moderate. Based on the characterization, we further propose a workload-aware cache eviction policy that improves the serving performance under real-world traces, especially with limited cache capacity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a characterization and optimization of KVCache (KV$) for serving large language models (LLMs) in a large cloud provider. It analyzes real-world LLM serving traces (to-C and to-B workloads), revealing insights into KV$ reuse patterns, reuse time, and cache capacity requirements that differ from findings based on synthetic workloads.  Key findings include:  single-turn requests significantly contribute to KV$ reuse (especially in to-B workloads), reuse time distributions are request-type dependent, and  KV$ lifespan is relatively short, enabling the use of smaller caches.  Based on the characterization, the authors propose a workload-aware KV$ eviction policy and demonstrate its improved performance in real-world traces compared to standard policies like LRU and LFU, achieving better cache hit ratios and reduced response times.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its characterization of *real-world* LLM serving workloads for KV$ caching.  Prior work has often relied on synthetic or limited datasets. The observation that single-turn API requests in to-B workloads contribute significantly to KV$ reuses is a genuinely novel and important finding, challenging the prevailing assumption that multi-turn conversational workloads dominate KV$ reuses.  The analysis of reuse time and lifespan distributions, tied to request types, also provides valuable insights previously lacking. However, the workload-aware cache eviction policy itself, while effective, is not conceptually groundbreaking; it builds upon existing GDFS-like approaches by incorporating the unique characteristics revealed in the analysis. The application of an exponential distribution to capture the reuse rate seems like a good solution, even though not very advanced.
* **Significance:** Understanding and optimizing KV$ is crucial for cost-effective LLM serving. The characterization provides practical guidance for designing KV$ caches tailored to realistic workload patterns. Specifically, that the KV$ required lifetime is short and therefore can be cached on-GPU is a meaningful finding. The proposed workload-aware eviction policy demonstrates tangible performance improvements, suggesting its potential for adoption in production systems. The paper bridges the gap between research and practice by analyzing real-world data. The provided insights are not limited to Alibaba's platform but can be generalized and employed by other LLM providers and cloud infrastructures to optimize their caching and serving strategies. In addition, a real-world trace has been made available, which should allow for further exploration of the topic.
* **Strengths:**
    *  Uses real-world data at scale, providing valuable insights that synthetic workloads cannot capture.
    *  Clear and well-structured presentation of findings, with compelling visualizations.
    *  Provides practical recommendations for KV$ cache design and optimization.
    *  The evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed eviction policy on real-world workloads.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *  The workload-aware eviction policy, while effective, is an incremental improvement on existing techniques rather than a radical innovation. The core idea of prioritising blocks that are likely to be reused again is something explored in prior work.
    *  The analysis, while comprehensive, is limited to one specific cloud provider's data. While the authors argue for generalizability, different LLM service characteristics might exist elsewhere.
    *  The paper only evaluates the improved KV$ eviction policy and doesn't explore the design of other system components that might benefit from the findings.
    *  There's limited discussion on the computational overhead of the workload-aware policy (although stated to be negligible, further detail would be beneficial).
    *  The reliance on salted hashes to protect data means that more advanced LLM techniques can't be employed, but that's a common constraint in these papers.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research and development efforts in LLM serving, particularly in optimizing KV$ cache policies.  The insights gleaned from real-world data will help inform design choices and guide future research directions. The publicly available trace data further encourages future exploration.

**Overall:** The paper makes a significant contribution by characterizing real-world LLM serving workloads and providing actionable insights for KV$ cache optimization. While the proposed eviction policy is not a revolutionary breakthrough, its effectiveness, combined with the valuable characterization, justifies a positive assessment.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale for the Score:** The score reflects the balance between the significant contribution of the real-world workload characterization (novel and impactful) and the more incremental nature of the proposed caching policy. The score is in the upper range reflecting the value of these traces being made available. The limitations around focusing on only one Cloud provider is also taken into account.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Truly Assessing Fluid Intelligence of Large Language Models through Dynamic Reasoning Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02648v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Yang, MingKang Chen, Qihua Liu, Mengkang Hu, Qiguang Chen, Gengrui Zhang, Shuyue Hu, Guangtao Zhai, Yu Qiao, Yu Wang, Wenqi Shao, Ping Luo
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive reasoning capacities that mirror human-like thinking. However, whether LLMs possess genuine fluid intelligence (i.e., the ability to reason abstractly and generalize rules in novel situations) remains an open question. Existing reasoning benchmarks either focus on domain-specific knowledge (crystallized intelligence) or lack interpretability. To address these limitations, we propose DRE-Bench, a dynamic reasoning evaluation benchmark grounded in a hierarchical cognitive framework. DRE-Bench consists of 36 abstract reasoning tasks organized across four cognitive levels, with each task featuring multiple dynamic variants that test the same underlying latent rule. This design enables fine-grained, interpretable, and reliable assessments of fluid intelligence. We evaluate a range of state-of-the-art LLMs, including both general LLMs (GPT-4o, Claude 3.7) and reasoning LLMs (o1, DeepSeek-R1, QwQ, Skywork-OR1). Experimental results reveal that although most LLMs achieve competent and robust performance in low-level cognition, they struggle with high-level cognition and exhibit limited generalization as task complexity grows. Our findings highlight the gap between current LLMs and true human-like fluid intelligence and offer a new path for systematically tracking reasoning progress in LLMs.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces DRE-Bench, a new benchmark for evaluating the fluid intelligence of large language models (LLMs). DRE-Bench consists of abstract reasoning tasks organized across a hierarchical cognitive framework, designed to provide fine-grained, interpretable, and reliable assessments. The benchmark features dynamic variants of each task, testing the same underlying latent rule. The paper evaluates several state-of-the-art LLMs using DRE-Bench and reveals that while most LLMs perform well in low-level cognition, they struggle with high-level cognition and generalization as task complexity increases. The authors argue that these results highlight the gap between current LLMs and true human-like fluid intelligence, and offer DRE-Bench as a new path for tracking reasoning progress.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation, but its novelty is somewhat limited. While existing benchmarks focus either on crystallized intelligence or lack interpretability, DRE-Bench attempts to address both shortcomings. The key strengths of the paper lie in the creation of a structured, code-verifiable benchmark with dynamic task generation and the alignment with a cognitive hierarchy. The demonstration that LLMs struggle with higher-level cognitive tasks, despite strong performance on lower-level ones, is also significant.

However, several aspects warrant a more critical assessment. The idea of using abstract reasoning to evaluate fluid intelligence is not new; existing benchmarks such as ARC already explore this domain. DRE-Bench's distinction lies mainly in its hierarchical cognitive framework and dynamic task generation. The cognitive hierarchy is inspired by existing work, thus the novelty lies more in its specific application and implementation within the DRE-Bench context. The dynamic task generation provides high confidence on correctness but the design of the tasks still required human annotation on "constraints", "generators", and "solvers" functions. This may imply that expansion of the DRE-Bench by new tasks will still need human input.

Moreover, the paper's findings, while significant, largely confirm what is already suspected: that current LLMs often struggle with genuine generalization and abstract reasoning, particularly as complexity increases. While the detailed analysis provided by DRE-Bench is helpful for understanding these limitations in a structured manner, the overall conclusion isn't particularly groundbreaking. The scope of 'reasoning' tasks that the DRE-Bench currently covers is also limited to visual abstract reasoning.

The paper also overstates its alignment with true human-like fluid intelligence. The used Ricardo cognitive hierarchy, while reasonable, is only an approximation of human cognition. It's likely that other dimensions of human intelligence are not covered within the four chosen aspects. Additionally, the experiments on auxiliary visual information produced inconclusive results.

Furthermore, the comparison against ARC can be more detailed. Although ARC is not structured into cognitive levels and cannot control complexity, the paper could provide insight on the commonalities and differences of the tasks that the existing LLMs cannot solve.

Justification of Score:

Given the considerations above, a score of 6 is appropriate. DRE-Bench represents a solid engineering effort, providing a well-designed and implementable benchmark for assessing LLM fluid intelligence. It contributes to the field by providing a more structured and interpretable evaluation framework. However, the core idea of using abstract reasoning for this purpose is not entirely novel, and the paper's findings, while reinforcing existing concerns, are not revolutionary. The reliance on human annotation and design also limits the scalability and automation of benchmark creation. While valuable, DRE-Bench's contribution is incremental rather than transformative.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Prompts to Protection: Large Language Model-Enabled In-Context Learning for Smart Public Safety UAV
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02649v1)
- **Authors**: Yousef Emami, Hao Zhou, Miguel Gutierrez Gaitan, Kai Li, Luis Almeida, Zhu Han
- **Abstract**: A public safety Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) enhances situational awareness in emergency response. Its agility and ability to optimize mobility and establish Line-of-Sight (LoS) communication make it increasingly vital for managing emergencies such as disaster response, search and rescue, and wildfire monitoring. While Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) has been applied to optimize UAV navigation and control, its high training complexity, low sample efficiency, and simulation-to-reality gap limit its practicality in public safety. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a compelling alternative. With strong reasoning and generalization capabilities, LLMs can adapt to new tasks through In-Context Learning (ICL), which enables task adaptation via natural language prompts and example-based guidance, without retraining. Deploying LLMs at the network edge, rather than in the cloud, further reduces latency and preserves data privacy, thereby making them suitable for real-time, mission-critical public safety UAVs. This paper proposes the integration of LLM-enabled ICL with public safety UAV to address the key functions, such as path planning and velocity control, in the context of emergency response. We present a case study on data collection scheduling where the LLM-enabled ICL framework can significantly reduce packet loss compared to conventional approaches, while also mitigating potential jailbreaking vulnerabilities. Finally, we discuss LLM optimizers and specify future research directions. The ICL framework enables adaptive, context-aware decision-making for public safety UAV, thus offering a lightweight and efficient solution for enhancing UAV autonomy and responsiveness in emergencies.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) and In-Context Learning (ICL) to improve the autonomy and responsiveness of Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) in public safety applications.  It argues that traditional Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) approaches have limitations in emergency scenarios due to high training complexity, low sample efficiency, and simulation-to-reality gaps. The paper suggests that LLMs, deployed at the network edge, can adapt to new tasks quickly through ICL, using natural language prompts and example-based guidance without requiring retraining.  The authors classify key functions in public safety UAVs (path planning, velocity control, data collection scheduling, power management) and provide task descriptions outlining how ICL can be applied. They present a case study on data collection scheduling, demonstrating reduced packet loss compared to conventional approaches and discuss ways to mitigate jailbreaking attacks. The paper also explores LLM optimizers and suggests future research directions, including the use of diffusion models for generating diverse training examples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The concept of using LLMs and specifically ICL for UAV control, particularly in public safety, demonstrates a good level of novelty. While LLMs are being explored in various domains, their application to real-time, mission-critical UAV operations, focusing on the challenges of rapid adaptation and resource constraints, is a worthwhile contribution. The system design using LLMs to act as optimizers is clever.  The classification of key UAV functions for public safety and the specific task descriptions with examples are helpful in defining the scope of the problem.

**Significance:**  The potential impact of this work is significant. Improving UAV autonomy and responsiveness in emergency scenarios can have tangible benefits for first responders and overall operational efficiency. The focus on edge deployment addresses critical issues of latency and data privacy, which are major concerns for time-sensitive and sensitive information gathering operations. The demonstration of improved performance in data collection scheduling and the awareness of vulnerabilities to jailbreaking attacks are important contributions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of traditional DRL approaches in the context of emergency response UAVs.
*   **Well-Defined ICL Framework:** The proposed framework for integrating LLM-enabled ICL with UAV functions is well-structured and easy to follow.
*   **Practical Case Study:**  The data collection scheduling case study provides concrete evidence of the potential benefits of the proposed approach.
*   **Comprehensive Discussion:** The discussion on LLM optimizers, future research directions (especially the use of diffusion models), and the consideration of adversarial attacks demonstrate a thorough understanding of the problem space.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While the data collection scheduling case study is informative, the paper would benefit from more extensive empirical validation.  The paper lacks details about how the jailbreak attack simulation was configured and performed, limiting insight. It’s not clear if the improved results from data collection are statistically significant, or what the benchmark approach is and why the ICL approach is better
*   **Lack of comparison** the method lacks a proper comparison to state-of-the-art techniques for the control of UAVs.
*   **Scalability and Real-World Deployment:**  The paper acknowledges the challenges of deploying LLMs at the edge, but it would be valuable to provide more detailed insights into how these challenges can be addressed in practice. What are the minimal compute resources required to achieve a specified performance.
*   **Convergence Properties:** The paper mentions the need to quantify the convergence properties of LLM-based optimization frameworks. Without a better discussion around the limitations of prompt design, it's impossible to assess how practical the proposed solution would be in practice.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a compelling argument for using LLMs and ICL to enhance the capabilities of public safety UAVs. The proposed framework addresses critical limitations of traditional DRL approaches and offers a promising path towards more adaptive, context-aware UAV operations. The case study provides initial evidence of the potential benefits, but more extensive empirical validation is needed to fully assess the practicality and scalability of the proposed approach. The focus on key areas of concern is valuable, but a comparison would be desirable.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a novel solution to a relevant and practically important problem. However, the limited empirical validation and the lack of a deeper discussion around convergence properties and deployment challenges prevent it from achieving a higher score. In particular, more detail on how jailbreaking attacks were tested in the case study would be valuable. Additionally, providing experimental data with real-world (rather than synthetic) scenarios would be beneficial to the paper's credibility. Thus a score of 7 is appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Computational Thinking Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02658v1)
- **Authors**: Kechi Zhang, Ge Li, Jia Li, Huangzhao Zhang, Jingjing Xu, Hao Zhu, Lecheng Wang, Jia Li, Yihong Dong, Jing Mai, Bin Gu, Zhi Jin
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable reasoning capabilities, they often struggle with complex tasks that require specific thinking paradigms, such as divide-and-conquer and procedural deduction, \etc Previous researches integrate external, reliable tools to alleviate logical inconsistencies and hallucinations in LLMs' problem-solving processes. However, we argue that the root challenge is more profound: LLMs lack the complex thinking paradigms (\ie, computational thinking) during reasoning. In this paper, we propose Computational Thinking Model (CTM), a novel framework that incorporates computational thinking paradigms into LLMs. This framework enables LLMs to reformulate complex problems through decomposition, abstraction, reduction, and simulation, among other techniques. Specifically, live code execution is seamlessly integrated into the reasoning process, allowing CTM to think by computing. CTM directly instills computational thinking objectives into LLMs through tailored reinforcement learning rewards, which encourages problem simplification, modular planning, and iterative verification. We conduct extensive evaluations on multiple code generation and mathematical benchmarks. The results demonstrate that CTM outperforms conventional reasoning models and tool-augmented baselines in terms of accuracy, interpretability, and generalizability. We hope this study offers valuable insights for AI reasoning, where LLMs can transform problems into robust, verifiable, and scalable computational workflows, much like computer scientists do.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Computational Thinking Model (CTM), a novel framework designed to integrate computational thinking paradigms into large language models (LLMs). The key idea is to enhance LLMs' reasoning capabilities by providing a live code execution environment where they can reformulate complex problems through decomposition, abstraction, reduction, and simulation. The framework uses a two-phase training approach: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a dataset of code-mixed reasoning tasks, followed by reinforcement learning (RL) with a custom reward function that encourages problem simplification, modular planning, and iterative verification. Experiments on code generation and mathematical benchmarks demonstrate that CTM outperforms conventional reasoning models and tool-augmented baselines in terms of accuracy, interpretability, and generalizability. The paper argues that by adopting the thinking paradigms of computer scientists, LLMs can transform problems into robust, verifiable, and scalable computational workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the explicit integration of computational thinking principles as a core reasoning strategy within LLMs. While prior work has focused on augmenting LLMs with external tools (e.g., code interpreters), CTM attempts to instill the *process* of computational thinking itself. The use of code execution not merely as a tool, but as an integral part of the thinking process is a distinguishing characteristic. The introduction of a specific training methodology (SFT + RL with tailored rewards) to encourage these thinking patterns is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is potentially high. Current LLMs often struggle with complex, structured reasoning tasks due to their reliance on purely generative processes and lack of systematic verification. By enabling LLMs to "think by computing" and apply computational thinking paradigms, CTM could lead to more reliable and robust solutions in domains requiring precision, consistency, and compositionality. The improved interpretability resulting from the interleaving of natural language and code is also a significant advantage.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a key limitation of existing LLMs: the lack of explicit computational thinking.
    *   The proposed CTM framework offers a promising approach to address this limitation.
    *   The two-phase training strategy and custom reward function are well-motivated and designed.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements over baselines on a variety of benchmarks.
    *   The qualitative analysis provides insights into how CTM influences reasoning trajectories.
    *   The case study provides an example of how CTM uses self-validation to correct errors.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on code execution introduces a dependence on the reliability and efficiency of the execution environment.
    *   The definition and annotation of "code-inspired reasoning" behaviors could be subjective. A more rigorous and automated annotation process would strengthen the analysis.
    *   While the results are promising, the benchmarks primarily focus on code generation and mathematical reasoning.  It would be helpful to evaluate CTM on a broader range of tasks to assess its generalizability further. Also, the specific implementation of the framework makes it hard to reproduce.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth comparison to existing tool-augmented approaches. While the distinction between using tools and "thinking by computing" is clear conceptually, the practical advantages in terms of performance and efficiency should be examined in more detail.

*   **Potential Influence:** If CTM can be scaled effectively and adapted to other domains, it could have a significant impact on AI reasoning. The framework's emphasis on interpretable reasoning and verifiable computation could lead to more trustworthy and reliable AI systems. The work could also inspire further research into the integration of cognitive processes and external computation within LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of AI reasoning by explicitly integrating computational thinking principles into LLMs. The CTM framework and associated training methodology show promising results on a range of code-related and mathematical benchmarks. While there are some limitations, such as the reliance on code execution and the need for further generalizability testing, the potential impact of this work is substantial. The paper is well-written, clearly explains the concepts, and provides thorough experimental evidence. A score of 7 reflects the novelty and potential impact of the research, while also acknowledging the limitations and areas for future improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are Economists Always More Introverted? Analyzing Consistency in Persona-Assigned LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02659v1)
- **Authors**: Manon Reusens, Bart Baesens, David Jurgens
- **Abstract**: Personalized Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in diverse applications, where they are assigned a specific persona - such as a happy high school teacher - to guide their responses. While prior research has examined how well LLMs adhere to predefined personas in writing style, a comprehensive analysis of consistency across different personas and task types is lacking. In this paper, we introduce a new standardized framework to analyze consistency in persona-assigned LLMs. We define consistency as the extent to which a model maintains coherent responses when assigned the same persona across different tasks and runs. Our framework evaluates personas across four different categories (happiness, occupation, personality, and political stance) spanning multiple task dimensions (survey writing, essay generation, social media post generation, single turn, and multi-turn conversations). Our findings reveal that consistency is influenced by multiple factors, including the assigned persona, stereotypes, and model design choices. Consistency also varies across tasks, increasing with more structured tasks and additional context. All code is available on GitHub.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Are Economists Always More Introverted? Analyzing Consistency in Persona-Assigned LLMs" introduces a framework for analyzing the consistency of personalized Large Language Models (LLMs). It investigates how consistently LLMs adhere to assigned personas across different tasks (survey answering, essay writing, social media posts, and conversations) and categories (happiness, occupation, personality, and political stance). The research reveals that persona consistency is influenced by the assigned persona itself, task structure, and the presence of stereotypes and model defaults. More specifically, happiness and occupation personas are expressed more consistently than personality and political stances, and certain tasks like surveys and essays lead to higher consistency than open-ended conversations. The research also identifies spillover effects where assigning a persona impacts other, unassigned characteristics, often reflecting social stereotypes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive and multi-faceted approach to evaluating consistency in persona-assigned LLMs. Prior works often focus on limited persona types (primarily personality) or rely on prompt perturbations, whereas this paper provides a standardized framework across various persona categories and task types. The inclusion of multi-turn conversations (multichat) as an evaluation dimension is also a novel contribution. Also, their insight on inter-persona consistency and the spill-over effects are interesting insights to the community.
*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the responsible deployment of personalized LLMs. Understanding the factors influencing consistency and the potential for unintended spillover effects is crucial for ensuring reliable and predictable model behavior, especially in high-stakes domains. The standardized framework provides a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners to evaluate and compare different LLMs in terms of persona adherence.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper provides a well-defined and systematic framework for analyzing consistency, making it a valuable contribution to the field.
    *   **Diverse Persona Categories:** The selection of persona categories is well-justified, covering a broad spectrum of traits and characteristics.
    *   **Multiple Evaluation Dimensions:** Evaluating consistency across different tasks, including survey, essay, social media, single-turn and multi-turn conversations, offers a comprehensive view of model behavior.
    *   **Identification of Spillover Effects:** The analysis of spillover effects and their link to social stereotypes is an important finding with ethical implications.
    *   **Real-World Applicability:** Adding examples for personas coming from Personahub is great to bridge the gap between the proposed experiments and the real world.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM-as-a-Judge:** The evaluation of open-ended tasks relies heavily on GPT4o as an LLM-as-a-judge. While the authors attempt to validate its reliability, it is still prone to bias and may not always align perfectly with human judgment.
    *   **Limited Control over Stereotypes:** While the paper identifies the presence of stereotypes, it doesn't fully explore methods for mitigating them or controlling their influence.
    *   **Generalizability:** The results are specific to the models and persona categories used in the study. Further research is needed to assess the generalizability of the findings to other LLMs and persona types.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with novel methodological contributions and insightful findings related to a complex and rapidly evolving field. The comprehensive framework and analysis of consistency across various dimensions significantly enhance our understanding of persona-assigned LLMs. The identification of spillover effects and the exploration of their connection to social stereotypes also add to the value of the study. While relying on GPT4o as the judge could be seen as a weakness, its effects has been taken into consideration in the study.

The limitations above should be addressed in future research to bolster the generalizability and robustness of the results.
Therefore, I assign the paper a score of:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MotionRAG-Diff: A Retrieval-Augmented Diffusion Framework for Long-Term Music-to-Dance Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02661v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyang Huang, Peng Zhang, Bang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Generating long-term, coherent, and realistic music-conditioned dance sequences remains a challenging task in human motion synthesis. Existing approaches exhibit critical limitations: motion graph methods rely on fixed template libraries, restricting creative generation; diffusion models, while capable of producing novel motions, often lack temporal coherence and musical alignment. To address these challenges, we propose $\textbf{MotionRAG-Diff}$, a hybrid framework that integrates Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with diffusion-based refinement to enable high-quality, musically coherent dance generation for arbitrary long-term music inputs. Our method introduces three core innovations: (1) A cross-modal contrastive learning architecture that aligns heterogeneous music and dance representations in a shared latent space, establishing unsupervised semantic correspondence without paired data; (2) An optimized motion graph system for efficient retrieval and seamless concatenation of motion segments, ensuring realism and temporal coherence across long sequences; (3) A multi-condition diffusion model that jointly conditions on raw music signals and contrastive features to enhance motion quality and global synchronization. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MotionRAG-Diff achieves state-of-the-art performance in motion quality, diversity, and music-motion synchronization accuracy. This work establishes a new paradigm for music-driven dance generation by synergizing retrieval-based template fidelity with diffusion-based creative enhancement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MotionRAG-Diff, a novel retrieval-augmented diffusion framework for generating long-term, coherent, and realistic music-conditioned dance sequences. The framework addresses the limitations of existing approaches by combining the strengths of motion graph methods and diffusion models. It employs contrastive learning to align music and dance representations, an optimized motion graph system for efficient retrieval and concatenation of motion segments, and a multi-condition diffusion model for enhancing motion quality and synchronization. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on the AIST++ and FineDance datasets, showcasing improvements in motion quality, diversity, and music-motion synchronization accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with a diffusion model for the task of music-to-dance generation. While individual components (contrastive learning, motion graphs, diffusion models) are not entirely new, their synergistic combination within the MotionRAG-Diff framework is innovative. Specifically, the contrastive learning approach eliminates the need for paired data, and multi-condition diffusion jointly conditions on raw music signals and contrastive features to enhance motion quality and global synchronization.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in human motion synthesis – generating long-term, coherent, and realistic dance sequences conditioned on music. The results indicate a substantial improvement over existing methods, particularly in balancing template fidelity with creative generation. The framework's ability to handle arbitrary long-term music inputs makes it highly practical. The improved BAS (Beat Alignment Score) is particularly notable, showing a clear improvement in temporal alignment between music and generated motion.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The hybrid approach effectively combines the benefits of motion graphs (temporal coherence, realism) and diffusion models (novelty, quality enhancement).
    *   The contrastive learning framework enables unsupervised semantic correspondence between music and dance, avoiding the need for paired data.
    *   The multi-condition diffusion model leverages various inputs (music, beat, motion candidates, embeddings) for enhanced generation.
    *   The paper provides comprehensive experimental results on two benchmark datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The motion diversity, while improved, remains constrained by the pre-built motion graph. The system is still somewhat limited in creating truly novel dance moves beyond what is available in the motion library.
    *   The two-stage pipeline (motion graph retrieval followed by diffusion refinement) increases computational cost, potentially hindering real-time applications.
    *   The complexity of the system (integration of three main components) may make it difficult to implement and optimize.
*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The MotionRAG-Diff framework sets a new paradigm for music-driven dance generation.
    *   The approach can be extended to other motion synthesis tasks, such as text-to-motion or speech-to-motion generation.
    *   The framework has potential applications in entertainment, virtual reality, and human-computer interaction.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

I am assigning a score of 8 to this paper. While the individual components aren't radically novel, the *integrated* architecture and the specific way it leverages contrastive learning and diffusion for motion refinement significantly advance the field. The performance gains on the benchmark datasets are substantial. However, the limitation in motion diversity (tied to the initial motion graph), and the complexity/computational cost prevent it from reaching a higher score. The improvement over a standard diffusion model is clear, but the reliance on a motion library means the "creative generation" aspect is primarily refinement rather than complete invention. Overcoming this limitation would be a significant step. The BAS score improvement is particularly notable, indicating the effectiveness of the system's ability to align dance to music, a key aspect in dance generation.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 8/10

### EvaLearn: Quantifying the Learning Capability and Efficiency of LLMs via Sequential Problem Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02672v1)
- **Authors**: Shihan Dou, Ming Zhang, Chenhao Huang, Jiayi Chen, Feng Chen, Shichun Liu, Yan Liu, Chenxiao Liu, Cheng Zhong, Zongzhang Zhang, Tao Gui, Chao Xin, Wei Chengzhi, Lin Yan, Qi Zhang, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: We introduce EvaLearn, a pioneering benchmark designed to evaluate large language models (LLMs) on their learning capability and efficiency in challenging tasks, a critical, yet underexplored aspect of model potential. EvaLearn contains 648 challenging problems across six task types, grouped into 182 sequences, each sequence dedicated to one task type. Diverging from most existing benchmarks that evaluate models in parallel, EvaLearn requires models to solve problems sequentially, allowing them to leverage the experience gained from previous solutions. EvaLearn provides five comprehensive automated metrics to evaluate models and quantify their learning capability and efficiency. We extensively benchmark nine frontier models and observe varied performance profiles: some models, such as Claude-3.7-sonnet, start with moderate initial performance but exhibit strong learning ability, while some models struggle to benefit from experience and may even show negative transfer. Moreover, we investigate model performance under two learning settings and find that instance-level rubrics and teacher-model feedback further facilitate model learning. Importantly, we observe that current LLMs with stronger static abilities do not show a clear advantage in learning capability across all tasks, highlighting that EvaLearn evaluates a new dimension of model performance. We hope EvaLearn provides a novel evaluation perspective for assessing LLM potential and understanding the gap between models and human capabilities, promoting the development of deeper and more dynamic evaluation approaches. All datasets, the automatic evaluation framework, and the results studied in this paper are available at the GitHub repository.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EvaLearn: Quantifying the Learning Capability and Efficiency of LLMs via Sequential Problem Solving":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EvaLearn, a new benchmark designed to evaluate large language models (LLMs) based on their learning capability and efficiency in sequential problem-solving. Unlike most existing benchmarks that assess models on independent, identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples, EvaLearn requires models to solve a series of related problems sequentially, allowing them to leverage experience from previous solutions. The benchmark includes 648 challenging problems across six task types, organized into 182 sequences. EvaLearn provides five automated metrics to quantify learning capability and efficiency. The authors benchmarked nine frontier LLMs, revealing varied performance profiles. They also investigate model performance with instance-level rubrics and teacher-model feedback, further facilitating learning. The paper highlights that models with strong static abilities do not always excel in learning capability, thus presenting a novel evaluation perspective for assessing LLM potential. All data and code are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the sequential problem-solving paradigm. It shifts the focus from purely static abilities to dynamic learning capabilities, which is a crucial aspect of intelligence often overlooked by traditional benchmarks. This is a significant departure from the parallel evaluation paradigm that dominates the field. The introduction of automated metrics tailored for assessing learning capability and efficiency within this sequential setting is also novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its attempt to address a gap in LLM evaluation. By emphasizing learning, the benchmark offers a more nuanced understanding of model potential. The findings, such as the diverse learning capabilities across tasks and the contrasting performance of different models, provide valuable insights for future LLM development. The availability of the benchmark will likely stimulate research on learning methods for LLMs and foster the development of more dynamic and realistic evaluation approaches. However, the reliance on closed-source LLMs such as GPT-4 for evaluation introduces certain challenges.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on a critical, underexplored aspect:** Learning capability is a key indicator of intelligence, and EvaLearn directly addresses this.
    *   **Well-defined sequential paradigm:** The setup forces models to leverage experience, mirroring real-world learning scenarios.
    *   **Comprehensive automated metrics:** Provides a quantifiable and objective assessment of learning capability and efficiency.
    *   **Extensive benchmarking:** Nine frontier models are evaluated, providing a broad performance overview.
    *   **Publicly available data and code:** Promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited task scope:** While diverse, the six task types may not encompass all aspects of LLM learning. Expanding the task categories would increase the benchmark's generalizability.
    *   **Dependency on LLM-as-a-judge:** Using another LLM for evaluation introduces potential biases and reliance on the judging model's own capabilities. While the paper demonstrates high reliability, potential issues remain.
    *   **Potential bias toward thinking-based models:** The design and metrics might implicitly favor models with specific architectures or training paradigms.
    *   **Complexity of problem creation:** Generating challenging, related problems and crafting reliable rubrics is a difficult and time-consuming task. This limits the scale and potential evolution of the benchmark.
    *   **Closed-source LLM evaluation framework dependency**: Reliance on a black-box LLM to assess the model responses introduces limitations, especially around transparency and control.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by shifting the evaluation focus towards dynamic learning capabilities. EvaLearn could serve as a standard benchmark for assessing new learning methods for LLMs and may inspire the development of more sophisticated evaluation paradigms. However, its impact will depend on its widespread adoption and the extent to which the community addresses its limitations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. The sequential problem-solving paradigm and automated learning metrics are novel and address a significant gap in current benchmarks. The findings are insightful and offer valuable guidance for future research. However, the limitations related to task scope, potential biases in metrics, dependence on LLM-as-a-judge, and the complexity of dataset creation prevent a higher score. The value of the work lies primarily in its pioneering approach to evaluating *learning*, rather than simply static task performance. If the benchmark sees widespread adoption and sparks further research into dynamic evaluation methods, it could be considered a highly significant contribution, retroactively increasing its score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TL;DR: Too Long, Do Re-weighting for Effcient LLM Reasoning Compression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02678v1)
- **Authors**: Zhong-Zhi Li, Xiao Liang, Zihao Tang, Lei Ji, Peijie Wang, Haotian Xu, Xing W, Haizhen Huang, Weiwei Deng, Ying Nian Wu, Yeyun Gong, Zhijiang Guo, Xiao Liu, Fei Yin, Cheng-Lin Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently achieved remarkable progress by leveraging Reinforcement Learning and extended Chain-of-Thought (CoT) techniques. However, the challenge of performing efficient language reasoning--especially during inference with extremely long outputs--has drawn increasing attention from the research community. In this work, we propose a dynamic ratio-based training pipeline that does not rely on sophisticated data annotations or interpolation between multiple models. We continuously balance the weights between the model's System-1 and System-2 data to eliminate redundant reasoning processes while preserving the model's reasoning capability. We validate our approach across models on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-7B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-14B and on a diverse set of benchmarks with varying difficulty levels. Our method significantly reduces the number of output tokens by nearly 40% while maintaining the accuracy of the reasoning. Our code and data will be available soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TL;DR: Too Long, Do Re-weighting for Efficient LLM Reasoning Compression":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a dynamic data re-weighting method called TLDR (Too Long, Do Re-weighting) to compress the reasoning process of Large Language Models (LLMs) during inference. TLDR aims to address the challenge of overly verbose or inefficient reasoning, especially when using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting. The method dynamically adjusts the balance between "System 1" data (short, intuitive reasoning on simpler problems) and "System 2" data (longer, more deliberative reasoning on complex problems) during training. This aims to reduce redundant reasoning steps while preserving accuracy. TLDR is validated on DeepSeek-R1-Distill models (7B and 14B parameters) using a diverse set of benchmarks. Results show a significant reduction in output token count (close to 40%) while maintaining or even slightly improving reasoning accuracy in some cases.  The method is presented as being easier to implement and less reliant on complex data engineering compared to existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamic re-weighting of data sources based on the model's current performance in terms of both efficiency (token length) and accuracy is reasonably novel within the specific context of LLM reasoning compression. While dynamic weighting schemes are not new in machine learning in general, its application to balancing short and long CoT data in LLMs represents a meaningful contribution. The paper distinguishes itself by avoiding complex data annotations or model interpolations that are common in related works like TOPS or CoT-Valve.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a relevant and increasingly important problem: making LLMs more efficient for practical deployment, especially for resource-constrained environments. Reducing token generation directly translates to lower inference costs and faster response times. A 40% reduction in tokens is a substantial improvement that could have real-world impact. The experimental results, demonstrating this compression without sacrificing accuracy, strengthens its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inefficient reasoning in LLMs, particularly those using CoT prompting.
    *   **Simple and Intuitive Approach:** TLDR's dynamic re-weighting approach is relatively simple to understand and implement, making it accessible to other researchers. It also has a strong intuitive basis of re-weighting depending on thinking lengths.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental evaluation demonstrates tangible improvements in token compression while maintaining accuracy across diverse datasets and model sizes.
    *   **Comparison to Baselines:** The paper provides a comprehensive comparison of TLDR against several existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scope:** The experiments primarily focus on DeepSeek-R1-Distill models.  It would be beneficial to see how well TLDR generalizes to other LLM architectures.
    *   **Ablation Studies Could Be Stronger:** While the paper includes ablation studies, a deeper dive into the effect of varying the re-weighting parameters (η, c) or the frequency of re-weighting would further strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** While diverse, the benchmarks mainly focus on mathematical reasoning. Evaluating TLDR on other reasoning tasks like common sense reasoning or logical inference would broaden its applicability.
    *   **Overclaiming:** The method does seem to avoid more complex training or intervention, but it relies on having a *base LLM with system 2 capabilities*, and creating a shorter solution. Creating these shorter solutions might be a bottleneck for some applications or domains and would need to be discussed more.

*   **Potential Impact:** TLDR has the potential to influence the development of more efficient LLM inference techniques. Its simplicity and effectiveness could lead to its adoption in real-world applications where reducing computational costs is critical. The open availability of code and data will facilitate further research and adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and valuable approach to LLM reasoning compression, backed by solid empirical results. It addresses a significant problem in the field, and its relative simplicity makes it accessible to other researchers. While the evaluation has some limitations in terms of model and task scope, the core contribution is well-defined and demonstrably effective. Considering all of this, a score of 7 is appropriate. While the method isn't groundbreaking in the sense of revolutionizing the field, it represents a significant and practical incremental improvement that is likely to be influential.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Solving Inverse Problems with FLAIR
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02680v1)
- **Authors**: Julius Erbach, Dominik Narnhofer, Andreas Dombos, Bernt Schiele, Jan Eric Lenssen, Konrad Schindler
- **Abstract**: Flow-based latent generative models such as Stable Diffusion 3 are able to generate images with remarkable quality, even enabling photorealistic text-to-image generation. Their impressive performance suggests that these models should also constitute powerful priors for inverse imaging problems, but that approach has not yet led to comparable fidelity. There are several key obstacles: (i) the encoding into a lower-dimensional latent space makes the underlying (forward) mapping non-linear; (ii) the data likelihood term is usually intractable; and (iii) learned generative models struggle to recover rare, atypical data modes during inference. We present FLAIR, a novel training free variational framework that leverages flow-based generative models as a prior for inverse problems. To that end, we introduce a variational objective for flow matching that is agnostic to the type of degradation, and combine it with deterministic trajectory adjustments to recover atypical modes. To enforce exact consistency with the observed data, we decouple the optimization of the data fidelity and regularization terms. Moreover, we introduce a time-dependent calibration scheme in which the strength of the regularization is modulated according to off-line accuracy estimates. Results on standard imaging benchmarks demonstrate that FLAIR consistently outperforms existing diffusion- and flow-based methods in terms of reconstruction quality and sample diversity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Solving Inverse Problems with FLAIR":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FLAIR, a novel training-free variational framework for solving inverse imaging problems using flow-based latent generative models like Stable Diffusion. FLAIR addresses key challenges in this area, including the non-linear forward mapping introduced by latent spaces, the intractability of the data likelihood term, and the tendency of generative models to favor typical data modes over rare or atypical ones.  FLAIR achieves this by: (1) introducing a variational objective for flow matching agnostic to the degradation type; (2) incorporating deterministic trajectory adjustments to handle atypical data; (3) decoupling data fidelity and regularization terms for precise data consistency; and (4) implementing a time-dependent calibration scheme to modulate regularization strength based on accuracy estimates. Experimental results on standard imaging benchmarks demonstrate that FLAIR outperforms existing diffusion- and flow-based methods in reconstruction quality and sample diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper exhibits a significant degree of novelty. Combining flow-based models with variational inference for inverse problems isn't entirely new, but the specific combination of techniques in FLAIR is unique and addresses several important shortcomings of prior work.  The key novel elements are:

*   **Degradation-Agnostic Flow Matching Objective:** Formulating a flow matching loss that doesn't depend on the specific degradation model is a notable contribution. This makes the framework more flexible and applicable to a wider range of inverse problems.

*   **Deterministic Trajectory Adjustments:** The method for handling rare or atypical data modes by deterministically adjusting the diffusion trajectory is innovative and tackles a crucial limitation of standard generative priors.

*   **Decoupled Optimization and Hard Data Consistency:** Explicitly decoupling the data fidelity and regularization terms to enable hard data consistency is an important design choice that improves reconstruction accuracy.

*   **Time-Dependent Calibration:** Adaptively adjusting the regularization strength based on offline accuracy estimates represents a well-motivated and potentially powerful method for robust inference.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a relevant and significant problem: integrating powerful generative priors into inverse imaging to improve reconstruction quality and sample diversity.  The results demonstrate that FLAIR consistently outperforms existing methods in various imaging tasks (super-resolution, inpainting, deblurring).  The performance gains are not marginal, suggesting that the proposed techniques are effective and offer a genuine advancement. The fact that FLAIR is training-free adds to its practical value, as it eliminates the need for task-specific fine-tuning. The paper is also well-written and clearly explains the proposed methods.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive approach:** FLAIR integrates multiple novel components to tackle the challenges of using flow-based models for inverse problems.
*   **Strong empirical results:** The paper demonstrates consistent improvements across several tasks and datasets compared to existing state-of-the-art methods.
*   **Training-free:** The training-free nature makes the method more practical.
*   **Clear presentation:** The paper provides a clear explanation of the proposed methods and their benefits.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Hyperparameter sensitivity:**  While the training free nature is an advantage, the addition of hyperparameters for deterministic trajectory adjustment creates a need for careful tuning.
*   **Dependency on SD3 limitations:**  The framework inherits the limitations of the underlying Stable Diffusion 3 model, including potential biases, resolution constraints, and difficulty with out-of-distribution data.
*   **Computational cost:** Running inference with flow-based generative models can be computationally demanding, and the added complexity of FLAIR might further increase the cost, although this isn't explicitly discussed.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in inverse imaging and generative modeling.  The proposed techniques for addressing the limitations of generative priors in inverse problems could be adopted or extended by other researchers.  The degradation-agnostic flow matching objective and deterministic trajectory adjustments are particularly promising ideas that warrant further exploration.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution by addressing key challenges in using flow-based models for inverse problems. The integration of several novel techniques, particularly the degradation-agnostic flow matching objective and deterministic trajectory adjustments, is significant and leads to compelling empirical results. The training-free nature is also a major advantage. However, the method depends on SD3 and adds hyperparameters that need tuning and may not extend well to completely new data scenarios. Given these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 8 is appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Decompose, Plan in Parallel, and Merge: A Novel Paradigm for Large Language Models based Planning with Multiple Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02683v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengdong Lu, Weikai Lu, Yiling Tao, Yun Dai, ZiXuan Chen, Huiping Zhuang, Cen Chen, Hao Peng, Ziqian Zeng
- **Abstract**: Despite significant advances in Large Language Models (LLMs), planning tasks still present challenges for LLM-based agents. Existing planning methods face two key limitations: heavy constraints and cascading errors. To address these limitations, we propose a novel parallel planning paradigm, which Decomposes, Plans for subtasks in Parallel, and Merges subplans into a final plan (DPPM). Specifically, DPPM decomposes the complex task based on constraints into subtasks, generates the subplan for each subtask in parallel, and merges them into a global plan. In addition, our approach incorporates a verification and refinement module, enabling error correction and conflict resolution. Experimental results demonstrate that DPPM significantly outperforms existing methods in travel planning tasks.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces Decompose, Plan in Parallel, and Merge (DPPM), a novel paradigm for large language model (LLM)-based planning tasks, particularly those involving multiple constraints. DPPM addresses limitations of existing planning methods, namely heavy constraints and cascading errors, by decomposing complex tasks into subtasks based on constraints, generating subplans for each subtask in parallel using individual LLM agents, and then merging these subplans into a coherent global plan. A verification and refinement module is also incorporated for error correction and conflict resolution. The approach is evaluated on travel planning tasks using the TravelPlanner and a modified version of the ChinaTravel dataset, demonstrating significant performance improvements over existing methods like Direct, CoT, and LLM-Modulo.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of DPPM – parallel planning after constraint-aware decomposition followed by a merging stage – represents a reasonable advancement over existing sequential or interleaved approaches. Parallel planning tackles the cascading error problem present in sequential methods. The constraint-aware decomposition is also sensible, addressing the 'heavy constraints' issue. The inclusion of a verification/refinement module is a standard technique, but its integration within the DPPM framework is well-motivated. The novelty isn't revolutionary; rather, it is an incremental improvement that combines several established ideas in a specific, well-defined manner. The authors clearly articulate the shortcomings of existing methodologies and convincingly show how the DPPM tackles these issues.

**Significance:** The experimental results consistently demonstrate superior performance compared to existing baselines across different datasets and LLM backbones. The ablation studies effectively isolate the contributions of the individual components (constraint instruction, verification/refinement, Cartesian product combination), providing strong evidence for the efficacy of each element. Specifically, DPPM's improved handling of multiple constraints is an important contribution, given that many real-world planning problems are inherently multi-constrained. The application of DPPM to travel planning is a good choice, as it provides a tangible and relatable scenario for evaluating the effectiveness of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Nature:** As mentioned above, the novelty lies in the specific combination and orchestration of existing techniques. While effective, the individual components are not groundbreaking.
*   **Limited Generalizability Demonstrated:** The paper primarily focuses on travel planning. While travel planning is a good testbed, the generalizability of DPPM to other planning domains (e.g., robotics, game playing) is not explicitly demonstrated. Although the principles behind DPPM *should* transfer to other areas, showcasing this would significantly strengthen the paper's contribution. There are subtle challenges with implementing DPPM for different tasks with varying constraints.
*   **Dependence on Good Decomposition:** The effectiveness of DPPM hinges on the quality of the constraint-aware task decomposition. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion on how this decomposition is achieved and how its performance is affected by different decomposition strategies. The prompts for the decomposition stage and some related discussions in appendices could be highlighted more in the main text.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a technically sound and well-evaluated approach to multi-constraint planning with LLMs. The core idea of DPPM is a logical and effective solution to the limitations of existing methods. The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the limited generalizability prevent it from receiving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Shaking to Reveal: Perturbation-Based Detection of LLM Hallucinations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02696v1)
- **Authors**: Jinyuan Luo, Zhen Fang, Yixuan Li, Seongheon Park, Ling Chen
- **Abstract**: Hallucination remains a key obstacle to the reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs) in real-world question answering tasks. A widely adopted strategy to detect hallucination, known as self-assessment, relies on the model's own output confidence to estimate the factual accuracy of its answers. However, this strategy assumes that the model's output distribution closely reflects the true data distribution, which may not always hold in practice. As bias accumulates through the model's layers, the final output can diverge from the underlying reasoning process, making output-level confidence an unreliable signal for hallucination detection. In this work, we propose Sample-Specific Prompting (SSP), a new framework that improves self-assessment by analyzing perturbation sensitivity at intermediate representations. These representations, being less influenced by model bias, offer a more faithful view of the model's latent reasoning process. Specifically, SSP dynamically generates noise prompts for each input and employs a lightweight encoder to amplify the changes in representations caused by the perturbation. A contrastive distance metric is then used to quantify these differences and separate truthful from hallucinated responses. By leveraging the dynamic behavior of intermediate representations under perturbation, SSP enables more reliable self-assessment. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SSP significantly outperforms prior methods across a range of hallucination detection benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new framework called Sample-Specific Prompting (SSP) for detecting hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs).  SSP addresses the limitations of existing self-assessment methods, which often rely on output confidence scores that can be unreliable due to accumulated biases in the model. Instead, SSP leverages the sensitivity of intermediate representations to input perturbations. It dynamically generates noise prompts tailored to each question-answer pair and uses a lightweight encoder to amplify changes in the intermediate representations caused by these perturbations. A contrastive distance metric then quantifies these differences to distinguish between truthful and hallucinated responses. The paper demonstrates SSP's effectiveness across various datasets and LLMs, showing improved hallucination detection accuracy compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its perturbation-based approach applied to the *intermediate representations* of LLMs for hallucination detection. While perturbation techniques have been used in other contexts (adversarial attacks, robustness), their application to *specifically analyzing intermediate layer sensitivity as a signal of hallucination* is a novel contribution. The dynamic generation of *sample-specific* noise prompts is also a key component that differentiates SSP from methods employing static or generic prompts.  The combination of perturbation, intermediate layer analysis, and contrastive learning yields a unique approach.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from the pressing need for reliable hallucination detection methods in LLMs. The demonstrated improvements in accuracy compared to existing methods are meaningful, especially on challenging datasets like TruthfulQA. The focus on intermediate representations helps mitigate biases that affect output-level confidence, leading to more robust detection.  The relative efficiency of SSP (requiring only a representation shift calculation) also makes it practically relevant for real-world deployment.  The insights regarding the role of intermediate layers in capturing contextual semantics are valuable for understanding LLM behavior. The inclusion of experiments relating to few shot learning and generalizability make the results more significant and useful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of using perturbation sensitivity at intermediate layers is innovative and well-motivated.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents thorough experiments across diverse datasets and LLMs, showcasing the effectiveness of SSP.
*   **Ablation Studies:**  Comprehensive ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of SSP (noise prompt generation, encoder, contrastive learning).
*   **Efficiency:** The method maintains good detection performance while using a small number of calculations, highlighting its efficiency.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:**  SSP introduces additional components (noise prompt generator, encoder, contrastive loss) which add complexity. It would be helpful to more directly compare the added computational costs compared to existing methods, but the data on inference speed is helpful in this regard.
*   **Dataset Dependence:** While the method shows strong generalization, it is still difficult to demonstrate its generalizability to all data and tasks, so additional evaluation here would have been useful.
*   **Limited Localizaiton:** The paper admits that the method is not able to determine precisely *which* tokens are incorrect.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**. The paper makes a novel and significant contribution to the field of hallucination detection in LLMs. The idea of leveraging perturbation sensitivity in intermediate layers is both innovative and effective.  The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating improved accuracy and robustness compared to existing methods. The ablation studies are thorough and provide valuable insights into the method's components. While the method adds complexity and is not a complete solution to all hallucination challenges, it advances the field by providing a valuable tool to aid LLM safety.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Smoothed Preference Optimization via ReNoise Inversion for Aligning Diffusion Models with Varied Human Preferences
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02698v1)
- **Authors**: Yunhong Lu, Qichao Wang, Hengyuan Cao, Xiaoyin Xu, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) aligns text-to-image (T2I) generation models with human preferences using pairwise preference data. Although substantial resources are expended in collecting and labeling datasets, a critical aspect is often neglected: \textit{preferences vary across individuals and should be represented with more granularity.} To address this, we propose SmPO-Diffusion, a novel method for modeling preference distributions to improve the DPO objective, along with a numerical upper bound estimation for the diffusion optimization objective. First, we introduce a smoothed preference distribution to replace the original binary distribution. We employ a reward model to simulate human preferences and apply preference likelihood averaging to improve the DPO loss, such that the loss function approaches zero when preferences are similar. Furthermore, we utilize an inversion technique to simulate the trajectory preference distribution of the diffusion model, enabling more accurate alignment with the optimization objective. Our approach effectively mitigates issues of excessive optimization and objective misalignment present in existing methods through straightforward modifications. Our SmPO-Diffusion achieves state-of-the-art performance in preference evaluation, outperforming baselines across metrics with lower training costs. The project page is https://jaydenlyh.github.io/SmPO-project-page/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SmPO-Diffusion, a novel method for aligning text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models with human preferences. It addresses the problem that preferences vary across individuals, a factor often neglected by existing Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) methods. SmPO-Diffusion improves upon the DPO objective by modeling preference distributions using a smoothed preference distribution instead of a binary one.  It also uses Renoise Inversion to more accurately estimate the trajectory preference distribution of the diffusion model, leading to more effective alignment and mitigating excessive optimization. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance compared to baselines on preference evaluation metrics, image quality, and training efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in combining smoothed preference modeling with Renoise Inversion for diffusion model alignment.  Smoothing the preference distribution is a useful technique to address the variability of human opinions and avoid overfitting to the limited labelled data. The use of renoise inversion is also a good alternative of a forward process to estimate trajectory in diffusion models.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a key limitation of existing DPO methods – the assumption of a uniform preference distribution. By modelling preferences with greater nuance, the method can potentially lead to more personalized and desirable image generation. The improved training efficiency compared to alternatives such as Diffusion-KTO is also a practical benefit.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a practically relevant problem: the heterogeneity of human preferences.
    *   Clear and well-motivated methodology.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in image generation quality and training efficiency across various metrics and using both SD1.5 and SDXL models.
    *   Ablation studies that provide insight into the contribution of each component.
    *   User studies that provide quantitative analysis of the methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on a pre-trained reward model (PickScore) introduces a dependency. The performance of SmPO-Diffusion is inherently linked to the accuracy and potential biases present in the reward model. The paper acknowledges this indirectly by discussing PickScore as a form of cleaned data, but a more thorough analysis of the reward model's impact would be beneficial.
    *   While the paper mentions societal biases and the potential for the model to produce undesirable outputs, it can be elaborated with more details and analysis.
    *   The experimental setup, while thorough, could benefit from a direct comparison against more recent preference alignment methods beyond the baselines.
    *   The paper acknowledges that the reward model may contain biases, and more analysis on this should be included.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by pushing research towards more nuanced modeling of human preferences in generative models. The increased training efficiency could make preference alignment more accessible. The renoise inversion could be a good alternative in solving estimation process during fine-tuning of diffusion model.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of preference alignment in T2I models. The combination of smoothed preference modeling and Renoise Inversion offers a practical improvement over existing methods. The empirical results clearly demonstrate state-of-the-art performance and improved efficiency. However, the reliance on the PickScore reward model and limitations of the work slightly diminish the overall impact. The paper also misses to cover recent preference alignment methods. Hence, the final score reflects the paper's significant contributions tempered by the identified limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Open-Set Living Need Prediction with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02713v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaochong Lan, Jie Feng, Yizhou Sun, Chen Gao, Jiahuan Lei, Xinlei Shi, Hengliang Luo, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Living needs are the needs people generate in their daily lives for survival and well-being. On life service platforms like Meituan, user purchases are driven by living needs, making accurate living need predictions crucial for personalized service recommendations. Traditional approaches treat this prediction as a closed-set classification problem, severely limiting their ability to capture the diversity and complexity of living needs. In this work, we redefine living need prediction as an open-set classification problem and propose PIGEON, a novel system leveraging large language models (LLMs) for unrestricted need prediction. PIGEON first employs a behavior-aware record retriever to help LLMs understand user preferences, then incorporates Maslow's hierarchy of needs to align predictions with human living needs. For evaluation and application, we design a recall module based on a fine-tuned text embedding model that links flexible need descriptions to appropriate life services. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that PIGEON significantly outperforms closed-set approaches on need-based life service recall by an average of 19.37%. Human evaluation validates the reasonableness and specificity of our predictions. Additionally, we employ instruction tuning to enable smaller LLMs to achieve competitive performance, supporting practical deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**
