### It's the Thought that Counts: Evaluating the Attempts of Frontier LLMs to Persuade on Harmful Topics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02873v1)
- **Authors**: Matthew Kowal, Jasper Timm, Jean-Francois Godbout, Thomas Costello, Antonio A. Arechar, Gordon Pennycook, David Rand, Adam Gleave, Kellin Pelrine
- **Abstract**: Persuasion is a powerful capability of large language models (LLMs) that both enables beneficial applications (e.g. helping people quit smoking) and raises significant risks (e.g. large-scale, targeted political manipulation). Prior work has found models possess a significant and growing persuasive capability, measured by belief changes in simulated or real users. However, these benchmarks overlook a crucial risk factor: the propensity of a model to attempt to persuade in harmful contexts. Understanding whether a model will blindly ``follow orders'' to persuade on harmful topics (e.g. glorifying joining a terrorist group) is key to understanding the efficacy of safety guardrails. Moreover, understanding if and when a model will engage in persuasive behavior in pursuit of some goal is essential to understanding the risks from agentic AI systems. We propose the Attempt to Persuade Eval (APE) benchmark, that shifts the focus from persuasion success to persuasion attempts, operationalized as a model's willingness to generate content aimed at shaping beliefs or behavior. Our evaluation framework probes frontier LLMs using a multi-turn conversational setup between simulated persuader and persuadee agents. APE explores a diverse spectrum of topics including conspiracies, controversial issues, and non-controversially harmful content. We introduce an automated evaluator model to identify willingness to persuade and measure the frequency and context of persuasive attempts. We find that many open and closed-weight models are frequently willing to attempt persuasion on harmful topics and that jailbreaking can increase willingness to engage in such behavior. Our results highlight gaps in current safety guardrails and underscore the importance of evaluating willingness to persuade as a key dimension of LLM risk. APE is available at github.com/AlignmentResearch/AttemptPersuadeEval
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "It's the Thought that Counts: Evaluating the Attempts of Frontier LLMs to Persuade on Harmful Topics."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical risk of Large Language Models (LLMs) being used to persuade individuals on harmful topics.  The authors introduce the "Attempt to Persuade Eval" (APE) benchmark, which shifts the focus from measuring persuasion *success* to measuring persuasion *attempts*. APE uses a multi-turn conversational setup with simulated persuader and persuadee agents to probe LLMs across a diverse range of topics, including conspiracies, controversial issues, and explicitly harmful content (e.g., joining a terrorist group). An automated evaluator is used to identify the LLM's willingness to persuade. The study reveals that many models, both open- and closed-weight, are frequently willing to attempt persuasion on harmful topics, and that jailbreaking techniques can increase this willingness. The authors conclude that current safety guardrails have gaps and highlight the importance of evaluating the willingness to persuade as a key dimension of LLM risk. The APE benchmark is made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on *attempted* persuasion rather than persuasion *success*. Existing benchmarks often measure changes in belief, which doesn't fully capture the risk of a model's *propensity* to persuade on dangerous topics, even if ultimately unsuccessful.  This shift is important because a single successful persuasion on a harmful topic can have serious consequences, regardless of the overall success rate. The introduction of the APE benchmark to specifically evaluate this dimension of LLM behavior is a valuable contribution. The idea of automatically evaluating multi-turn conversations for persuasive intent is also a valuable contribution, moving beyond simple classification tasks.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a pressing and significant concern: the potential misuse of LLMs for malicious persuasion. As LLMs become more powerful and widely deployed, understanding and mitigating this risk is crucial for responsible AI development. The paper provides concrete evidence that even state-of-the-art models are vulnerable and that jailbreaking techniques can further exacerbate the problem. This is significant because it highlights the limitations of current safety mechanisms and the need for more robust defenses. The breakdown of performance by model and topic category provides valuable insights for developers looking to improve the safety of their systems. By openly releasing the benchmark, the authors enable further research and evaluation in this critical area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of malicious persuasion and articulates the limitations of existing evaluation methods.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation is relatively comprehensive, spanning diverse topics and a range of LLMs.
    *   **Automated Evaluation:**  The automated evaluator allows for efficient and scalable assessment of persuasion attempts. Human evaluation confirms a relatively strong inter-rater reliability with the models’ judgments
    *   **Demonstration of Vulnerability:**  The paper effectively demonstrates the vulnerability of LLMs to persuasion attempts on harmful topics, even with existing safety measures.
    *   **Publicly Available Benchmark:**  The release of the APE benchmark promotes further research and development of safety mechanisms.
    *   **Impactful Findings:**  The finding that jailbreaking significantly increases the willingness to persuade is alarming and underscores the need for more robust defenses.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulated Interactions:**  The use of simulated persuader and persuadee agents is a limitation. While scalable, these simulations might not fully capture the complexity of real-world human interactions and susceptibility to persuasion. There is a need for future tests that show humans being persuaded on harmful topics.
    *   **Evaluator Model Bias:** The automated evaluator model may have its own biases, influencing the assessment of persuasion attempts. The authors acknowledge the limitations of persuasion as defined by the system.

    *   **Limited Generalizability of Topics:**  While the topic set is diverse, it may not be fully representative of all potential harmful topics or cultural contexts.
    *   **Refusal to Engage:** The study does not fully explore the reasons why some models refuse to engage with certain topics. Further investigation into the decision-making process behind these refusals could provide valuable insights.
    *   **Lack of Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper highlights the problem, it doesn't delve deeply into potential mitigation strategies.  Exploring possible defenses would further enhance the paper's practical value.

*   **Potential Influence on the Field:** The paper is likely to influence the field by:

    *   Shifting the focus of LLM safety evaluations toward propensity to persuade.
    *   Providing a valuable benchmark for assessing and comparing the safety of different LLMs.
    *   Motivating the development of more robust safety mechanisms to prevent malicious persuasion.
    *   Raising awareness of the vulnerabilities of LLMs to jailbreaking attacks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8** to this paper. The paper makes a novel and significant contribution by focusing on the *attempt* to persuade on harmful topics, rather than simply measuring persuasion success. This shift in perspective is crucial for responsible AI development. The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of several LLMs using the APE benchmark, demonstrating their vulnerability to harmful persuasion attempts and the negative impact of jailbreaking. While the simulated interactions and potential biases of the evaluator model are limitations, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses. The open release of the APE benchmark will facilitate further research and development of more robust safety mechanisms, making it a highly influential contribution to the field. The finding that even small alterations to the systems are effective at jailbreaking these language models further illustrates how language models should be carefully assessed by developers before releasing systems into production.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### CoT is Not True Reasoning, It Is Just a Tight Constraint to Imitate: A Theory Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02878v1)
- **Authors**: Jintian Shao, Yiming Cheng
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has demonstrably enhanced the performance of Large Language Models on tasks requiring multi-step inference. This success has led to widespread claims of emergent reasoning capabilities in these models. In this paper, we present a theoretical counter-perspective: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) does not elicit genuine, abstract reasoning. Instead, we argue that Chain-of-Thought functions as a powerful structural constraint that guides Large Language Models to imitate the form of reasoning. By forcing the generation of intermediate steps, Chain-of-Thought leverages the model immense capacity for sequence prediction and pattern matching, effectively constraining its output to sequences that resemble coherent thought processes. Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has demonstrably enhanced the performance of Large Language Models on tasks requiring multi-step inference. This success has led to widespread claims of emergent reasoning capabilities in these models. In this paper, we present a theoretical counter-perspective: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) does not elicit genuine, abstract reasoning. Instead, we argue that Chain-of-Thought functions as a powerful structural constraint that guides Large Language Models to imitate the form of reasoning. By forcing the generation of intermediate steps, Chain-of-Thought leverages the model immense capacity for sequence prediction and pattern matching, effectively constraining its output to sequences that resemble coherent thought processes.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the widespread interpretation that Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting enables genuine reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that CoT is primarily a mechanism for *constrained imitation* rather than abstract reasoning.  They propose that CoT acts as a structural constraint that guides LLMs to reproduce patterns observed during training, specifically sequences resembling coherent thought processes. The paper contrasts "true reasoning" (characterized by systematicity, abstract manipulation, causal understanding, logical consistency, and robust generalization) with imitation, where LLMs reproduce patterns without deeper understanding.  The paper discusses theoretical implications, including limitations on generalization, robustness, and potential for reasoning fallacies, and suggests future research directions focused on better evaluation metrics and distinguishing imitation from abstraction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea that CoT is more about pattern matching than reasoning isn't entirely new, but the *explicit framing as constrained imitation learning* is a valuable contribution. Prior work has hinted at this, but the authors synthesize these ideas and present a clear, theoretically grounded perspective. The paper does a good job of connecting this perspective to ideas from control theory (system identification, behavioral cloning). The novelty lies in the explicit connection and well-articulated framework rather than a completely original discovery.

* **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its potential to reshape the understanding of LLMs and their capabilities. If CoT primarily relies on imitation, it has profound implications for how we design and evaluate LLMs.  It cautions against over-interpreting performance gains on benchmark tasks and highlights the importance of developing evaluation methodologies that probe deeper understanding rather than surface-level fluency. It suggests a move away from simply measuring accuracy and toward assessments that test the ability to generalize to structurally novel problems, detect logical inconsistencies, and demonstrate causal understanding. The proposed future research directions are important and potentially transformative.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and well-articulated argument:** The paper presents its hypothesis in a clear, concise, and accessible manner.
    * **Strong theoretical grounding:**  The authors draw connections to relevant concepts from imitation learning and other areas, strengthening their argument.
    * **Thought-provoking discussion of implications:**  The paper effectively explores the theoretical and practical implications of the constrained imitation view.
    * **Addresses an important debate:** The paper directly engages with a key debate in the field of LLMs and provides a valuable counter-perspective.
    * **Good writing quality:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, even for readers without extensive background in LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper is primarily theoretical.  While it discusses potential limitations and research directions, it doesn't present new empirical evidence to directly support its claims.  This makes the argument persuasive but not definitively proven. It would benefit from at least some thought experiments or examples that highlight the differences in behaviour between a 'true' reasoning system and an LLM under the CoT framework.
    * **Somewhat abstract definition of "true reasoning":** The criteria for "true reasoning" while common sense might be challenging to operationalize and measure. While it outlines key aspects such as Systematicity, Abstract Manipulation, Causal Understanding, Logical Consistency, and Robust Generalization, it requires more details on how to test for these traits.
    * **Potential for alternative explanations:** While imitation is presented as the primary mechanism, other factors might also contribute to CoT's success.  It's possible that CoT prompts help LLMs access latent reasoning abilities or improve their search for optimal solutions in complex spaces.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    * **Shifting the focus of evaluation:** Encouraging researchers to develop more nuanced evaluation metrics that go beyond accuracy.
    * **Inspiring new architectures and training paradigms:** Motivating research into LLMs that are capable of more robust and reliable reasoning.
    * **Promoting a more critical perspective on LLM capabilities:**  Cautioning against overly optimistic claims about LLM intelligence.

* **Score:** 7

* **Justification:** The paper presents a valuable and well-reasoned counter-perspective on the effectiveness of CoT prompting.  It synthesizes existing ideas and articulates a clear, theoretically grounded argument that CoT is primarily a mechanism for constrained imitation rather than abstract reasoning. While the paper lacks new empirical validation, its theoretical contributions and proposed research directions have the potential to significantly influence the field by shaping future research, inspiring more critical evaluation methodologies, and promoting the development of more robust reasoning capabilities in LLMs. Its lack of empirical evidence and the somewhat abstract definition of "true reasoning" are its primary weaknesses.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scaling Fine-Grained MoE Beyond 50B Parameters: Empirical Evaluation and Practical Insights
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02890v1)
- **Authors**: Jakub Krajewski, Marcin Chochowski, Daniel Korzekwa
- **Abstract**: Mixture of Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as pivotal for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) efficiently. Fine-grained MoE approaches - utilizing more numerous, smaller experts - have demonstrated potential in improving model convergence and quality. This work proposes a set of training recipes and provides a comprehensive empirical evaluation of fine-grained MoE, directly comparing its scaling properties against standard MoE configurations for models with up to 56B total (17B active) parameters. We investigate convergence speed, model performance on downstream benchmarks, and practical training considerations across various setups. Overall, at the largest scale we show that fine-grained MoE achieves better validation loss and higher accuracy across a set of downstream benchmarks. This study offers empirical grounding and practical insights for leveraging fine-grained MoE in the development of future large-scale models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a comprehensive empirical evaluation of fine-grained Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, scaling up to 56B total parameters (17B active).  It compares fine-grained MoE architectures against standard MoE variants (Top-1 and Top-2 routing) across various training durations and model sizes. The study investigates convergence speed, downstream benchmark performance, and practical training considerations. The key findings are that fine-grained MoE achieves better validation loss and higher accuracy on downstream tasks at the largest scale, extended training enhances the benefits of fine-grained MoE, and the order of softmax and Top-k in the router significantly impacts training. The paper provides training recipes and insights for leveraging fine-grained MoE in large-scale models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is a large-scale empirical evaluation of fine-grained MoE architectures. While the concept of fine-grained MoE isn't entirely new (Dai et al., Krajewski et al. cited), the systematic comparison against standard MoE at this scale, including downstream evaluations, adds significant value. However, the architectural elements themselves are evolutionary rather than revolutionary. It builds on existing MoE concepts, specifically the benefits of a larger number of smaller experts.

*   **Significance:**  MoE architectures are becoming increasingly important for scaling LLMs efficiently. This paper directly addresses a critical area: understanding how to best utilize MoE to optimize performance and resource usage. The exploration of the softmax/Top-k ordering in routers is a specific, practically relevant finding. The downstream task evaluations are a welcome addition, moving beyond just perplexity as a metric. The insights into how extended training benefits fine-grained MoE are also valuable for practitioners. However, the paper does not address issues related to inference costs which can be significant for this type of MoE design.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a detailed and well-controlled experimental setup, allowing for fair comparisons between different MoE variants. The consistent use of the same datasets and evaluation protocols strengthens the findings.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers actionable recommendations for training fine-grained MoE models, such as the importance of the softmax/Top-k ordering and considerations for training duration.
    *   **Downstream Task Evaluations:** The inclusion of downstream task performance provides a more comprehensive view of model quality compared to just relying on perplexity.
    *   **Addressing Load Imbalance:** Showing balanced assignment of tokens between EP groups addresses a potential concern of this architecture.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The paper largely confirms and extends existing knowledge about MoE. The core concept is not entirely new. The novelty arises primarily from the scaling and empirical investigation.
    *   **Limited Architectural Exploration:** The paper focuses mainly on existing MoE architectures, with no exploration of potentially novel MoE architectures themselves. There is little discussion of potential scaling limitations of the architecture, or the relationship between the granularity and the router's complexity.
    *   **Hardware/Implementation Details:** The paper relies on Megatron-LM for its experiments, claiming >46% MFU. The specific implementation details of their custom MoE router are not discussed, and might make it difficult to reproduce their results. A key claim is that they are able to do efficient training of this architecture, but the reasons behind that are not investigated.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to be influential for practitioners working on scaling LLMs with MoE architectures. The empirical results and practical insights will inform design choices and training strategies. However, the evolutionary nature of the work might limit its theoretical impact. The lack of a theoretical grounding of the observed benefits makes it difficult to extrapolate to new scenarios.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Diffusion Buffer: Online Diffusion-based Speech Enhancement with Sub-Second Latency
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02908v1)
- **Authors**: Bunlong Lay, Rostilav Makarov, Timo Gerkmann
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models are a class of generative models that have been recently used for speech enhancement with remarkable success but are computationally expensive at inference time. Therefore, these models are impractical for processing streaming data in real-time. In this work, we adapt a sliding window diffusion framework to the speech enhancement task. Our approach progressively corrupts speech signals through time, assigning more noise to frames close to the present in a buffer. This approach outputs denoised frames with a delay proportional to the chosen buffer size, enabling a trade-off between performance and latency. Empirical results demonstrate that our method outperforms standard diffusion models and runs efficiently on a GPU, achieving an input-output latency in the order of 0.3 to 1 seconds. This marks the first practical diffusion-based solution for online speech enhancement.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a method called "Diffusion Buffer" (DB) for online speech enhancement (SE) using diffusion models, addressing the high computational cost that makes standard diffusion models impractical for real-time streaming applications.  The core idea is to process a sliding window (the "Diffusion Buffer") of recent noisy speech frames. Frames closer to the present time within the buffer are progressively corrupted with more noise according to a diffusion process, while older frames are denoised. The oldest frame in the buffer is output as the enhanced speech, introducing a delay proportional to the buffer size.  This approach allows for a trade-off between enhancement performance and latency.  The authors demonstrate that the method outperforms standard diffusion models, runs efficiently on a GPU, and achieves sub-second input-output latency, making it a practical solution for online SE. They highlight that the score model is only called once per input frame, significantly reducing the computational burden.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

This paper addresses a *critical* gap in the application of diffusion models to real-time speech enhancement. Diffusion models have shown impressive results in offline SE but have been computationally prohibitive for online scenarios. The key novelty lies in adapting the diffusion process to a sliding window framework, aligning the diffusion time-steps with the time axis of the input stream.  Several aspects need a more critical evaluation:

*   **Novelty of the Diffusion Buffer:** The sliding window approach itself is not entirely new in signal processing, but the *specific combination* with a progressive diffusion process within the window is novel. The authors *clearly demonstrate* that a naïve application of existing diffusion models for SE directly does not work. The innovative arrangement is the distribution of noise across the buffer, where the amount of noise decreases farther from the input. This design is essential for the efficient application of diffusion models in online SE.

*   **Significance of Reduced Computational Cost:** This is the most *significant* aspect of the paper. The reduction in computational complexity by only calling the score model once per frame allows the model to operate online. The authors effectively demonstrate this by reporting RTF values. However, the authors need to discuss the effect of model parameter size on performance. Is it possible to further reduce the parameter size?

*   **Performance Trade-off:** The paper demonstrates a trade-off between latency and performance (PESQ, WVMOS). The authors are *mostly convincing* here, but it would have been beneficial to compare directly against state-of-the-art online SE methods, even if they are not diffusion-based, to provide a clearer understanding of the overall performance gains. The inclusion of DEMUCS is a good start, but a more in-depth comparison with other online-suitable methods would have increased the impact.

*   **Dataset and Generalization:** The experiments are performed on the filtered EARS-WHAM dataset. While EARS-WHAM is an excellent dataset, the filtering (removing "whisper", "highpitch", "lowpitch") could limit the generality of the findings. A more diverse test set including unseen noise types and acoustic conditions would further strengthen the claims of the paper.

*   **Loss Function**: Although the choice to include additional leading zeros into the input data for training is an interesting approach, it is worth exploring whether the model can be trained such that initialization does not require this modification.

*   **Missing Baseline Evaluation Metrics**: The study would be more rigorous if it assessed model latency by evaluating its runtime across CPU, single GPU, and multi-GPU setups.

Overall, the paper addresses a significant problem and presents a novel and potentially impactful solution. The experimental results are promising, but a broader comparison and analysis could further increase the paper's significance.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates novelty through the Diffusion Buffer concept for online SE, enabling sub-second latency. The main strength is the reduction of computational complexity and its ability to achieve reasonable SE performance using a diffusion model with a relatively small buffer size (320-960ms delay). However, its limitations include a narrow experimental scope (filtered EARS-WHAM) and the lack of direct comparisons against strong state-of-the-art online SE baselines beyond DEMUCS. A higher score would require these comparisons, as well as a more detailed analysis of the parameter tuning space for the diffusion model and the impact of different architectural choices on computational cost and performance. The current score of 7.5 reflects a solid contribution but also acknowledges areas where further research is needed to solidify its position within the field.

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cell-o1: Training LLMs to Solve Single-Cell Reasoning Puzzles with Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02911v1)
- **Authors**: Yin Fang, Qiao Jin, Guangzhi Xiong, Bowen Jin, Xianrui Zhong, Siru Ouyang, Aidong Zhang, Jiawei Han, Zhiyong Lu
- **Abstract**: Cell type annotation is a key task in analyzing the heterogeneity of single-cell RNA sequencing data. Although recent foundation models automate this process, they typically annotate cells independently, without considering batch-level cellular context or providing explanatory reasoning. In contrast, human experts often annotate distinct cell types for different cell clusters based on their domain knowledge. To mimic this workflow, we introduce the CellPuzzles task, where the objective is to assign unique cell types to a batch of cells. This benchmark spans diverse tissues, diseases, and donor conditions, and requires reasoning across the batch-level cellular context to ensure label uniqueness. We find that off-the-shelf large language models (LLMs) struggle on CellPuzzles, with the best baseline (OpenAI's o1) achieving only 19.0% batch-level accuracy. To fill this gap, we propose Cell-o1, a 7B LLM trained via supervised fine-tuning on distilled reasoning traces, followed by reinforcement learning with batch-level rewards. Cell-o1 achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming o1 by over 73% and generalizing well across contexts. Further analysis of training dynamics and reasoning behaviors provides insights into batch-level annotation performance and emergent expert-like reasoning. Code and data are available at https://github.com/ncbi-nlp/cell-o1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CellPuzzles, a novel benchmark for evaluating cell type annotation as a batch-level reasoning task, mimicking how human experts annotate cells. Unlike traditional methods that annotate cells independently, CellPuzzles requires jointly assigning unique labels to cells within a batch, considering gene expression profiles and contextual metadata. The authors find that off-the-shelf large language models (LLMs) struggle with CellPuzzles. To address this, they propose Cell-01, a 7B LLM trained via supervised fine-tuning on distilled reasoning traces and reinforcement learning with batch-level rewards. Cell-01 achieves state-of-the-art performance on CellPuzzles, outperforming existing methods. Further analysis reveals emergent expert-like reasoning capabilities, such as self-reflection and curriculum reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *problem formulation* of cell type annotation as a batch-level reasoning task (CellPuzzles).  This is a significant departure from existing single-cell annotation methods. The introduction of Cell-01 itself, while a reasonable application of existing RL and distillation techniques, is less novel in terms of its algorithmic components. The emergent behaviors reported (self-reflection, curriculum learning) are interesting but require further, more rigorous validation to prove they are genuine and not artifacts of the training process or evaluation metrics. The focus on explainability and interpretability in the annotation process is another positive contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the accuracy, interpretability, and robustness of single-cell annotation. Batch-level reasoning is more aligned with how experts perform annotation, suggesting that CellPuzzles and Cell-01 could pave the way for more reliable and biologically meaningful annotations. The evaluation on unseen disease datasets further highlights the potential for generalization.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The CellPuzzles benchmark is a valuable contribution, providing a more realistic and challenging task for evaluating cell type annotation methods.
    *   Cell-01 demonstrates a significant performance improvement over existing LLMs on the CellPuzzles benchmark, highlighting the effectiveness of the proposed training strategy.
    *   The analysis of emergent reasoning behaviors is intriguing and provides valuable insights into the model's decision-making process.
    *   The evaluation on unseen diseases helps demonstrate Cell-01's generalization capabilities.
    *   Emphasis on structured reasoning, interpretability, and evaluation of not just accuracy but also the reasoning traces is a huge plus.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the emergent behaviors are mentioned, more rigorous quantitative validation and analysis of them are needed (beyond the win/tie/loss comparison).  How consistent are these behaviors? Are they truly helping performance, or are they just byproducts?
    *   The reliance on a *predefined candidate label set* limits the model's applicability to truly novel cell types, which might be encountered in new biological settings. The discussion on open-ended QA is useful but further work is required on how to extend the method and maintain performance on a broader set of cell types.
    *   While the experiments demonstrate strong performance, the model is still limited to the features used, namely gene expression profiles. The broader availability and usage of multi-modal datasets in scRNA-seq requires this method to extend to multi-modal contexts to be truly useful to most biologists and researchers.
    *   The computational overhead of RL training can be a limitation. GRPO helps reduce this overhead, but it is still more expensive than simple supervised learning.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the development of more accurate, interpretable, and robust single-cell annotation methods.  The CellPuzzles benchmark could become a standard evaluation tool, and the training strategy of Cell-01 could be adapted for other single-cell analysis tasks. The focus on incorporating expert knowledge and reasoning into automated methods is likely to gain traction in the field.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. While the CellPuzzles benchmark and expert reasoning approach are novel, the algorithmic components (SFT, RL) are established techniques. The claims regarding emergent behaviors are interesting but require more rigorous validation. The reliance on a predefined candidate label set is a significant limitation. Cell-01 achieves significant improvement on the established baseline models and could become a standard in future cell type annotation experiments.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sample, Predict, then Proceed: Self-Verification Sampling for Tool Use of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02918v1)
- **Authors**: Shangmin Guo, Omar Darwiche Domingues, Raphaël Avalos, Aaron Courville, Florian Strub
- **Abstract**: Tool use in stateful environments presents unique challenges for large language models (LLMs), where existing test-time compute strategies relying on repeated trials in the environment are impractical. We propose dynamics modelling (DyMo), a method that augments LLMs with a state prediction capability alongside function calling during post-training. This enables LLMs to predict the future states of their actions through an internal environment model. On the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard V2, DyMo improves success rates and significantly reduces hallucinations. We further integrate the internal environment model into self-verification sampling (SVS), and show that this substantially improves pass^k over number of trials k, and allows the model to refuse unreliable outputs. Together, DyMo and SVS greatly enhance the effectiveness and reliability of LLMs for tool use. We believe this work charts a path towards scalable planning RL methods for LLM inference without repeatedly querying the oracle environment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DyMo (Dynamics Modelling) and Self-Verification Sampling (SVS) to improve the tool-use capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in stateful environments. DyMo trains LLMs to predict the state changes resulting from their tool-use actions, essentially building an internal environment model. SVS leverages this internal model to sample multiple potential tool-use actions, predict their outcomes, and choose the most promising action without repeatedly querying the actual external environment. This approach addresses the challenge of tool use in scenarios where environmental interactions are costly or irreversible. The authors demonstrate that DyMo enhances performance and reduces hallucinations on the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard V2 (BFCL-V2).  Furthermore, SVS improves performance over increasing numbers of trials and allows the model to decline unreliable requests, enhancing overall reliability.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable perspective shift in how LLMs interact with tools in stateful environments. While the idea of incorporating "world models" into RL agents isn't new, the application and adaptation of this concept to LLMs for tool use, specifically focusing on reducing reliance on external environment queries during inference, has merit. The integration of this dynamics modeling with a self-verification strategy seems logical and represents a worthwhile contribution. The key is to reduce reliance on costly environment interactions. DyMo and SVS together seem to have the best of both worlds.
*   **Significance:**  The paper has practical significance because of its potential to improve the reliability and efficiency of LLMs in real-world applications involving tool use, particularly in scenarios where repeated interactions with the environment are costly or impossible. The reported improvements on the BFCL-V2 demonstrate the potential of the approach. If DyMo and SVS could be applied to Agentic scenarios, that would be a tremendous outcome.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a key limitation:** The paper tackles a critical bottleneck in LLM-based tool use: the difficulty of scaling test-time compute when interactions with the environment are expensive.
    *   **Well-defined methods:** DyMo and SVS are clearly defined and relatively straightforward to implement.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper provides experimental results on a well-known benchmark, demonstrating improved performance and reduced hallucinations.
    *   **Conceptually sound:**  The approach of building an internal environment model and using it for planning aligns with principles of reinforcement learning and cognitive science.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments primarily focus on single-turn interactions. The extension of DyMo and SVS to more complex, multi-turn interactions in dynamic environments is unclear. It may be harder to accurately predict future states as the number of steps increases.
    *   **Dependency on Pre-training:** The effectiveness of DyMo relies on the underlying LLM's ability to learn complex relationships. If the pre-trained model has limitations, DyMo may not be able to fully overcome them.
    *   **Dataset Creation Overhead:** Collecting the environment function dataset $D_{sp}$ might be more tedious than simply querying.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's ideas are likely to influence future research in LLM-based tool use, particularly in agentic systems and robotics. It could stimulate exploration of more sophisticated internal environment models and planning algorithms that minimize the need for external interactions. The study on "reliability" is also quite important, because as LLMs become smarter, it is imperative that they "know" when to "refuse" a request, as emphasized in the Space Odyssey quote.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a novel approach to a pressing challenge in LLM tool use, provides strong empirical validation, and connects its findings to established principles. Its potential influence on the field is significant. However, it has some limitations regarding the scale of interactions and dependence on the pre-trained model. Thus, a score of 7 reflects a solid contribution with room for future refinement.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Processor Chip Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02929v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyan Chang, Mingzhi Chen, Yunji Chen, Zhirong Chen, Dongrui Fan, Junfeng Gong, Nan Guo, Yinhe Han, Qinfen Hao, Shuo Hou, Xuan Huang, Pengwei Jin, Changxin Ke, Cangyuan Li, Guangli Li, Huawei Li, Kuan Li, Naipeng Li, Shengwen Liang, Cheng Liu, Hongwei Liu, Jiahua Liu, Junliang Lv, Jianan Mu, Jin Qin, Bin Sun, Chenxi Wang, Duo Wang, Mingjun Wang, Ying Wang, Chenggang Wu, Peiyang Wu, Teng Wu, Xiao Xiao, Mengyao Xie, Chenwei Xiong, Ruiyuan Xu, Mingyu Yan, Xiaochun Ye, Kuai Yu, Rui Zhang, Shuoming Zhang, Jiacheng Zhao
- **Abstract**: Computer System Architecture serves as a crucial bridge between software applications and the underlying hardware, encompassing components like compilers, CPUs, coprocessors, and RTL designs. Its development, from early mainframes to modern domain-specific architectures, has been driven by rising computational demands and advancements in semiconductor technology. However, traditional paradigms in computer system architecture design are confronting significant challenges, including a reliance on manual expertise, fragmented optimization across software and hardware layers, and high costs associated with exploring expansive design spaces. While automated methods leveraging optimization algorithms and machine learning have improved efficiency, they remain constrained by a single-stage focus, limited data availability, and a lack of comprehensive human domain knowledge. The emergence of large language models offers transformative opportunities for the design of computer system architecture. By leveraging the capabilities of LLMs in areas such as code generation, data analysis, and performance modeling, the traditional manual design process can be transitioned to a machine-based automated design approach. To harness this potential, we present the Large Processor Chip Model (LPCM), an LLM-driven framework aimed at achieving end-to-end automated computer architecture design. The LPCM is structured into three levels: Human-Centric; Agent-Orchestrated; and Model-Governed. This paper utilizes 3D Gaussian Splatting as a representative workload and employs the concept of software-hardware collaborative design to examine the implementation of the LPCM at Level 1, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach. Furthermore, this paper provides an in-depth discussion on the pathway to implementing Level 2 and Level 3 of the LPCM, along with an analysis of the existing challenges.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Large Processor Chip Model (LPCM), an LLM-driven framework aimed at automating the design of computer system architectures, from high-level specifications to low-level hardware implementation. It proposes a three-level development roadmap: Level 1 (Human-Centric), Level 2 (Agent-Orchestrated), and Level 3 (Model-Governed), gradually increasing the level of automation. The paper outlines the integration of LLMs into key aspects of chip design, including compiler design, binary translation, simulator creation, hardware/software partitioning, design space exploration (DSE), and HDL generation. A case study involving 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is presented to illustrate the Level 1 implementation, and future challenges and strategies are discussed. The overarching goal is to shift from manual design processes to machine-based automated design, leveraging LLMs for improved efficiency, performance, and innovation in computer system architecture.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a highly relevant and important problem: automating computer system architecture design to reduce development time, improve design quality, and explore larger design spaces. The core idea of using LLMs to achieve end-to-end automation is intriguing and timely, given the recent advancements in LLMs and agent systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper covers a wide range of areas within computer system architecture, presenting a holistic vision for LLM integration.
*   **Well-Defined Roadmap:** The three-level development roadmap provides a clear progression towards full automation, making the problem more manageable and allowing for incremental progress.
*   **Strong Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of traditional design methods and convincingly argues for the potential benefits of LLM-based automation.
*   **Illustrative Case Study:** The 3DGS case study provides a concrete example of how the LPCM framework can be applied, even in its early stages of development.
*   **Detailed Discussion of Challenges:** The paper acknowledges and discusses the significant challenges involved, including multi-modal knowledge integration, verification, and trustworthiness, demonstrating a realistic understanding of the problem's complexity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:** While the overall framework is novel, many of the individual components (e.g., using LLMs for HDL generation or DSE) are based on existing research. The key novelty lies in integrating these components into a cohesive, end-to-end automated design flow.
*   **Lack of Concrete Implementation Details:** The paper provides a high-level overview of the proposed framework, but lacks detailed implementation specifics for most of the modules, especially for Level 2 and Level 3.  There is a need for much more detail on how agents are orchestrated, how knowledge is represented and transferred, and how verification is handled in an automated manner.
*   **Overly Optimistic Expectations:**  The paper sometimes presents an overly optimistic view of what LLMs can achieve, especially regarding autonomous decision-making and guarantees of correctness. The limitations of current LLMs in handling complex reasoning and verification tasks should be more explicitly acknowledged. Level 3 feels very aspirational and distant.
*   **Limited Evaluation:** The case study focuses primarily on Level 1, and there is no experimental validation or demonstration of the higher levels of automation. This limits the assessment of the actual benefits of the proposed framework. The performance improvement over "a high performance A100" is vague and does not provide much information.
*   **Risk of "Frankenstein" Designs:** By automatically combining various system components (Compiler, RTL, Simulator, etc.), there is a significant risk of creating systems that don't work well together or have unforeseen side effects. The problem of system-level verification becomes much more complex and is not fully addressed in the paper.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to be highly influential in shaping future research directions in computer system architecture and automated design. It presents a compelling vision for how LLMs can transform the field, and it identifies key challenges and areas for future investigation. However, the success of the LPCM framework will depend on overcoming the technical hurdles and achieving practical implementations that demonstrate tangible benefits.  It serves more as a vision statement and position paper rather than a concrete solution.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable vision and direction for the future of computer system architecture design. However, it's primarily a position paper outlining a grand vision, with limited implementation details and experimental validation. While it integrates several existing research directions under one umbrella, its practical novelty remains unproven. The paper has the potential to stimulate significant research and development efforts, but the lack of concrete results at the higher automation levels warrants a tempered assessment.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 6/10

### A Multi-agent LLM-based JUit Test Generation with Strong Oracles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02943v1)
- **Authors**: Qinghua Xu, Guancheng Wang, Lionel Briand, Kui Liu
- **Abstract**: Unit testing plays a critical role in ensuring software correctness. However, writing unit tests manually is laborious, especially for strong typed languages like Java, motivating the need for automated approaches. Traditional methods primarily rely on search-based or randomized algorithms to generate tests that achieve high code coverage and produce regression oracles, which are derived from the program's current behavior rather than its intended functionality. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled oracle generation from natural language descriptions. However, existing LLM-based methods often require LLM fine-tuning or rely on external tools such as EvoSuite for test prefix generation. In this work, we propose CANDOR, a novel end-to-end, prompt-based LLM framework for automated JUnit test generation. CANDOR orchestrates multiple specialized LLM agents to generate JUnit tests, including both high-quality test prefixes and accurate oracles. To mitigate the notorious hallucinations in LLMs, we introduce a novel strategy that engages multiple reasoning LLMs in a panel discussion and generate accurate oracles based on consensus. Additionally, to reduce the verbosity of reasoning LLMs' outputs, we propose a novel dual-LLM pipeline to produce concise and structured oracle evaluations. Our experiments on the HumanEvalJava and LeetCodeJava datasets show that CANDOR can generate accurate oracles and is slightly better than EvoSuite in generating tests with high line coverage and clearly superior in terms of mutation score. Moreover, CANDOR significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art, prompt-based test generator LLM-Empirical, achieving improvements of 15.8 to 25.1 percentage points in oracle correctness on both correct and faulty source code. Ablation studies confirm the critical contributions of key agents in improving test prefix quality and oracle accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CANDOR, a novel multi-agent framework for automated unit test generation in Java.  CANDOR employs a prompt engineering-based approach, leveraging multiple specialized LLM agents (Initializer, Planner, Tester, Inspector) to collaboratively generate complete JUnit tests, including both test prefixes and oracles.  To improve oracle accuracy and mitigate LLM hallucinations, the framework incorporates a panel discussion involving multiple reasoning LLMs, a dual-LLM pipeline to produce concise oracle evaluations, and a curator agent to generate accurate oracles based on consensus. The authors evaluate CANDOR on HumanEvalJava and a LeetCode-derived dataset, comparing it to EvoSuite and LLM-Empirical. The results demonstrate CANDOR's effectiveness in generating high-coverage test prefixes, superior mutation scores, and more accurate specification-based oracles, especially for faulty code. Ablation studies confirm the contributions of key agents to test quality and oracle accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents several novel components. The multi-agent architecture, combining specialized LLM roles for test case generation, goes beyond existing approaches that often rely on a single LLM or hybrid approaches with traditional test generation tools. The panel discussion for oracle generation, designed to address LLM hallucinations, and the dual-LLM pipeline for mitigating reasoning verbosity are also innovative. Using strong oracles created from requirements derived from a natural language description of the SUT is a major improvement compared to traditional methods that rely on regression oracles or tests derived from the existing, possibly faulty, code.

*   **Significance:** Automating unit test generation remains a crucial challenge in software engineering. The paper's contribution addresses key limitations of existing LLM-based approaches, such as reliance on fine-tuning, dependence on external tools like EvoSuite for test prefix generation, and issues with oracle correctness. CANDOR's ability to generate accurate, specification-based oracles, especially for faulty code, has the potential to significantly improve the effectiveness of automated testing. The gains in test coverage metrics and oracle quality are substantial compared to the baseline methods, demonstrating the practical impact of CANDOR's innovations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   End-to-end automated test generation using prompt engineering without relying on fine-tuning or EvoSuite.
    *   Novel multi-agent architecture with specialized roles for improved test generation.
    *   Effective panel discussion and dual-LLM pipeline for improving oracle accuracy and mitigating LLM hallucinations.
    *   Clear empirical evaluation demonstrating CANDOR's effectiveness compared to existing methods.
    *   Addresses a crucial need for high-quality, specification-based oracles.
    *   Thorough evaluation including ablation studies
    *   Rigorous experimental setup, including statistical significance tests

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation focuses on method-level datasets, which limits the ability to assess CANDOR's performance on more complex project-level programs with dependencies.
    *   The choice of specific LLMs (LLaMA 3.1 70B and DeepSeek R1) may impact performance, and further exploration with other LLMs is warranted.
    *   The study might be susceptible to data leakage as the LLMs may have been exposed to parts of the datasets during training, however the mutation testing attempts to mitigate this.
    *   Limited assessment of performance compared to the fine-tuned TOGLL method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in automated unit test generation by demonstrating the effectiveness of multi-agent LLM architectures, panel discussions for oracle generation, and dual-LLM pipelines for handling verbose reasoning LLMs. It encourages a move away from reliance on regression oracles and towards specification-based testing. The approach also provides valuable insights into effectively instructing and combining LLMs for complex software engineering tasks.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of automated unit test generation. The novelty of the multi-agent architecture, the panel discussion, and the dual-LLM pipeline are significant.  The performance gains compared to baselines are substantial, highlighting the practical value of the approach. The paper could be stronger with an evaluation on more complex project-level datasets and a more detailed comparison to fine-tuned approaches like TOGLL. However, the innovations in oracle generation and the comprehensive empirical evaluation justify a high score. The paper offers a practical advance that can have a substantial impact on software testing and thus warrants an 8.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Towards More Effective Fault Detection in LLM-Based Unit Test Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02954v1)
- **Authors**: Guancheng Wang, Qinghua Xu, Lionel C. Briand, Kui Liu
- **Abstract**: Unit tests play a vital role in uncovering potential faults in software. While tools like EvoSuite focus on maximizing code coverage, recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have shifted attention toward LLM-based test generation. However, code coverage metrics -- such as line and branch coverage -- remain overly emphasized in reported research, despite being weak indicators of a test suite's fault-detection capability. In contrast, \textit{mutation score} offers a more reliable and stringent measure, as demonstrated in our findings where some test suites achieve 100\% coverage but only 4\% mutation score. Although a few studies consider mutation score, the effectiveness of LLMs in killing mutants remains underexplored. In this paper, we propose MUTGEN, a mutation-guided, LLM-based test generation approach that incorporates mutation feedback directly into the prompt. Evaluated on 204 subjects from two benchmarks, MUTGEN significantly outperforms both EvoSuite and vanilla prompt-based strategies in terms of mutation score. Furthermore, MUTGEN introduces an iterative generation mechanism that pushes the limits of LLMs in killing additional mutants. Our study also provide insights into the limitations of LLM-based generation, analyzing the reasons for live and uncovered mutants, and the impact of different mutation operators on generation effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MUTGEN, a mutation-guided, LLM-based approach to unit test generation aimed at maximizing fault detection capabilities, measured by mutation score. It argues that traditional code coverage metrics are insufficient indicators of a test suite's effectiveness and that mutation score provides a more reliable measure. MUTGEN incorporates mutation feedback (live and uncovered mutant information) into the prompt to guide the LLM in generating more effective test cases. The approach also includes a code summarization step to mitigate misleading comments and an iterative generation mechanism to improve mutation scores further.  The performance of MUTGEN is evaluated against EvoSuite (a search-based technique) and a vanilla LLM prompting strategy (GENvanilla) on HumanEval-Java and a newly created LeetCode-Java dataset. The results show MUTGEN significantly outperforms the baselines in terms of mutation score. The paper also presents an analysis of mutation operator effectiveness, reasons for live/uncovered mutants, and an ablation study to assess the contribution of each component within MUTGEN.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by explicitly using mutation feedback to guide LLM-based test generation. While previous work has explored mutation testing in the context of LLMs, this paper focuses on maximizing mutation score by incorporating live and uncovered mutant information, and by fixing assertions. The iterative generation strategy with mutation feedback is a significant contribution. Code summarization to avoid misleading the LLM and fixing test errors are also relevant enhancements. The new LeetCode-Java dataset also increases the evaluation scope and diversity.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue in LLM-based test generation: the over-reliance on code coverage metrics as a measure of test suite quality. By demonstrating the superiority of mutation score and proposing a method to explicitly optimize for it, the paper makes a compelling argument for rethinking evaluation practices. The performance improvements shown by MUTGEN over established techniques like EvoSuite, especially in mutation score, are significant. The analysis of different mutation operators and the reasons for mutants surviving or remaining uncovered provides valuable insights into the limitations of LLMs in test generation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined approach with clear explanations of each component.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with multiple datasets and baselines.
    *   Detailed analysis of results, including ablation studies and mutation operator analysis.
    *   Address the assertion failures with a iterative fixing step
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The subjects used are relatively simple method-level functions. While using Defects4J as a subject is mentioned in the threats to validity section, it is not actually implemented. Generalizing to more complex, real-world programs remains a potential challenge.
    *   While model-agnostic in design, the evaluation only uses Llama-3.3. A more thorough evaluation across various LLMs would strengthen the findings.
    *   While the performance of MUTGEN is better than that of EvoSuite on HumanEval-Java in terms of coverage, EvoSuite is slightly better in terms of coverage in Leetcode-Java dataset. There could be more explanations of why that is the case.
    *   Although the paper states in the introduction that "high code coverage does not necessarily imply strong fault detection capability", it does report branch coverage and line coverage. It is not clear whether these metrics are reported to make the evaluation more thorough or if it actually influences the design of the MUTGEN.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in LLM-based test generation by promoting mutation testing as a more rigorous evaluation metric and by demonstrating the effectiveness of mutation feedback in guiding LLMs. The insights gained from the mutation operator analysis can inform the design of more targeted prompting strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of LLM-based test generation by introducing a novel approach that optimizes for mutation score. The comprehensive evaluation and analysis provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of MUTGEN. The identified weaknesses relate primarily to the scope of the evaluation (simple subjects, single LLM) and the need for further investigation into the generalizability and scalability of the approach. Still, the clear problem definition, well-designed solution, and substantial performance gains warrant a high score. The approach is likely to be adopted by other researches in the community to be used in their LLM-based test generation.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### HACo-Det: A Study Towards Fine-Grained Machine-Generated Text Detection under Human-AI Coauthoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02959v1)
- **Authors**: Zhixiong Su, Yichen Wang, Herun Wan, Zhaohan Zhang, Minnan Luo
- **Abstract**: The misuse of large language models (LLMs) poses potential risks, motivating the development of machine-generated text (MGT) detection. Existing literature primarily concentrates on binary, document-level detection, thereby neglecting texts that are composed jointly by human and LLM contributions. Hence, this paper explores the possibility of fine-grained MGT detection under human-AI coauthoring. We suggest fine-grained detectors can pave pathways toward coauthored text detection with a numeric AI ratio. Specifically, we propose a dataset, HACo-Det, which produces human-AI coauthored texts via an automatic pipeline with word-level attribution labels. We retrofit seven prevailing document-level detectors to generalize them to word-level detection. Then we evaluate these detectors on HACo-Det on both word- and sentence-level detection tasks. Empirical results show that metric-based methods struggle to conduct fine-grained detection with a 0.462 average F1 score, while finetuned models show superior performance and better generalization across domains. However, we argue that fine-grained co-authored text detection is far from solved. We further analyze factors influencing performance, e.g., context window, and highlight the limitations of current methods, pointing to potential avenues for improvement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HACo-Det: A Study Towards Fine-Grained Machine-Generated Text Detection under Human-AI Coauthoring" addresses the limitations of existing MGT detection methods that primarily focus on binary, document-level classification.  It introduces a new task and dataset, HACo-Det, designed for fine-grained (word-level and sentence-level) MGT detection in human-AI co-authored texts. The dataset is generated via an automated pipeline involving iterative paraphrasing by instruction-tuned LLMs, with word-level authorship labels. The paper then retrofits and evaluates seven existing document-level MGT detectors on the HACo-Det dataset. The empirical results demonstrate that while metric-based methods struggle, finetuned models show promising performance but are still far from solved. The paper also analyzes factors influencing performance, such as context window size, and highlights limitations of existing techniques, suggesting avenues for improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by explicitly tackling the under-explored problem of fine-grained MGT detection in human-AI co-authored contexts.  This is a timely and relevant area, given the increasing prevalence of human-AI collaboration in text generation. The HACo-Det dataset is a novel resource for research in this direction, and the automated pipeline for generating co-authored text is a practical contribution. The reformation of existing detectors for the fine-grained setting and the subsequent benchmark provides valuable insights. The exploration of zero-shot performance and analysis of limitations are also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its focus on a real-world problem that is becoming increasingly important.  Binary MGT detection is becoming inadequate as human-AI collaboration becomes more seamless. Fine-grained detection offers potential benefits in authorship attribution, identifying plagiarism, and understanding the extent of AI's contribution. The empirical analysis highlights the deficiencies of current methods and opens up avenues for future research. The analysis on generalization capabilities is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Framing:** Clearly identifies and motivates the need for fine-grained MGT detection.
    *   **Dataset Creation:**  Introduces a novel dataset, HACo-Det, addressing a specific need.  The automated pipeline for generating co-authored text is a valuable asset. The dataset is constructed with word level labels.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Systematically evaluates a range of existing MGT detectors.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** Analyzes factors affecting performance and discusses limitations. This discussion is one of the paper's most valuable parts, contributing to understanding the challenges in this area.
    *   **Out-of-Domain Evaluation**: Out-of-Domain evaluation demonstrates some important limitations in the current models, specifically on the generalization capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Task Design Assumptions:** While the iterative paraphrasing is a good start, it's still a simplified model of human-AI interaction. Human-AI coauthoring can be more nuanced and involve more complex forms of collaboration than simple paraphrasing.The specific instructions for paraphrasing might influence the dataset biases.
    *   **Limited Scope of Baselines:** The set of detectors used as baselines is somewhat limited; although several relevant approaches are considered.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The improvements shown by the baseline models are relatively incremental and not substantial enough to solve the problem.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in MGT detection by shifting the focus towards finer-grained analysis and human-AI co-authored content. The HACo-Det dataset will likely serve as a valuable resource for future research, and the analysis of limitations will guide the development of more effective techniques. The out-of-domain analysis will also contribute to a more critical analysis of current and future models.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7 to this paper.

*The paper successfully identifies a growing need in MGT detection (fine-grained detection in human-AI co-authored contexts), proposes a novel dataset addressing this need, and performs a comprehensive empirical analysis of existing detectors.*

*The insights obtained by the analysis are useful, and are likely to direct future research efforts. The limitations associated with the datasets are explicitly mentioned which is useful for future research.*

*While the paper's contributions are significant, the limitations of the simulated co-authoring process, relatively incremental performance of existing methods, and limited scope of baselines do not allow for a higher score.*

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FlowerTune: A Cross-Domain Benchmark for Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02961v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Gao, Massimo Roberto Scamarcia, Javier Fernandez-Marques, Mohammad Naseri, Chong Shen Ng, Dimitris Stripelis, Zexi Li, Tao Shen, Jiamu Bai, Daoyuan Chen, Zikai Zhang, Rui Hu, InSeo Song, Lee KangYoon, Hong Jia, Ting Dang, Junyan Wang, Zheyuan Liu, Daniel Janes Beutel, Lingjuan Lyu, Nicholas D. Lane
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved state-of-the-art results across diverse domains, yet their development remains reliant on vast amounts of publicly available data, raising concerns about data scarcity and the lack of access to domain-specific, sensitive information. Federated Learning (FL) presents a compelling framework to address these challenges by enabling decentralized fine-tuning on pre-trained LLMs without sharing raw data. However, the compatibility and performance of pre-trained LLMs in FL settings remain largely under explored. We introduce the FlowerTune LLM Leaderboard, a first-of-its-kind benchmarking suite designed to evaluate federated fine-tuning of LLMs across four diverse domains: general NLP, finance, medical, and coding. Each domain includes federated instruction-tuning datasets and domain-specific evaluation metrics. Our results, obtained through a collaborative, open-source and community-driven approach, provide the first comprehensive comparison across 26 pre-trained LLMs with different aggregation and fine-tuning strategies under federated settings, offering actionable insights into model performance, resource constraints, and domain adaptation. This work lays the foundation for developing privacy-preserving, domain-specialized LLMs for real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "FlowerTune: A Cross-Domain Benchmark for Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FlowerTune, a new benchmarking suite for evaluating federated fine-tuning (FL) of Large Language Models (LLMs) across four domains: general NLP, finance, medical, and coding.  It provides federated instruction-tuning datasets and domain-specific evaluation metrics for each domain. The authors present results from comprehensive experiments comparing 26 pre-trained LLMs under various FL settings, exploring different aggregation and fine-tuning strategies.  The work aims to offer insights into model performance, resource constraints, and domain adaptation in FL settings, facilitating the development of privacy-preserving and domain-specialized LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in creating a *unified benchmark* for federated fine-tuning of LLMs across diverse domains.  Prior work has explored FL for LLMs, but FlowerTune offers a consolidated and comprehensive platform. The large-scale empirical study involving 26 LLMs and various aggregation and fine-tuning techniques contributes valuable data to the nascent field. However, some elements, such as the use of LoRA/DoRA and basic FL aggregation methods (FedAvg, FedProx), are not inherently novel in isolation.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper stems from addressing a critical gap in LLM research.  The reliance on vast, publicly available data raises concerns about data scarcity and accessibility, especially for domain-specific, sensitive information.  FL offers a promising solution, but practical deployment requires thorough evaluation. FlowerTune aims to standardize this evaluation, enabling researchers and practitioners to:

    *   Compare the performance of different LLMs and FL techniques in realistic settings.
    *   Understand the trade-offs between model size, performance, and resource constraints (communication, memory).
    *   Advance the development of privacy-preserving, domain-adapted LLMs.

    The availability of the benchmark, code, and datasets promotes reproducible research and community-driven exploration. However, the lack of extensive hyperparameter tuning, while understandable for the scale of the study, somewhat limits the optimality of the reported results. The insights drawn from the experiments, such as the relative performance of certain model families and the importance of base model selection, are insightful but somewhat preliminary and require further investigation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper presents a large-scale evaluation of LLMs under FL settings.
    *   **Open-Source and Community-Driven:** Promotes reproducibility and collaboration.
    *   **Multiple Domains:** Addresses the need for domain-specific LLMs in sensitive areas.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** Examines resource constraints relevant to FL deployment.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Hyperparameter Tuning:** Lack of extensive tuning may limit the optimality of reported results.
    *   **Reliance on Basic FL Techniques:** While understandable, exploring only standard FL methods could limit insight.
    *   **Preliminary Findings:** The conclusions drawn are useful but require further in-depth analysis.
    *   **Task Simplicity Consideration:** Some of the tasks can be seen as comparably simpler to others, for example the financial task. This results in lightweight models exhibiting superior results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The FlowerTune benchmark has the potential to become a valuable resource for the FL and LLM communities. It can accelerate research on privacy-preserving AI, domain adaptation, and resource-efficient model deployment. The insights gained can inform the design of more effective FL algorithms and the selection of appropriate models for different applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a much-needed benchmark in the FL and LLM space, particularly concerning specialized domains. However, the core techniques used are not ground breaking, and the study is limited by only a minimal exploration of the hyperparameters. While the results and findings are insightful and will be useful for future research, they are nonetheless preliminary.
