### Memory-Efficient and Privacy-Preserving Collaborative Training for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02965v1)
- **Authors**: Ze Yu Zhang, Bolin Ding, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low
- **Abstract**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) has been gaining popularity due to its successful adaptation to large language models (LLMs). In this work, we introduce Privacy-preserving Collaborative Mixture-of-Experts (PC-MoE), which leverages the sparsity of the MoE architecture for memory-efficient decentralized collaborative LLM training, enabling multiple parties with limited GPU-memory and data resources to collectively train more capable LLMs than they could achieve individually. At the same time, this approach protects training data privacy of each participant by keeping training data, as well as parts of the forward pass signal and gradients locally within each party. By design, PC-MoE synergistically combines the strengths of distributed computation with strong confidentiality assurances. Unlike most privacy-preserving schemes, which pay for confidentiality with lower task accuracy, our framework breaks that trade-off: across seven popular LLM benchmarks, it almost matches (and sometimes exceeds) the performance and convergence rate of a fully centralized model, enjoys near 70% peak GPU RAM reduction, while being fully robust against reconstruction attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Privacy-preserving Collaborative Mixture-of-Experts (PC-MoE), a novel framework for training large language models (LLMs) in a decentralized and privacy-preserving manner. PC-MoE leverages the sparsity of MoE architectures to enable multiple parties with limited resources to collaboratively train capable LLMs. It distributes expert parameters across parties and keeps training data, forward pass signals, and gradients local to each participant, protecting data privacy. The framework combines distributed computation with confidentiality guarantees and demonstrates comparable or even superior performance to centralized models across several LLM benchmarks. It also exhibits significant GPU RAM reduction and robustness against reconstruction attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of collaborative MoE training with a strong focus on both memory efficiency and data privacy is innovative. While distributed training and privacy-preserving techniques exist, combining them specifically for MoEs with architectural sparsity as a core privacy mechanism is a significant contribution. The novelty lies in *how* they've architected the training process to minimize data leakage by routing activations and gradients along the same sparse paths. Prior work usually involves either sharing gradients with differential privacy (leading to performance degradation) or not considering collaboration at all. Their technique offers a better balance.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in LLM development: the prohibitive hardware and data requirements that limit participation to a few well-funded entities. PC-MoE democratizes access to LLM training, potentially enabling smaller organizations and research labs to contribute to the field. This has significant implications for open-source LLM development and broader participation in AI research. The empirical results show a compelling combination of memory savings and performance that rivals centralized training, making the approach practically viable. The resistance to reconstruction attacks is also a crucial component.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Privacy Guarantees:** The architectural design inherently limits data leakage, validated by empirical results demonstrating robustness against gradient inversion attacks. The assumptions laid out for privacy analysis are clearly stated.
*   **Memory Efficiency:** Demonstrates substantial GPU RAM reduction, allowing training on less powerful hardware. This lowers the barrier to entry for collaborative LLM training.
*   **Performance Preservation:** Achieves performance comparable to centralized training across a range of benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the collaborative approach.
*   **Practical Viability:** The protocol is designed to be practical, minimizing inter-party communication and synchronization overhead.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results, including ablation studies and comparisons with baselines.
*   **Scalability Analysis:** Experiments demonstrate that the performance benefit increases as the number of parties grows.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Semi-honest Threat Model:**  The threat model relies on the assumption of an exponentially decaying likelihood of collusion. While this simplifies analysis, it might not hold in all real-world scenarios, and more sophisticated attack models could reveal vulnerabilities.
*   **Communication Overhead (Not explicitly measured):** While the paper mentions minimizing communication, a detailed analysis and measurement of communication costs (especially compared to fully centralized or federated learning approaches) would strengthen the evaluation.
*   **Heterogeneous Compute Capabilities:** The paper assumes parties have similar compute capabilities and data sizes. An analysis of how the approach adapts to highly heterogeneous settings would be valuable.
*   **Limited Attack Robustness to Colluding Parties:** The success of the approach is heavily dependent on the collusion prior in Eq (1). While the paper attempts to address this via the privacy analysis, there is a lack of empirical evaluations regarding the number of colluding parties needed to successfully perform a gradient inversion attack.

**Justification for Score:**

PC-MoE presents a valuable and well-executed contribution to the field of collaborative LLM training. The framework effectively addresses the challenges of hardware constraints and data privacy, while maintaining competitive performance. The combination of architectural privacy, memory efficiency, and performance preservation makes this work particularly relevant.

However, the reliance on the semi-honest threat model and limited empirical analysis of highly colluding attackers means the work needs to be improved before it is considered near perfection. As such, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Expanding before Inferring: Enhancing Factuality in Large Language Models through Premature Layers Interpolation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02973v1)
- **Authors**: Dingwei Chen, Ziqiang Liu, Feiteng Fang, Chak Tou Leong, Shiwen Ni, Ahmadreza Argha, Hamid Alinejad-Rokny, Min Yang, Chengming Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable capabilities in text understanding and generation. However, their tendency to produce factually inconsistent outputs, commonly referred to as ''hallucinations'', remains a critical challenge. Existing approaches, such as retrieval-based and inference-time correction methods, primarily address this issue at the input or output level, often overlooking the intrinsic information refinement process and the role of premature layers. Meanwhile, alignment- and fine-tuning-based methods are resource-intensive. In this paper, we propose PLI (Premature Layers Interpolation), a novel, training-free, and plug-and-play intervention designed to enhance factuality. PLI mitigates hallucinations by inserting premature layers formed through mathematical interpolation with adjacent layers. Inspired by stable diffusion and sampling steps, PLI extends the depth of information processing and transmission in LLMs, improving factual coherence. Experiments on four publicly available datasets demonstrate that PLI effectively reduces hallucinations while outperforming existing baselines in most cases. Further analysis suggests that the success of layer interpolation is closely linked to LLMs' internal mechanisms. To promote reproducibility, we will release our code and data upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Premature Layers Interpolation (PLI), a novel, training-free, and plug-and-play method to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs).  PLI inserts new "premature" layers into the LLM architecture by interpolating the parameters of adjacent existing layers. This expands the depth of information processing within the model, improving factual coherence without requiring retraining or fine-tuning. The method is inspired by stable diffusion, where more steps generally lead to higher quality outputs. Extensive experiments on various datasets demonstrate that PLI effectively reduces hallucinations and can be integrated with existing hallucination mitigation techniques.  The paper includes analyses investigating the relationship between layer interpolation effectiveness and the internal mechanisms of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of inserting interpolated layers is relatively novel in the context of mitigating hallucinations in LLMs.  While techniques like model merging exist, applying interpolation specifically to expand the *depth* of information processing and focusing on intermediate layer representations is a unique approach. The inspiration drawn from stable diffusion is also a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:**  The potential significance stems from the method's simplicity and efficiency. PLI is training-free and plug-and-play, making it readily applicable to existing LLMs without the resource-intensive process of fine-tuning or alignment. The experimental results, showing improvements across multiple datasets and integration with other methods, suggest that PLI could be a valuable tool for improving the factuality of LLM outputs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity and Practicality:**  The training-free and plug-and-play nature of PLI is a significant advantage, making it easily adoptable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results on several standard benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of PLI in reducing hallucinations. The integration with existing methods further strengthens the results.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Analysis:** The paper includes ablation studies exploring layer placement and interpolation techniques, providing insights into the method's inner workings. The KL divergence analysis is a good attempt to understand how PLI affects the model's internal representations.
    *   **Adaptability:** The method is tested across multiple different base models, showing its generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Understanding:** While the paper attempts to explain the effectiveness of PLI, the underlying mechanisms are not fully elucidated. The reliance on an analogy to stable diffusion could be stronger with a more rigorous theoretical connection. Further investigation into *why* interpolation at specific layers is beneficial is warranted.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The improvements, while consistent, are often incremental rather than transformative.  A more dramatic improvement on at least one dataset could have significantly increased the paper's impact.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** Although the paper claims the computational overhead is minimal, the analysis shows that inference time increases. A more detailed discussion of the trade-off between accuracy and inference speed would be helpful. The cost increase from 1-3 layers is presented, but the optimal number of layers is left relatively vague.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence the field by inspiring further research into intermediate layer manipulation for improving LLM performance.  The plug-and-play nature of PLI could lead to its adoption by practitioners seeking to enhance the factuality of existing models without significant computational investment.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant method for mitigating hallucinations in LLMs.  The strong empirical validation, adaptability testing, and ablation studies are compelling. However, the incremental nature of the improvements and the limited theoretical understanding prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. This means the method presents an improvement but is not a fundamentally new way of approaching LLM hallucinations, and more justification and evidence is required to properly explain its success.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Astrophotography turbulence mitigation via generative models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02981v1)
- **Authors**: Joonyeoup Kim, Yu Yuan, Xingguang Zhang, Xijun Wang, Stanley Chan
- **Abstract**: Photography is the cornerstone of modern astronomical and space research. However, most astronomical images captured by ground-based telescopes suffer from atmospheric turbulence, resulting in degraded imaging quality. While multi-frame strategies like lucky imaging can mitigate some effects, they involve intensive data acquisition and complex manual processing. In this paper, we propose AstroDiff, a generative restoration method that leverages both the high-quality generative priors and restoration capabilities of diffusion models to mitigate atmospheric turbulence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AstroDiff outperforms existing state-of-the-art learning-based methods in astronomical image turbulence mitigation, providing higher perceptual quality and better structural fidelity under severe turbulence conditions. Our code and additional results are available at https://web-six-kappa-66.vercel.app/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AstroDiff, a novel generative restoration method designed to mitigate atmospheric turbulence in astrophotography. AstroDiff leverages the strengths of both generative priors (diffusion models) and restoration techniques within a dual-branch framework. The method uses Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics (SGLD) to fuse a generative branch trained on clean astronomical images with a restoration branch trained on turbulence-degraded images.  The paper also contributes three new datasets: PlanetSYN, TechSYN, and AstroEVA, tailored for training and evaluating astrophotography turbulence mitigation models.  Experiments demonstrate that AstroDiff outperforms existing state-of-the-art learning-based methods in terms of perceptual quality and structural fidelity, especially under severe turbulence conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing techniques (diffusion models, SGLD, U-Net architecture) adapted and tailored for a specific problem: astrophotography turbulence mitigation. The dual-branch approach, combining generative priors with a restoration branch, is a key novelty. The creation of the three datasets is a valuable contribution in itself, addressing the scarcity of appropriate training data in this domain. While the core components (diffusion models, SGLD) are not novel *per se*, their application and integration in this specific context is, along with the dataset contributions.

*   **Significance:** Atmospheric turbulence is a significant challenge in ground-based astronomy, degrading image quality and hindering scientific discovery. Methods that can effectively mitigate turbulence are therefore highly valuable. The paper's demonstration that AstroDiff outperforms existing methods on real data and under severe turbulence highlights its potential significance. The improved perceptual quality and structural fidelity are particularly important for astronomical image analysis. The performance improvements under severe turbulence, where existing methods falter, suggests a real advance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dual-Branch Architecture:** The integration of generative priors and restoration offers a promising way to address the limitations of purely data-driven approaches, particularly when dealing with complex degradations and limited training data. The dual branch allows the incorporation of high-level knowledge about the underlying objects, while incorporating image-specific noise.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:**  The datasets (PlanetSYN, TechSYN, and AstroEVA) are a valuable resource for the community, addressing a critical bottleneck in training and evaluating turbulence mitigation methods for astrophotography. AstroEVA, especially, is important because it contains real telescope captures, something which allows it to be much more useful in determining the models’ actual applicability to the problem.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides compelling experimental results demonstrating the superiority of AstroDiff over state-of-the-art methods, both qualitatively and quantitatively (although the lower PSNR compared to competing methods could be seen as a weakness and is properly addressed by the authors). The use of real data in AstroEVA increases the impact of the experiments. The results of the ablation study show that the two-branch architecture actually does provide a benefit over only a restoration-based method.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method and its motivation.
    *   **Addressing a Critical Problem:** Mitigating atmospheric turbulence is an essential task for ground-based astronomy, thereby enhancing the scientific output and efficiency of ground-based observations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:** While the combination is novel, the individual components of AstroDiff (diffusion models, U-Net, SGLD) are well-established. However, given the nature of engineering research, this is not necessarily a fatal flaw.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not explicitly discuss the computational cost of AstroDiff. Generative models, especially diffusion models, can be computationally expensive, which might limit their practical applicability. This is a potential limitation that needs to be addressed.
    *   **PSNR Tradeoff:** The paper notes that the method favors perceptual quality over pixel-perfect accuracy, which leads to lower PSNR compared to other methods. For some scientific applications, precise pixel-level accuracy may be important, and this tradeoff might be a limitation.
    *   **Limited Scope of Real Data:** The evaluation on real data is focused on Jupiter, Mars, and the moon. While it’s good to have the real data, further evaluations with different kinds of astronomical objects would be beneficial, as different objects may have different structures/textures/phenomena which may not generalize for these models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of astrophotography by providing a more effective turbulence mitigation method and by contributing valuable training data. The dual-branch approach could inspire other researchers to explore similar architectures for other image restoration tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field. The AstroDiff method combines existing techniques in a novel way to address a significant problem in astrophotography. The creation of the three datasets is a major asset for future research. The experimental results are compelling. While the reliance on existing techniques and the computational cost are minor drawbacks, the overall impact of the paper is positive. Overall, while each component isn’t new, the overall construction of AstroDiff provides a net new benefit to the scientific community.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Performance of leading large language models in May 2025 in Membership of the Royal College of General Practitioners-style examination questions: a cross-sectional analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02987v1)
- **Authors**: Richard Armitage
- **Abstract**: Background: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated substantial potential to support clinical practice. Other than Chat GPT4 and its predecessors, few LLMs, especially those of the leading and more powerful reasoning model class, have been subjected to medical specialty examination questions, including in the domain of primary care. This paper aimed to test the capabilities of leading LLMs as of May 2025 (o3, Claude Opus 4, Grok3, and Gemini 2.5 Pro) in primary care education, specifically in answering Member of the Royal College of General Practitioners (MRCGP) style examination questions. Methods: o3, Claude Opus 4, Grok3, and Gemini 2.5 Pro were tasked to answer 100 randomly chosen multiple choice questions from the Royal College of General Practitioners GP SelfTest on 25 May 2025. Questions included textual information, laboratory results, and clinical images. Each model was prompted to answer as a GP in the UK and was provided with full question information. Each question was attempted once by each model. Responses were scored against correct answers provided by GP SelfTest. Results: The total score of o3, Claude Opus 4, Grok3, and Gemini 2.5 Pro was 99.0%, 95.0%, 95.0%, and 95.0%, respectively. The average peer score for the same questions was 73.0%. Discussion: All models performed remarkably well, and all substantially exceeded the average performance of GPs and GP registrars who had answered the same questions. o3 demonstrated the best performance, while the performances of the other leading models were comparable with each other and were not substantially lower than that of o3. These findings strengthen the case for LLMs, particularly reasoning models, to support the delivery of primary care, especially those that have been specifically trained on primary care clinical data.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary**

The paper investigates the performance of leading large language models (LLMs) available in May 2025 (OpenAI's o3, Anthropic's Claude Opus 4, xAI's Grok-3, and Google's Gemini 2.5 Pro) on questions from the Royal College of General Practitioners (RCGP) GP SelfTest, a resource used by GPs preparing for membership exams. The LLMs were tasked with answering 100 multiple-choice questions, and their accuracy was compared to the average scores of GPs and GP registrars on the same questions. The results showed that all the LLMs substantially outperformed human test-takers, with o3 achieving near-perfect accuracy. While the LLMs demonstrated superior performance, the study acknowledges that they are not a complete replacement for human GPs due to the complexities of real-world clinical information, and the fact that these models reported the same confidence whether the answer was right or wrong.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Timeliness:** The paper directly addresses a current and rapidly evolving area of research—the application of LLMs in medicine. The study is forward-looking, evaluating models available in the near future (May 2025), which provides insights into their potential impact on healthcare and how fast the technology is improving.
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly defines its research question: assessing the competence of leading LLMs in answering RCGP exam questions.
    *   **Methodology:** The use of a standardized, validated question bank (GP SelfTest) strengthens the study's rigor and allows for direct comparison of LLM performance to human performance. The study uses a cross-sectional design that assesses LLMs answering capability as of the end of May 2025. The paper also carefully defines how each LLM model was assessed.
    *   **Significant Findings:** The findings are impactful, highlighting the remarkable accuracy of LLMs in a medical domain that requires both factual knowledge and reasoning abilities.
    *   **Balanced Discussion:** The discussion is well-balanced, acknowledging the strengths of LLMs while also addressing limitations and potential risks. It highlights the importance of viewing LLMs as tools to augment, rather than replace, clinicians.
    *   **Acknowledges Limitations:** The authors clearly stated limitations of the study; acknowledging the number of examination questions, the number of non-textual data such as tables of results, graphs, and clinical images in order to more thoroughly assess the broadness of the models' analysing abilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Sample Size:** While 100 questions are reasonable, a larger sample size, especially a stratified sample covering different subspecialties within general practice, would enhance the statistical power and generalizability of the results.
    *   **Single Prompt Style:** The experiment uses one prompt style (e.g. "Answer the following question as a GP in the UK..."). Exploring the robustness of these model's performance for various prompts would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of Statistical Significance Tests:** The paper presents percentage scores but does not include statistical tests to determine if the differences in performance between the different LLMs are statistically significant. Without this, it's difficult to confidently claim that o3 is superior to the other models.
    *   **Artificial Setting:** As the authors acknowledge, exam questions are a simplified representation of real-world clinical scenarios. The study does not assess the LLMs' ability to handle unstructured, incomplete, or ambiguous information, which are common in clinical practice.
    *   **Lack of cost analysis** A cost analysis of each of the various models may influence the decisions that are made when selecting a particular model for a given use.
    *   **Reproducibility** Future researchers may find it difficult to reproduce the exact results of this experiment due to various changes with LLMs over time.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper's novelty stems from its timely evaluation of advanced reasoning models within a specific medical specialty, primary care. It provides a snapshot of the state-of-the-art performance of LLMs on questions, a task relevant to medical education and decision support.
    *   The study contributes to the growing body of literature assessing LLMs in medicine and emphasizes the importance of evaluating reasoning models, which are showing particularly promising results.
    *   The paper's findings have implications for the design of medical education tools, clinical decision support systems, and healthcare workflows.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The study could influence medical educators to explore the use of LLMs for training and assessment.
    *   It may encourage developers of clinical decision support systems to incorporate LLMs into their tools.
    *   It may prompt further research into the integration of LLMs into primary care settings, focusing on tasks such as diagnosis, treatment planning, and patient education.

**Overall Score and Justification**

Based on the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7/10**.

*   **Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by evaluating the performance of leading LLMs in a specific medical context. Its results are significant, showcasing the potential of these models to assist clinicians and improve patient care. However, its limited sample size, lack of statistical analysis, reliance on an artificial setting, and potential lack of reproducibility detract from its overall impact. If future studies address these limitations through larger, more diverse datasets and real-world clinical data, the field would greatly benefit. Also, the analysis is forward-looking, evaluating models available in the near future (May 2025), which may influence the novelty of the work.

Score: 7
